# Thoughts for the Day by Shri Janakiraman (Jani). Newest first.
# @@ number | series | title | related song (book:number, optional), then paragraphs separated by blank lines.

@@ 253 | Epic Series 123 | Why Do We Suffer Twice? | dns:8.4
Have you noticed how often we worry about things that haven’t even happened?

Our daughter is late returning from office. We know she may be held up with work. But as the minutes pass, our mind starts imagining all kinds of things. We call her, but she doesn’t answer. We don’t want to disturb her by calling again. Yet, how do we stop worrying?

We develop a slight sore throat. Immediately, we wonder whether it could be something serious.

We read a WhatsApp message about someone’s sudden illness or death. For the rest of the day, we keep thinking about our own health and family.

Our son and daughter-in-law haven’t called for a few days. We understand that they have their own lives. Still, a small thought hurts us. Have we become less important to them?

And then come thoughts about old age. Will we remain healthy? Will our children be around when we need them? Will we become dependent on others?

Sometimes, nothing has happened. Nobody has hurt us. No bad news has come. Yet, we have already spent hours feeling anxious and unhappy.

Why do we do this to ourselves?

An incident from the Mahabharata

Just before the Kurukshetra war, Arjuna stood on the battlefield with Krishna as his charioteer.

When he looked at the people standing on the opposite side, he saw his own teachers, elders, cousins and relatives.

His mind began to race. What if I have to kill them? What will happen to their families? What is the use of winning a kingdom at such a cost?

He began imagining the terrible consequences of the war even before it had begun.

His hands trembled. His mouth became dry. He could not even hold his bow. He told Krishna that he did not want to fight.

Krishna listened to him and guided him through the Bhagavad Gita.

He helped Arjuna see things clearly and understand his duty. He brought him back to the situation before him.

Something to reflect upon

Are we not sometimes like Arjuna?

Our daughter is ten minutes late, and our mind has already imagined an accident.

We have a small health problem, and our mind has already imagined a serious illness.

Our children don’t call for a few days, and we have already imagined a lonely old age.

Of course, our concerns are genuine. We love our families. We care about our health. We want to feel secure.

But are we helping ourselves or hurting ourselves by repeatedly imagining the worst?

We cannot control everything that happens in life. But we can certainly try not to suffer over things that haven’t happened.

A small takeaway: Three questions

The next time a worrying thought troubles us, let us pause and ask:

1. What is the fact? What do I really know?

2. What is my fear? Am I imagining something that hasn’t happened?

3. What can I do now? Is there one sensible action I can take?

If our daughter is late, we can call her or send a message. If we have a health concern, we can consult a doctor. If our children haven’t called, we can call them.

Let us do what is needed. After that, let us try not to keep replaying the same thoughts.

It may not be easy. But perhaps we can begin with just one worry at a time.

A thought to carry with us

I sometimes wonder how much of our happiness is lost, not because of what life has done to us, but because of what we imagine life might do to us.

We cannot fight every possible battle in our minds. Let us not borrow tomorrow’s sorrow and bring it into today.

The next time we catch ourselves worrying, perhaps we can ask:

Has this really happened, or am I making myself suffer over something that may never happen?

How many Kurukshetras do we create in our minds every day, even before the battle has begun?

Don’t fight tomorrow’s battle today. Let us take care of today. Tomorrow, we will face what comes.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 252 | Epic Series 122 | Do we wait until we have enough to give? | 
Recently, I got a query from one of the TFD readers :

“Sometimes I feel like helping someone or contributing to a good cause. But then I start thinking about my own commitments, future needs and financial security. I tell myself that I will give more when I am in a better position. Is there anything wrong with thinking this way?”

I could relate to this question. All of us have responsibilities, commitments and concerns about the future. We naturally want to feel secure before we share what we have.

But where do we draw the line? How much security is enough?

A story from the life of King Harishchandra came to mind.

The king who gave what little he had. King Harishchandra had lost his kingdom and was walking through a scorching desert with his wife and young son. They were hungry and thirsty, and had very little left with them.

A starving stranger approached him, pleading for food and water for his children.

His ministers advised him not to give away what little they had. His own family was suffering. Why should he worry about a stranger? But Harishchandra chose to share what he had.

Something to reflect upon

How often do we tell ourselves:

“Let me become financially comfortable. Then I will help.”

“Let me secure my family’s future. Then I will contribute.”

“Let me have a little more. Then I will share.”

The truth is, there is always something more to secure, something more to save and something more to worry about. Without realising it, we sometimes keep postponing the act of giving.

Of course, we have to take care of our families and fulfil our responsibilities. Giving should not mean neglecting them. But do we sometimes become so concerned about our own security that we overlook the needs of others?

And giving need not always be money. A little time, a kind word, encouragement, knowledge or simply being there for someone can mean so much.

Perhaps we don’t have to wait until we have everything in place to start giving.

An insight

I wonder whether generosity is really about how much we have, or how much we are willing to share.

When we give only from what is left over, we may never really learn the joy of giving.

Perhaps kindness is not something we practise when life is comfortable. It is something we can carry with us through every stage of life.

A small prayer

Swami, please help me overcome this constant feeling that I need a little more before I can share what I have.

Help me take care of my responsibilities while remaining sensitive to the needs of others.

May I learn to give without calculating too much, to help without expecting anything in return and to see Your presence in those who come to me seeking help.

After all, what we have is Your gift. May I learn to share it with gratitude.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 251 | Epic Series 121 | When is enough, enough? | 
Today, I met one of our TFD readers who told me that he would soon be turning 80.

As he spoke about his life, he mentioned how his thinking and way of living had changed when he turned 60 and 70. Now, as he approaches 80, he feels that he may travel less and spend more time looking within.

His words made me think further.

We all celebrate our birthdays. But do we ever stop to think about what should change in us as we grow older?

I started asking myself this question. And then, a story from the Mahabharata came to my mind.

A king who wanted more

King Yayati was a powerful king who had everything that most people would wish for. But a curse made him grow old before his time.

He was not ready to give up the pleasures of youth. So he asked his sons if any of them would exchange their youth for his old age.

His youngest son, Puru, agreed. He was willing to become old so that his father could become young again.

Yayati got back his youth and continued to enjoy life. He did so for a thousand years.

Just think about it. A thousand years! Surely, by then, he would have had enough of everything he wanted. But he had not.

Finally, Yayati realised something that changed his way of thinking. He understood that desires are like a fire. When we pour ghee into a fire, it burns even more. In the same way, the more we try to satisfy our desires, the more we may want. He then gave back his youth to Puru and accepted his old age.

This story makes me look at myself

I wonder how many times I have told myself, “Let me finish this work. Then I will slow down.”

Or, “Let me take care of these responsibilities. Then I will have more time for myself.”

But when one responsibility is over, another comes along. When one task is completed, I find something else to do.

I want to do so many things. I want to learn, to serve, to share what I have learnt and to do more meaningful work. These are all good things. But sometimes, I wonder whether I am also caught up in the habit of always wanting to do more.

Am I making enough time to sit quietly, look within and understand myself?

I do not have a clear answer. I am still trying to understand this myself.

That is why the story of Yayati touched me so deeply.

He had a thousand years, but even that did not make him feel that he had enough. Perhaps the question is not how much time we have, but what we are doing with the time we have.

As we grow older

When we turn 50, 60, 70 or 80, life gives us a chance to pause and think.

Is there something I should stop doing?

Is there something I should start doing?

Is there something I have been putting off for years?

Perhaps we need not wait until we are old to ask these questions.

I, too, need to ask myself what I should be doing differently at this stage of my life. I may have many plans for the years ahead, but am I also making room for silence, prayer and inner growth?

I hope I can learn to do this better.

A small prayer

Swami, You have given me so much, and I still keep asking for more time and more opportunities.

Please help me understand how best to use what You have already given me.

Help me know when to act and when to be still, when to hold on and when to let go.

I am still learning, Swami. Please guide me.

Perhaps growing older is not just about adding years to our lives. Perhaps it is also about learning something new about ourselves at every stage.

I hope I can learn to do that.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 250 | Epic Series 120 | When love becomes worry | 
A question posed to me by one of our TFD readers :

“I am constantly worried about my children, their studies, careers and future. I know worrying doesn’t help, but I cannot stop. How do I learn to let go?”

I am sure many of us have felt this way. A familiar scene plays out in many homes.

It is late at night. A parent asks, “Have you thought about your future?”

The child replies, “Please, let me handle it.”

The parent walks away, hurt. The child goes back to what he was doing. But the parent lies awake, thinking. “What if he makes the wrong choice? What if she struggles later? Have I done enough?”

The child feels pressured. The parent feels helpless. Both love each other. Yet neither feels understood.

I see a little of this struggle in myself too. My son is at a crossroads in his medical career, deciding which postgraduate specialisation to pursue. Should he choose psychiatry or pulmonology? As a parent, I keep thinking about which would be the right choice for him.

Then I ask myself, why am I breaking my head over a decision that he must ultimately live with?

Of course, I want him to make the right choice. I want him to be happy and settled. But can I really know today which path will make his life meaningful ten or twenty years from now?

I can guide him. I can share my thoughts. But I cannot live his life for him.

Perhaps this is what makes parenting so difficult. We want our children to stand on their own feet, but we are frightened of what might happen when we let go of their hands.

A mother’s lullaby

The Markandeya Purana tells us about Queen Madalasa, who sang a beautiful lullaby to her infant son.

“Shuddhosi, Buddhhosi, Niranjanosi…”

You are pure. You are aware. You are untouched by the impurities of the world.

The traditional story tells us that her sons chose renunciation, while her son Alarka was raised to rule a kingdom.

What touches me is the message behind her song. She was not merely preparing her children for success in the world. She was reminding them of their deeper worth.

Before the world could make her children worry about what they must become, she reminded them of who they were.

We parents often do the opposite, though our intentions are good.

“Study well. What will happen to your future?”

“Choose carefully. Your whole life depends on it.”

“Look at others. They seem to be doing so well.”

We say these things because we care. But sometimes, our children begin to feel that one wrong decision could ruin their lives.

Is that really what we want them to believe?

Education matters. Careers matter. Making good decisions matters. But life rarely goes exactly as we plan. Sometimes, a path that looks perfect turns out to be difficult. Sometimes, a decision we fear turns out well. And sometimes, we learn our greatest lessons from the choices that did not work out.

What are we really worried about?

Perhaps we need to ask ourselves an uncomfortable question. Are we helping our children make better decisions, or are we trying to reduce our own anxiety by making sure they choose what feels safest to us?

There is a difference.

We can guide them, but we cannot make every decision for them. We can warn them about difficulties, but we cannot protect them from every disappointment.

And when they stumble, perhaps they need us to say, “It’s all right. Let us see how we can work this out,” rather than, “I told you so.”

I say this to myself as much as to anyone else. It is easy to understand these things. It is much harder to practise them when our own children are involved.

A small prayer

Swami, I want so much for my children that sometimes I forget they have their own journey to live.

Give me the wisdom to guide them, the patience to listen to them, and the strength to trust them.

When I worry about their future, remind me that worrying cannot guarantee their happiness.

Help me do my duty with love and leave what is beyond my control in Your hands.

Perhaps the hardest part of being a parent is learning when to hold on and when to let go.

When our children were little, we held their hands because they could not walk on their own. As they grow, we must slowly loosen our hold, not because we love them any less, but because they need to learn to walk by themselves.

We spend years preparing our children for life. Perhaps we must also learn to trust them to live it.

I am still learning this. Perhaps many of us are.

May we have the wisdom to guide our children, the patience to let them grow, and the faith to trust that God will walk with them even when we cannot.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 249 | Epic Series 119 | When the mind knows, but the heart does not listen | 
Another question from the same reader:

“Many of us pray to God for different reasons. Does prayer help us balance our emotions? Sometimes emotions take over and we do things without even realising it. Later, we wonder whether what we did was right or wrong. What makes our emotions change so much? Could planetary influences be responsible?”

I have been thinking about this. And honestly, I don’t want to answer this as someone who has mastered his emotions.

I have not. I know all the theories. I have read about the mind. I understand how emotions work. I have counselled people on many of these things.

And still, sometimes I get angry. I react. I say something which I should not have said. And then, when I become calm, I feel terrible.

Many times, I am the first person to say, “I am sorry. I shouldn’t have spoken like that.” The apology comes easily.

But controlling the anger before it comes out?

That is much harder. Perhaps that is the strange thing about us human beings.

Knowing is one thing. Doing is another.

The Mahabharata makes this very clear through Arjuna.

Arjuna knew his duty. He was a great warrior. He had trained all his life.

But when Krishna placed the chariot between the two armies and Arjuna saw Bhishma, Drona and his relatives, his emotions overwhelmed him.

His hands trembled. His bow slipped. His mind went blank. For that moment, all his knowledge was still inside him. But he could not use it.

I sometimes feel that this is exactly what happens to us. When we are calm, we know how we should behave.

“Don’t shout.”

“Don’t react immediately.”

“Listen first.”

“Don’t hurt someone you love.”

Very easy to say. Then something happens. A word touches us in the wrong place.An old hurt comes back.

The voice becomes louder. And before the sensible mind gets a chance to say anything, we have already reacted.

Later we wonder, “Why did I do that?”

I think there is a simple way of looking at this.

The conscious mind is the part of us that knows what we should do.

The subconscious mind is the part that has learnt how we usually react.

When emotions become very strong, the old learning can take over. That is why simply telling ourselves, “Next time I will control myself,” doesn’t always work.

Perhaps we have to teach the subconscious something new.

I have been thinking about one very simple practice.

If I know that a particular situation makes me angry, I can practise before it happens.

Just close my eyes for half a minute. Imagine the person saying those words.

Feel the irritation coming. And then imagine myself stopping. Taking one breath. Keeping quiet for a few seconds. Then answering gently. It may sound almost silly.

But perhaps the mind learns through repetition.

If I keep practising the response when I am calm, perhaps one day, when the real situation comes, the mind may remember the new path.

Maybe that is also where prayer comes in. Not just asking God to change the other person. Not just asking Him to make the situation go my way.

But saying, “Swami, I know I am going to get angry. Please help me stop before I hurt someone.” I find that a much more honest prayer.

As for planetary influences, I know our tradition gives importance to them. I don’t want to dismiss that. But I also don’t want to use planets as an excuse for everything I do.

Some things are simply my own patterns. And perhaps patterns can slowly be changed.

I am still learning this….I still lose my temper sometimes…..I still regret things…..I still have to say sorry.

But perhaps there is one small consolation. Every time I realise what happened, I have another opportunity to learn.

Maybe progress is not that I never get angry. Maybe progress is that I notice it a little earlier. Maybe one day I will notice it before the words come out.

And perhaps one day, instead of saying, “I am sorry, I lost my temper,” I will be able to say, “I felt the anger coming. But this time, I stopped.”

That day may still be some distance away.

Until then, I can only keep trying, keep praying and keep asking His forgiveness.

Perhaps Arjuna’s greatest lesson for me is not that he was confused. It is that he did not hide his confusion from Krishna.

He admitted, “I don’t know what to do. Please guide me.”

Maybe that is where real change begins. Not when we pretend we have control. But when we honestly tell God, “Swami, I know all the right things. But sometimes I still cannot do them. Please help me.”

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 248 | Epic Series 118 | Who Is Really in Control? | 
Dear Friends,

A reader sent me a very candid question. I read it once, then read it again, and for a few seconds wondered whether I should answer it at all. I don’t want to dig my own grave. 😃

Here is the question:

“Why do wives want to dominate their husbands? Is it insecurity? Do they want their husbands to listen to them and not do anything differently? If the husband does something differently, the repercussions are different. Sometimes the man’s IQ gets lowered or completely blanked out!”

I am sure many husbands are smiling while reading this. And I am equally sure some wives are thinking, “Exactly! What is wrong with this man?” 😄

But if we keep the husband and wife labels aside, this question opens up something much deeper about human nature.

Why do we want the people we love to behave the way we want them to?

A husband wants his wife to understand him. A wife wants her husband to listen to her. Parents want their children to listen, and children want their parents to understand. When the other person doesn’t behave as we expect, we get irritated, we argue, we withdraw, or sometimes we keep explaining the same thing until the other person finally says, “Okay, okay!” And we quietly feel that we have won.

But have we?

Psychology tells us something interesting. Very often, what looks like control is actually fear wearing a different shirt.

“If you don’t listen to me, something may go wrong.”

“If you do things differently, I may not be able to handle it.”

“If you don’t agree with me, perhaps you don’t care about me.”

So we try to control the situation. The trouble is that the more one person tries to control, the more the other person resists. The more they resist, the more the first person tries to control.

Soon, a simple discussion about where to go for dinner has become a Mahabharata. And, as usual, nobody remembers how the war started.

There is a beautiful lesson in the Ramayana.

Kaikeyi loved Rama. Rama loved Kaikeyi. But when fear entered her mind about Bharata’s future, her love became mixed with the need to control what would happen. She asked for the two boons, and Dasharatha pleaded with her, but once the mind becomes convinced that “I must have things my way”, even love can find it difficult to get through.

Perhaps that is the deeper lesson.

We don’t always try to control because we don’t love. Sometimes we try to control precisely because we love, but we are afraid of losing what we love.

And this happens to men and women alike.

So perhaps the real question is not, “Why does my spouse want to dominate me?” Perhaps it is, “Why does my spouse feel the need to control this situation?”

That small change in the question can change the entire conversation.

There is another important distinction. Listening is not the same as obeying. A husband can listen to his wife without agreeing with everything she says.

A wife can listen to her husband without accepting everything he proposes. We can disagree and still respect each other.

A healthy relationship is not one where one person always wins. It is one where both people can disagree without making the other person feel unloved.

Sometimes the husband is right. Sometimes the wife is right. And occasionally, both are wrong, but neither is willing to admit it!

Perhaps the real freedom in a relationship is this: “I don’t have to control you to feel secure with you.”

When I am insecure, I try to control. When I am afraid, I react. When I feel unheard, I become louder. But when I feel secure, I can listen. I can allow the other person to be different. I can let go.

Perhaps that is what real love slowly teaches us.

Not, “You must become the person I want you to be.”

But, “I can love you even when you are not exactly the way I want you to be.”

That is a much harder lesson. And perhaps a much more beautiful one.

So, dear reader who asked this question, you may have started by asking about wives and husbands. But somewhere along the way, the question has come much closer to all of us.

The next time someone at home does something differently from what we wanted, perhaps we can pause and ask ourselves, “Why am I so uncomfortable when this person doesn’t do things my way?”

That question may be uncomfortable. But sometimes the uncomfortable questions take us to the deepest truths.

With His grace, may we learn to replace control with trust, reaction with understanding, and the need to win with the desire to protect the relationship.

And if all else fails, take a deep breath, smile, and remember that sometimes maintaining peace at home is also a form of spiritual sadhana!

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 247 | Epic Series 117 | Why do good people suffer? | 
Dear Friends,

A reader in his sixties sent me a difficult question.

He had recently seen an elderly relative going through a very difficult time. The person had lived a good life, worked hard, looked after the family and done his duties. But now, in old age, his body was giving him one problem after another.

There was pain. There were health problems. He needed help for many simple things. At times, he felt that he had become a burden to his family.

Seeing all this deeply disturbed the reader.

Perhaps because he himself is now in his sixties, it made him think, “Is this what old age can become? After spending our whole life working and looking after everyone, why should we end up like this?”

He then asked a very difficult question.

“Why does God allow good people to suffer so much? Is it karma? Did they do something wrong knowingly or unknowingly? Is God making them suffer because of something they did in this life or an earlier life?”

I felt this was not just a question about karma. It was a question about all of us. What will happen to me when I grow older?

Will I remain healthy?

Will I become dependent on my children?

Will I become a burden?

None of us knows.

The Mahabharata gives us a beautiful thought through Kunti. We have seen this story in an earlier TFD.

Kunti went through many difficulties in her life. Yet those difficulties made her remember Krishna more deeply.

We don’t have to ask God for difficulties. But perhaps we can learn one simple thing from Kunti.

When something difficult happens, instead of only asking, “Why me?”, ask, “What can I do now?”

This question can help us in our own homes.

If an old father cannot walk properly, hold his hand.

If an elderly mother asks the same question again, answer her patiently.

If a parent has to visit the doctor again and again, help without making them feel guilty.

And if we ourselves are growing old and need help, let us not keep telling our children, “I have become a burden to you.”

We looked after them when they were small. There may come a time when they have to look after us. That is not a burden. That is life.

At the same time, children also have their own problems. They have work, children, money worries and many responsibilities.

So we too can ask ourselves, “What can I still do for myself?”

Can I take my medicines properly?

Can I walk a little every day?

Can I keep myself busy and positive?

Can I avoid calling my children for every small thing?

Can I thank them when they help me?

Small things like these can make life easier for everyone. We may never know why one person suffers more than another.

Maybe there is karma. Maybe there are reasons related to age, health and circumstances. Maybe there are some things about life that we will never understand.

But perhaps we don’t need to know the reason to do the right thing.

When someone is suffering, our first job is not to find out why. Our first job is to make their suffering a little less.

And when something difficult happens to us, instead of asking the whole day, “Why is God doing this to me?”, perhaps we can ask, “What can I do today?”

Take the medicine.

Go for the walk.

Ask for help.

Call someone.

Say sorry.

Say thank you.

Pray.

And if nothing more can be done, accept it and remember Him. We cannot know what our old age will look like. But we can decide what kind of person we will be when old age comes.

And until then, we can make the journey easier for someone who has reached that stage before us.

Perhaps that is the lesson. We may not be able to remove someone’s pain. But we can make sure they don’t face it alone.

With His grace, may we learn to face our own future without fear and meet another person’s suffering with patience, love and kindness.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 246 | Epic Series 116 | I thought it was not working | 
Dear Friends,

My cousin wrote a very thoughtful comment on my last TFD. She shared her experience with her Yoga group. Hundreds join, but only a few continue regularly. Many attend the training, learn what they have to do and then stop. Some have genuine reasons such as work or health. But many think, “Now I know what to do. I will practise on my own.” Slowly, the practice stops. This happens in almost every part of our life.

We know that walking is good for us, but we do not walk regularly. We know we should pray, but we postpone it. We learn a sloka but do not practise it enough. We decide to control our anger, but lose our temper again. We start something with great enthusiasm, but when we do not see a quick result, we slowly give up.

Perhaps we stop not because it is not working, but because we cannot see that it is working.

This reminded me of a beautiful incident from the Ramayana.

When Rama and his team reached the seashore, they had to cross the ocean to reach Lanka. Hanuman was there, but he had forgotten his own strength. Jambavan reminded him of what he was capable of.

Hanuman did not keep discussing whether he could do it. He remembered his strength, took the leap and kept going.

The strength was already within him. What changed was that he believed in it and used it.

Something similar happens in our lives.

We walk for ten days and see no change. We practise a sloka for a week and still make mistakes. We try to become calmer and still get angry. We make an effort and ask, “What is the use?”

So we stop.

But perhaps the effort was already changing us.

A seed does not become a tree the day after it is planted. For many days, we see nothing. But something is happening beneath the soil.

Many changes in our lives are like that.

The person who walks regularly may first find that climbing stairs has become easier. The person practising a sloka may suddenly realise that the words are beginning to come naturally. The person working on anger may still get angry, but may recover faster. These small changes are easy to miss because we are waiting for a big result.

This is why regular practice matters.

We do not have to be perfect. If we miss a day, we can start again. If we forget, we can learn again. If we stop, we can begin again.

There is no shame in starting again.

Perhaps this is also the role of a teacher or a friend. Jambavan did not cross the ocean for Hanuman. He only reminded him of the strength already within him.

We too can encourage someone, show a path or share a thought. But finally, each person has to take the step.

That is why my cousin’s comment made me think. It is not enough to say, “I know what to do.”

The real question is, “Am I doing it regularly enough to see the change?”

Perhaps we should not expect a big change overnight.

Let us take one small step today and another tomorrow. If we fall, let us get up. If we stop, let us begin again.

As Geetacharya teaches us, let us do what is in our hands sincerely without worrying too much about when the result will come.

Sometimes the change has already begun. We simply cannot see it yet.

With His grace, let us keep going.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 245 | Epic Series 115 | The story I created was not the truth | 
Dear Friends,

It has been a while since I wrote a TFD. The last few days have once again been quite full with travel and Namasankeerthanam programmes.

I have met many people, worked with different teams and organisers, and had the opportunity to listen to many people. Each meeting leaves me with something to think about.

For me, that is what these TFDs are about. I try to take a small incident or a thought from our great epics and look at it in the light of our everyday life. Many things that happened thousands of years ago are still very much part of our lives today. If something I write can make someone pause, think or feel a little better, I feel happy.

Recently, I noticed something very simple in myself. After sending a TFD, sometimes I see many people responding to it. Sometimes there are only a few responses. When that happens, I naturally wonder whether the thought reached people or whether I could have expressed it better.

There is nothing unusual about that. When we share something with the hope that it may be useful to someone, we naturally want to know whether it connected.

But then I noticed how easily the mind can go one step further. A few responses can become, “Perhaps nobody found this useful.” Silence can become, “Maybe people did not like what I wrote.” Without having any real information, we can create our own explanation.

That made me think about how often we do this in our daily life.

Imagine a mother calling her son. He answers the phone, but he sounds hurried and says, “Amma, I am busy now. I will call you later.” The call ends. The mother starts wondering why he spoke like that. “Is he upset with me? Did I say something to him? Has something changed?”

She may feel bad for the rest of the day. But the son may simply be sitting in an important meeting and trying to end the call quickly. The mother knows what happened. Her son spoke hurriedly. But she does not know why. The reason she imagined may not be the real reason at all.

We see this happening in many relationships. A husband comes home and his wife is quiet. He thinks she is angry with him. A friend does not call for a few days. We feel that the friendship is changing. Someone changes a plan without telling us. We feel that they do not respect our opinion. Someone does not acknowledge our work. We feel that they do not value us.

The incident may be very small. But our mind adds a meaning to it. And once we believe that meaning, we start feeling hurt. Sometimes we even change the way we behave with that person. The other person may have no idea that all this is happening inside our mind.

This reminded me of a beautiful incident from the Srimad Bhagavatham.

King Parikshit once saw a cow and a bull in great distress. The bull had lost three of its four legs and a man was troubling it. Parikshit was deeply disturbed and wanted to know who was responsible.

He asked the bull who had caused this suffering.

What the bull said is what touched me. The bull did not simply point to the man and say, “He is responsible.” Instead, the bull said that it was difficult to know the real cause of suffering. Was it another person? Was it fate? Was it one’s own actions? The bull did not want to make a judgement without knowing the complete truth.

That is a very simple thought, but it is very difficult to practise in our own lives.

We often know what happened, but we think we also know why it happened.

Someone did not reply to our message. We know that much. But we do not know why.

Someone spoke sharply to us. We know that much. But we do not know what was happening in that person’s life before that conversation.

Someone changed a plan. We know that much. But we may not know what made them change it.

The mind does not like an unanswered question. So it quickly fills the empty space with an answer. Many times, the answer comes from our own fears, past experiences or expectations. And then we start treating that answer as the truth.

Perhaps we can try something very simple the next time we feel hurt by someone’s behaviour. Before reacting, we can ask ourselves, “What do I actually know?” Then we can ask, “What am I assuming?”

These two questions may save us from many unnecessary hurts.

Sometimes, instead of thinking about it for hours, we can also ask the person directly. “Is everything okay?” “Did I misunderstand something?” “Was there a reason for this?” A simple conversation can sometimes clear a misunderstanding that has been growing inside us for days.

I see this often when people talk about their relationships. Many times, the pain is real, but the reason they have given for that pain may not be the real reason.

A person may feel ignored, rejected or disrespected because of something that happened. But when we look at the situation carefully, we may find that there were many other things happening at the same time.

This is something I also want to remember when working with people. In Namasankeerthanam, many of us come together with one purpose, but each person has a different way of thinking and different things happening in their life. When we give each other some space and keep the Nama at the centre, small differences do not become big problems.

Perhaps the same is true in our homes and relationships.

We do not have to understand everything immediately. We do not have to decide what another person is thinking. We can wait a little. We can ask. We can give the other person the benefit of doubt.

I am finding this thought useful in my own life. Sometimes what hurts us is not what another person actually did. It is what we thought their action meant.

There is a big difference between the two.

The more I think about the bull in the Bhagavatham, the more I feel that there is great wisdom in not rushing to blame. We may not have the whole picture. We may be seeing only one small part of a much bigger story.

So perhaps, before we become hurt or angry with someone, we can pause and ask ourselves, “Do I really know this, or do I only think I know this?”

Sometimes the answer may surprise us.

And sometimes, when we finally understand what really happened, we may look back and realise that the truth was much simpler than the story we had created in our mind.

That is today’s small thought.

Let us be careful about the stories we create when we do not know the whole truth.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 244 | Epic Series 114 | I thought I had gone beyond this | 
Dear Friends,

It has been a few days since I wrote to you. I have missed these quiet moments of sharing with all of you.

The last few days have been very full with Hari Nama Sankeerthanam. We had our “Walking with the Saints” programme for children and parents, followed by another beautiful programme in Tirupur.

There was so much happening one after another that I simply did not get the quiet time needed to sit down and write.

But perhaps there was something else I missed too. In the middle of all the activity, I did not get enough time to sit quietly and look within.

A question that was asked of me recently brought this thought back to me.

“I have spent so many years trying to improve myself. I have learnt to control my anger. I have learnt to be patient. I have learnt from my mistakes. I have tried to become a better person. Then how could I have behaved like this? After all these years, how could I still make such a mistake?”

This person was not angry with somebody else. The person was disappointed with oneself.

That is a very different kind of pain.

When somebody else behaves badly with us, we can blame them. But when we ourselves behave in a way that we never expected from ourselves, we begin to question ourselves.

Why did I react like that?…. I should have known better….. After all these years, should I not have changed?

And finally comes the thought that hurts the most. “I thought I had gone beyond this.”

The Bhagavatham tells us a beautiful story of King Citraketu that made me think about this.

Citraketu was not an ordinary person. He had gone through many experiences in life. He had gained great knowledge and had become a great devotee of the Lord.

One day, Citraketu was travelling in his celestial aircraft. He came upon a great gathering of sages and divine beings. Lord Shiva was also present there. Lord Shiva was seated among the great sages, and Goddess Parvati was sitting on his lap.

Citraketu saw this and made a light hearted remark.

He said, in effect, “How wonderful! Lord Shiva is the great teacher of the world. He is surrounded by all these great sages. Yet here he is, sitting in this public assembly with Parvati seated on his lap. What an unusual way for such a great teacher to sit before everyone.”

Citraketu was not speaking out of hatred. He was not trying to insult Shiva. He probably thought he was simply making a humorous observation.

But there was something deeper in his words. He was judging Lord Shiva based on his own idea of what was proper and what was not.

Lord Shiva remained completely calm. He did not become angry. He understood Citraketu. But Parvati was deeply upset.

She felt that Citraketu, despite all his spiritual knowledge, had spoken about Lord Shiva without understanding his greatness. She therefore cursed Citraketu to take birth as a demon.

What is striking is that Citraketu had travelled so far on the spiritual path. He had great knowledge. He had great devotion. Yet, in one small moment, he judged someone else.

And then comes the part of the story that touches me the most.

When Parvati cursed him, Citraketu did not argue. He did not say, “You have misunderstood me.”

He did not become angry. He did not use his spiritual knowledge to defend himself.

He simply accepted the curse with humility. Perhaps this is where the story becomes very relevant to our own lives.

I find myself wondering how often we are disturbed not merely because we made a mistake, but because the mistake does not fit the image we have of ourselves.

“I am not an angry person.”

“I am not judgmental.”

“I have learnt to be patient.”

And then one day, something happens and we react exactly in the way we thought we had left behind.

Maybe the real pain is not just that we made the mistake. It is that we suddenly discover that there is still a part of us that we thought had disappeared.

What do we do at that moment?

This is where I feel Citraketu has something very important to teach me. When his mistake was pointed out, he did not spend his energy proving that he was a better person than his mistake.

He simply accepted it. Perhaps we too can try doing that.

The next time I surprise myself by saying or doing something that I thought I had gone beyond, instead of immediately defending myself or asking, “How could I have done this?”, I would like to pause and ask:

“What is this incident showing me about myself?”

That question changes the whole way we look at a mistake. It does not make the mistake smaller. It makes the learning bigger.

And perhaps that is the lesson I would like to carry with me from Citraketu.

I do not have to be someone who has conquered every weakness. I only have to be honest enough to recognise one when it appears.

And humble enough to say, “Maybe there is still something here for me to learn.”

That, perhaps, is real growth.

Not the feeling that “I have gone beyond this,” but the humility to say, “I still have something to learn.”

I would like to remember this the next time I disappoint myself.

Perhaps the mistake is not the end of the journey. Perhaps it is simply showing me where the next part of the journey begins.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏
