If you are in Chennai, please do join us. It will be wonderful to sing the Nama together once again. Looking forward to seeing many of you

@@ 243 | Epic Series 113 | Why does one small incident stay in our mind for so long? | 
Have you noticed this?. Someone says something to us. It may be just one sentence. Perhaps they did not even mean to hurt us. We smile, move on and continue with our day.

But later, when we are alone, our mind goes back to that one sentence. “Why did he say that?” “What did she mean?” “Maybe I should have answered differently?” “I should have told him this.”

Suddenly, the conversation starts all over again in our head.

The actual conversation may have lasted only two minutes. But we may spend the next two hours thinking about it. The other person may have completely forgotten it, while we are still carrying it with us. I am sure many of us have experienced this.

Something similar happened to me recently. The last few weeks were quite full with bhajans, satsangs and our Walking with the Saints programme for children. After all this activity, I took a small break.

During this time, there was a small interaction. A few words did not feel right to me. It was not a big argument. Nothing serious had happened. Yet, later, I noticed that my mind kept going back to it.

The conversation was over. The person was no longer with me. But inside my mind, the conversation was still continuing. Then I asked myself, “Who am I actually arguing with now?” The answer was quite clear. I was arguing with my own mind.

This took me to a beautiful and rather deep story from the Bhagavatham.

The story begins with four great sages called the Sanat Kumaras. They are Sanaka, Sanandana, Sanatana and Sanat Kumara. They are said to be the mind born sons of Brahma, created at the beginning of creation.

They were not interested in worldly pleasures or possessions. They remained ever young and spent their lives seeking the Truth and Bhagavan.

So when these sages approached Brahma with a question, it was not an ordinary question. They asked about something that every one of us faces.

The mind is pulled towards the world. The senses see, hear, taste, touch and enjoy. The mind remembers these experiences. Then the mind wants them again.

The senses pull the mind towards the outside world, while the impressions already stored in the mind pull it back towards the same things. It becomes a circle.

The Sanat Kumaras asked, “How does one break free from this circle?”

This is where the story becomes very interesting. Even Brahma, their own father and the creator, could not give them a satisfactory answer.

Then Bhagavan appeared before them in the form of a beautiful swan, the Hamsa Avatar.

The Hamsa teaches something very simple. You are not the mind. You are the one who knows the mind.

Think about what happens when someone says something that hurts us. The mind immediately reacts. “He should not have said that.” “She does not respect me.” “I should teach him a lesson.” “I should have answered differently.” The person may have already walked away, but our mind continues the conversation.

Why?

Because we have become one with the thought.

The Hamsa teaching asks us to take one small step back. Instead of saying, “I am angry,” we can notice, “Anger has come into my mind.” Instead of saying, “I am worried,” we can notice, “My mind is worrying.” Instead of saying, “I cannot stop thinking about this,” we can notice, “My mind is going back to this thought again.”

That small distance is very powerful. Once we can see a thought, we are no longer completely trapped inside it.

Think of the sky. Clouds move across it all the time. Some are white. Some are dark. Some bring rain. Some disappear quickly. But the sky does not become the cloud. The cloud comes and goes. The sky remains.

Our thoughts are also like clouds. They come, stay for some time and go. But when we hold on to them and keep thinking about them, we give them more and more strength.

This is exactly what happened to me in that small interaction. The incident itself was over, but my mind kept bringing it back. The other person was not doing anything anymore. I was doing it to myself.

That was the internal dilemma. One part of me knew that the matter was small. Another part of my mind wanted to continue the argument. One part said, “Leave it.”

Another part said, “But what he said was not right.” One part wanted peace. Another part wanted to prove that I was right.

Is this not the battle we often have within ourselves?

The outside world may be quiet, but inside there can be a full conversation. This happens with anger, worry, jealousy, hurt and even when someone does not appreciate us.

Sometimes somebody does not reply to our message, and within minutes our mind creates a whole story. The event may be small, but the mind can make it very big.

The Sanat Kumaras were asking Brahma how to break this very cycle. Bhagavan, as Hamsa, gives us the key. We do not have to fight every thought. We do not have to chase every thought. We do not have to believe every thought. We can simply watch.

This is not running away from life. It is learning to live without being controlled by every movement of the mind.

So what can we actually do with this teaching in our daily life?

Let us keep it very simple. When something upsets us, pause. Do not reply immediately. Take one slow breath. Then notice what is happening inside.

Tell yourself, “My mind is upset right now.” Then wait. Give yourself a little time before speaking or acting. Finally, choose. Ask yourself, “What is the right thing for me to do now?”

We do not have to become perfect. We only have to create a small gap between what we feel and what we do.

In that small gap, there is freedom.

Perhaps this is the beautiful message hidden in the story of the Sanat Kumaras and the Hamsa Avatar.

The problem is not that thoughts come. The problem is that we believe every thought and immediately become part of it.

The next time our mind starts replaying an old conversation, perhaps we can smile and say, “Ah, my mind has started again.”

And then let the thought pass.

The conversation is over. The person has moved on. Let us move on too.

The cloud will pass. The sky will remain.

Perhaps these four words are enough to carry the teaching of Hamsa into our daily life:

PAUSE. NOTICE. WAIT. CHOOSE.

A very small practice. But perhaps a very big step towards inner freedom.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

P.S. Please continue sending your thoughts, questions and suggestions. Many of these TFDs begin with something very ordinary that happens in our lives. When we look at it a little more deeply, we often discover that the Bhagavatham has already given us a beautiful way to understand it.

@@ 242 | Epic Series 112 | Why does someone else’s life make me feel smaller? | 
First, a big thank you to all those who keep sending me your questions, thoughts and suggestions for the TFD. I genuinely value them.

I keep all these questions in a little “to-do list”. Whenever I am looking for a topic, I go through the list and pick one that touches me.

One question from a satsang friend particularly drew my attention this time:

Why do people feel they should be more than others? What kind of emotion is this? Is it competitiveness, jealousy or our own insecurity because we are unable to visualise what others have achieved?

The more I thought about it, the more I felt that this is something we all experience in small ways.

We meet people socially and, without intending to, our mind starts comparing.

We visit someone’s house. We notice the furniture, the kitchen, the garden, the way everything is maintained. We may genuinely admire it, but somewhere inside comes another thought: “Our house could also have been like this.”

We attend a wedding. We enjoy the occasion, but we also notice the hall, the decoration, the food and the arrangements. On the way back, we may find ourselves discussing what they did well, what we could have done differently, or what we might do when our own turn comes.

We meet someone’s children. We hear about their studies, career or achievements. We are happy for them, but almost immediately our mind turns towards our own children.

And sometimes, we don’t even need to meet anyone.

We watch a movie or a television serial. We see a husband remembering every little thing about his wife, speaking lovingly to her and making her feel special.

For a moment, we enjoy the scene. Then, quietly, our mind may say, “Why doesn’t my husband speak to me like that?” Or, “Why isn’t my wife more affectionate like this?”

But we have just compared our real life with a few carefully written minutes on a screen.

Their dialogue was written. The scene was rehearsed. The music was added. Our life is simply happening, with tired days, responsibilities, misunderstandings and ordinary moments. Yet the comparison can feel real.

Social media makes this even easier. We see someone’s holiday, beautiful home, family photograph, achievement or celebration. We see a few happy moments and compare them with the complete reality of our own life.

Perhaps the problem is not that we notice what others have.

The problem begins when what others have becomes the measure of what we think our own life is worth.

There is another interesting side to this.

We quickly notice someone who has more than us. But how often do we notice someone who has less and quietly think, “Bhagavan, thank You for what You have given me”?

We look upwards and feel inadequate. We rarely look around us and feel grateful.

And there will always be somebody ahead of us.

A bigger house. A better car. More money. A more successful child. More recognition. More opportunities.

If we keep looking only upwards, there may never be a point at which we feel we have enough.

This brings me back to a story from the Bhagavatham which we have already covered in an earlier TFD.

It is the story of Dhruva. We know the story. But perhaps it is worth looking at the same story once again from this particular angle.

Dhruva was the young son of King Uttanapada. The king had two wives, Suniti and Suruchi. Dhruva was Suniti’s son, while Uttama was Suruchi’s son.

One day, Dhruva saw his father sitting on the throne with Uttama on his lap. Like any little child, he too wanted to sit on his father’s lap.He went towards his father.But Suruchi stopped him.

She told him that he did not have that privilege because he was not her son. If he wanted that position, she said, he would have to be born again as her son.

For us, this may sound like palace politics. For a little boy, it was much simpler.

“My brother is sitting on my father’s lap. Why can’t I?”

That simple hurt went very deep. Dhruva ran to his mother crying. Suniti had no power to change the situation. She could only console him and tell him to seek Bhagavan.

But something had already changed inside Dhruva. He no longer wanted only his father’s affection.

He wanted to prove himself. He wanted a position that nobody could take away from him. He wanted to become greater.

Dhruva went into the forest and performed intense penance to see Bhagavan. Narada met him and tried to help him understand his feelings, but Dhruva remained determined.

Finally, Bhagavan appeared before him.

And this is the part of the story that touches me.

The little boy who had gone into the forest because he felt small was now standing before Bhagavan Himself.

The very comparison that had consumed him suddenly seemed insignificant.

Dhruva realised that what he had been desperately seeking was very small compared with what he had now received.

The Bhagavatham gives us a beautiful image: it was like someone searching for pieces of broken glass and suddenly finding a priceless jewel.

Dhruva had gone looking for something small. He had found something immeasurably greater.

When I look at this story in this context, I wonder whether something similar happens to us in much smaller ways.

Someone gets something we wanted…..Someone receives appreciation…..Someone’s child achieves something…..Someone has a beautiful home……Someone seems to have a better marriage.

For a few moments, we feel that we are somehow less. But perhaps they haven’t become greater. We have simply started measuring ourselves against them.

And perhaps there is no need to feel guilty about this. It is simply something to notice.

The next time that little feeling comes, perhaps we can pause for a moment and ask ourselves:

“Why did that bother me?”

“What exactly am I comparing?”

“Am I inspired by this, or am I feeling inadequate?”

We don’t have to immediately find an answer. Sometimes simply noticing what is happening inside us is enough. Because once we notice it, something changes.

We can admire without comparing……We can learn without feeling inferior…. We can see a beautiful home without feeling that ours is inadequate…. We can see a happy couple and simply wish them happiness….. We can hear about someone’s successful child without turning it into a judgement about our own.

Their blessing does not have to become our deficiency. Perhaps we can also learn to look in the other direction.

When we see someone who has less than us, perhaps instead of feeling superior, we can quietly feel grateful.

A spouse who has stood beside us for years…..Children who still need us…..Friends who care…..A home to return to……Food on the table……The ability to see, hear, walk, speak, travel and enjoy simple things.

Many of these have become so familiar that we have stopped seeing them as blessings. Maybe that is where the change begins.

Not through philosophy. Just through awareness. There is nothing wrong with wanting to improve. There is nothing wrong with ambition.

Ambition says: “I want to become better.”

Comparison says: “I want to be better than you.”

One brings us back to our own journey. The other takes us into somebody else’s.

Perhaps our real progress is not in becoming more than another person.

It is in becoming a little better than who we were yesterday.

A little more grateful.

A little more peaceful.

A little less dependent on appreciation.

A little more capable of being happy when someone else is happy.

Maybe this is what Dhruva’s story quietly reminds us.

He spent enormous energy trying to become greater because he felt smaller.

But when he finally found Bhagavan, he realised that what he had been seeking was very small compared with what he had received.

Perhaps we too sometimes spend too much energy trying to be “more” and forget to notice how much we already have.

Someone else’s life is not the measuring scale for ours.

Their success does not make our life smaller. We feel smaller only when we use someone else’s life to measure our own.

Perhaps that is the only question I would like to carry with me today:

“Am I looking at someone else’s life with admiration, or am I quietly using it to judge my own?”

Maybe simply noticing the difference is enough.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

PS: Please continue sending your thoughts, questions and suggestions. They genuinely help me discover what we should reflect upon together - Jani /9845204620

@@ 241 | Epic Series 111 | When Did We Become So Calculative? | 
There is something I have been noticing, and perhaps many of us will recognise it in ourselves.

We have slowly become very good at calculating. Almost everything has a price now. Even when something is being done for a good cause, our first thought is often not, “How can I help?” but, “How much should I give?”

Imagine a friend calling and saying, “We are organising a small satsang. We need some support. Can you contribute?” We may genuinely want the satsang to happen. We may even attend it and enjoy it.

But the moment we are asked to contribute, our mind starts working. “How much are others giving?” “Why should I give so much?” “They are already collecting from many people.” “Let me give something small.” We may finally give an amount that does not really hurt us, and feel that we have done our part.

There is nothing wrong with being careful with money. In fact, many of us were brought up that way. Our parents taught us not to waste money. They taught us to save for tomorrow, live within our means and think before spending. These are good values. But somewhere along the way, being careful with money can quietly become being afraid to give.

The interesting thing is that we are not always equally careful when we spend on ourselves. We may think nothing of spending several thousand rupees on a restaurant, a holiday, a new gadget, something for the house or something we simply like.

We may make the purchase without much thought because it gives us pleasure. But when the same money is requested for a good cause, suddenly we become extremely careful.

We say, “We have to spend carefully.” We say, “There are so many other expenses.” We say, “Someone else will contribute.” All of that may be true. But sometimes, if we are completely honest with ourselves, we may realise that the issue is not really affordability.

We simply find it harder to spend money when the pleasure comes to someone else rather than to us.

This is not only about money. It is about the way we have started looking at giving itself. We want good satsangs to happen. We want temples to function well. We want music and spiritual programmes to continue. We want good organisations to serve society. We want someone to help the poor, educate children and care for the sick. We are happy when all these things happen.

But when it is our turn to support them, we sometimes step back and think, “Why should I? Surely someone else can do it.”

Slowly, without realising it, we can become people who want the world to remain generous while we ourselves become more and more calculating.

There is a beautiful incident in the Bhagavatham that makes us look at this tendency in a very different way.

The Weaver of Mathura

When Krishna and Balarama entered Mathura, they were still dressed like simple cowherd boys from Brindavan. They were young and radiant, but they did not look like wealthy princes entering a great city.

As they walked through the streets of Mathura, they came across a royal washerman carrying beautiful clothes meant for the king. Krishna asked him for some clothes. The washerman refused. He saw Krishna as a village boy who had no right to ask for royal clothes. Perhaps he was afraid of losing his position. Perhaps he was worried about the king. Perhaps he simply did not want to part with something that was not his to give.

Whatever the reason, he saw only the risk of giving. He did not see the person standing before him.

Krishna moved on.

Soon after, He met a humble weaver. This man was not a royal official. He was not a rich merchant. He earned his living through his own hands and his own skill. He made clothes for a living.

When the weaver saw Krishna and Balarama, his heart was filled with joy. He did not start asking questions. He did not ask who they were, how much they would pay or what he would get in return. He simply saw two beautiful young boys and felt that they deserved beautiful clothes.

So he brought out the finest garments he had made and offered them to Krishna and Balarama.

Think about what that meant for the weaver. These were not old clothes that he no longer needed. They were not leftovers. He had spent his time making them. He had used his skill. They represented his work and his livelihood. He could have sold them and earned money.

Yet when Krishna stood before him, something more important than calculation took over.

He gave.

He did not know what would happen next. He did not know that Krishna would bless him. He did not know that this simple act would change his life. He simply gave because his heart wanted to give.

Krishna accepted the clothes with love and blessed the weaver abundantly. He received prosperity, strength, fame and, most importantly, spiritual grace.

The weaver gave Krishna clothes. Krishna gave the weaver something far greater.

And this is where the story becomes relevant to us.

The washerman and the weaver were both faced with an opportunity. Both had something valuable. But they responded very differently. One thought about what he might lose. The other thought about what he could give.

One saw a transaction. The other saw a person. One protected what was in his hands. The other opened his hands. Perhaps that is the real lesson of this story.

We often think that generosity is about having a lot of money. It is not. Generosity is a state of mind. A person can have very little and still have a generous heart. Another person can have everything and still find it difficult to give even a little.

We may have seen this in our own lives. Someone may spend ₹10,000 on something they want without thinking twice. But when a friend asks for ₹10,000 towards a meaningful cause, the same person may spend days thinking about whether it is really necessary.

The amount has not changed. The person asking has changed. In one case, we are giving ourselves pleasure. In the other, we are giving somebody else an opportunity.

That is why giving can be uncomfortable. When we spend on ourselves, something comes back immediately. We get a meal, a holiday, a new phone, a beautiful object or some comfort. But when we give to a good cause, something leaves our hands and we may never see a personal return.

That is exactly what makes giving special. If we give only when we know what we will get back, it is not really giving. It is an exchange.

True giving begins when we can say, “This matters to me. I believe in it. I have enough to share. So I will give.”

We should certainly be responsible with money. We have families to look after. We have future needs. We should not give recklessly. But there is a difference between being responsible and being unnecessarily tight-fisted.

There is a difference between saving wisely and being unable to part with anything.

Our parents taught us to save. Perhaps now we need to teach ourselves another lesson: we must also learn to give.

We can give money. We can give time. We can give our knowledge. We can give encouragement. We can give someone an opportunity. We can give our attention to an elderly person who simply wants someone to listen. We can support a satsang we believe in. We can help someone who is trying to do something good. We can quietly make someone’s difficult day a little easier.

And perhaps we should stop keeping an account of these things.

“I did this for him.”

“I gave this much.”

“I helped her when nobody else did.”

“I was there for them.”

The moment we start keeping a mental account, giving begins to feel like an investment. We start waiting for the return. When it does not come, we become disappointed and say, “After everything I did for them, this is what I get.”

Perhaps the real art of giving is to give and let it go.

We should not give because we want people to praise us. We should not give because we want our name displayed somewhere. We should not give because we expect the same person to help us tomorrow. We should give because we believe something is worth supporting and because we have been fortunate enough to contribute.

The amount is not always important. What matters is the spirit in which it is given.

A small amount given freely can carry more meaning than a large amount given grudgingly. A few minutes given with genuine attention can mean more to someone than an expensive gift. A simple act of kindness can stay in another person’s memory for years.

Perhaps this is what the weaver understood.

He did not ask, “What will I get?”

He saw Krishna, felt joy and gave what he had.

And Krishna did not merely return the value of what he had received. He blessed the giver far beyond what the giver could have imagined.

That is the mystery of generosity.

We think something is leaving our hands. But something is entering our heart.

The more we learn to give without calculation, the less frightened we become of losing. We begin to understand that abundance is not only about how much we possess. It is also about how freely we can share what we possess.

So the next time we are asked to support something good, perhaps we can pause before our usual calculation begins. We do not have to give more than we can afford. But if we genuinely believe in the cause, let us not automatically look for reasons not to give.

Let us ask ourselves one simple question: “Can I contribute something meaningfully?”

If the answer is yes, let us give with happiness and move on. No announcement. No expectation. No mental account.

Because perhaps the greatest blessing of giving is not what the other person receives.

It is what giving does to us.

It makes the heart softer. It makes us less self-centred. It reminds us that everything we have is not entirely ours. And slowly, it changes our relationship with wealth, people and life itself.

The weaver gave Krishna his finest clothes. He may have thought he was giving away something valuable.

But in that moment, he received something far more valuable.

He discovered the joy of an open heart.

Perhaps that is the lesson for all of us.

Save what you need. Spend what you can. But when something truly good comes before you, do not let calculation close your hands.

Because one day, we may realise that the things we carefully kept for ourselves are gone.

But the things we freely gave may still be living in someone else’s life.

Dhsnyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

Ps: Please share your thoughts/ feedback/questions if any directly to me - Jani 9845204620

@@ 240 | Epic Series 110 | We live in the same house, But different worlds | 
A reader sent me a message, “Jani , please write something about today’s children. Their attitude and behaviour have become very difficult. They seem impatient and insensitive. Their tantrums and the way they respond to parents can be painful.”

I am sure many parents will relate to this. This is not about blaming an entire generation. There are many wonderful and caring young people. But perhaps there is something happening around us that is worth looking at quietly.

Think of a normal evening at home. Mother has spent time preparing dinner. Father comes home tired. Grandmother is sitting in the hall. The child comes home with earphones on and goes straight to the bedroom. Mother calls, “Come and eat.”

The answer is, “Two minutes.” She calls again. “I am coming.” Twenty minutes pass. Finally, when she gets irritated, the child comes out and says, “Why are you shouting? I told you I was coming.” Everyone is under the same roof. Yet nobody is really with anybody.

Sometimes the virtual world seems more important than the real world at home. A child may know who has broken up, who is dating whom, who went to a party and who unfollowed whom. They may know every new reel and every new trend.

But they may not notice that their mother is tired or that their father is worried. They may not notice that grandmother has been sitting alone. They know what is happening everywhere. But they may not know what is happening at home.

Now imagine another scene. The wife has prepared dinner. She is waiting for everyone to come and eat. Suddenly the calling bell rings. The husband opens the door. A Swiggy delivery person is standing there with a food packet. The husband is puzzled. “Who ordered this?”

Before he can understand, one bedroom door opens. The son or daughter comes out, takes the packet and quietly goes back inside. The husband looks at his wife. The wife looks at him. Neither knows what to say. Someone in Room 1 or Room 2 has ordered food for themselves, even though food is already waiting in the kitchen.

It sounds funny. But it also says something about the way we live today. A house with three bedrooms can sometimes feel like three different houses.

One child is in Room 1. Another is in Room 2. The parents are in their room. Everyone has their own phone, friends, entertainment and world. Food is ordered separately. Entertainment is watched separately. Even communication can happen separately.

Sometimes husband and wife are lying on the same bed, only a few feet apart, and are still communicating through WhatsApp. “Did you pay the bill?” “What time are you coming?” “Please remind the driver.” The messages may be useful. But something is missing. Two people who are physically together are slowly losing the habit of simply talking to each other.

Even the family WhatsApp group has become the heartbeat of many families. Good morning messages, birthday wishes, photographs, festival greetings and forwards keep everyone connected. We know what everyone has posted. But do we know what everyone is feeling? We may know the status of someone’s life without knowing the state of their heart.

Perhaps this is the strange part of our times. We have never had so many ways to communicate, yet simple personal communication is slowly disappearing.

We send a message when we could speak. We send an emoji when we could smile. We forward a greeting when we could make a phone call.

Our children are growing up in this world. So perhaps we should not ask only, “What is wrong with today’s children?” We can also ask ourselves, “What are they seeing around them?”

When parents try to understand their children, they may hear, “Why do you want to know?” “Why are you asking me so many questions?” “Please give me some space.” “It is my life.” Every young person needs some privacy. But sometimes the parent is not trying to control the child. The parent is simply trying to know, “Are you okay?”

The emotional world of young people can also be very fast and intense. A friendship can suddenly become very important. A message may not come. Someone may block someone. There may be an argument or a break-up.

The child may become upset, stop eating or stay awake late into the night. The next week everything may be normal again. Parents often watch this roller coaster without knowing what to do.

Peer pressure adds to this. “Everyone is going.” “Everyone is allowed.” “Everyone has one.” “Everyone’s parents are okay with it.” Sometimes “everyone” may mean only a few friends. But to a young person it can feel like the whole world. Nobody wants to be left out. Nobody wants to miss the party, the trip or the sleepover.

Social media makes this pressure stronger. We are not only living our lives. We are showing our lives. A birthday becomes photographs. A holiday becomes posts. A party becomes stories and reels. Sometimes the child is not fully enjoying the moment. The child is busy showing everyone else that the moment is wonderful.

Perhaps this is why some children appear insensitive. It may not always be because they do not care. Their attention is constantly being pulled somewhere else. There is another message, another video, another notification, another friend and another comparison. Slowly, they may stop noticing the quiet things happening around them.

But there is a beautiful thought in the Mahabharata through Vidura. He repeatedly tried to make Dhritarashtra see what was happening around him. Dhritarashtra was physically blind. But there is another kind of blindness. We can have good eyes and still fail to see what is right in front of us.

Today we can see what is happening anywhere in the world within seconds. But can we see that our wife is tired? Can we see that our husband is worried? Can we see that our child is unhappy? Can we see that an elderly parent is lonely? Perhaps seeing another person is not only about our eyes. It is about giving them our attention.

Perhaps there is no need for a big solution. We can start with something very simple. Tomorrow, if a child is watching something on the phone, instead of immediately saying, “Put that phone away,” we could ask, “What are you watching?” We may not like the answer. We may not even understand it. But at least we have entered their world for a moment.

We could also create small reasons to be together. “Shall we go out for an ice cream?” There need not be any special occasion. We can simply go. Sit across the table. Look at each other. Talk about their friends, their interests, their worries and the things that matter to them. Not every conversation needs to be about studies, money, phones or rules.

And perhaps we need to remember that their world is not our world. We grew up in a different time, with different pressures, different friendships and different ways of communicating. It is easy to look at today’s children through the template of our own childhood and say, “In our days, we never did this.” But our childhood cannot always be the measuring scale for theirs.

This does not mean that everything they do is right. Children still need boundaries. They need to learn respect, responsibility, patience and the ability to accept a “no”. But perhaps we can first try to understand what is happening in their world before deciding how to respond.

Empathy does not mean agreeing with everything. It simply means trying to see the world from the other person’s side before judging it from our own.

If a child says, “Give me some space,” perhaps we can give the space without becoming distant. We can simply say, “Okay. Take your time. I am here whenever you want to talk.” No lecture. No ten questions. Just an open door.

And perhaps we parents can make one small change too. When someone at home speaks to us, let us look up. If our spouse is telling us something, let us finish listening before checking the next message. If an elderly parent starts talking, let us give them a few minutes. If the family is eating, perhaps the phone can wait.

These are very small things. But family life is made up of small things. A meal together. A conversation. A few minutes of listening. An unexpected ice cream. A walk. A laugh. A question asked with genuine interest.

Our children do not need perfect parents. And parents do not need perfect children. We are all trying to understand a changing world.

Perhaps tomorrow, instead of trying to change our children, we can first try to understand them a little better. Instead of seeing them through our own filters, we can try to see the world through their eyes.

And perhaps, when we do that, something beautiful may happen. They may also begin to look at the world through our eyes.

The phone will still be there. The messages will still come. The reels will still be waiting. But the people we love will not always be sitting beside us.

Perhaps we can look up a little more often. Perhaps we can talk a little more. Perhaps we can listen a little longer.

And perhaps we may discover that the person we were trying to reach through WhatsApp, Instagram or a family group was actually sitting right beside us all along.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 239 | Epic Series 109 | Are we giving ourselves enough time to be with ourselves? | 
I sometimes wonder what has happened to our relationship with silence.

Earlier, if we had a few free minutes, we simply sat. We watched people passing by, listened to music, looked out of the window, or allowed our thoughts to wander. Today, the moment there is a little gap, our hand naturally reaches for the phone.

There is nothing wrong with staying connected. Our groups, conversations and messages can bring friendship, information and joy. But I have been noticing something in my own life.

A few days ago, I was sitting at home with my phone beside me. I had actually planned to spend some quiet time reading.

Then came a message. I opened it. Before I could finish reading, another message appeared in a different group. Then a photo. Then a forwarded video. Someone had asked a question. Someone else had replied. I felt I should acknowledge one message. Then another.

Twenty minutes passed.

I suddenly realised that I had not actually chosen to read any of those things. I had simply followed whatever happened to arrive in front of me. It made me smile.

The phone had not demanded my time. I had given it my attention one notification at a time. And perhaps that is how mental clutter quietly builds.

The more groups we are part of, the more our mind keeps its antennas tuned to every little sound. A message arrives and we feel we should respond. If we don’t, perhaps someone may think we are ignoring them or have changed.

Sometimes, we respond not because we really want to, but because we worry that our silence may be misunderstood.

Slowly, without realising it, our attention gets divided.

Perhaps occasionally stepping back from a few busy spaces can help. It is not a reflection of how we feel about the people there. Sometimes, we simply need a little quiet space for ourselves.

A simple message saying,

“I am taking a little break from the group for some time. Nothing personal. I just need a little quiet space. I hope you will understand.”

is enough.

Those who know us will understand. And those who matter to us need not mistake our silence for indifference.

Perhaps digital detox is not about disconnecting from people. It is about disconnecting from unnecessary noise, to help us focus better.

I have also noticed that when we choose something with a purpose, our attention becomes sharper.

If we genuinely want to learn something, we look for the right book, the right teacher, the right music or the right resource. Because we have chosen it, we listen differently.

Perhaps the same can apply to our digital lives.

We don’t have to consume everything simply because it has arrived before us.

We can choose what we read. What we listen to. Whom we engage with. And, just as importantly, what we allow ourselves to ignore.

Our epics remind us of the power of such attention. When Arjuna was asked to aim at the eye of the fish, he was not distracted by everything around him. His attention was on what mattered.

Our lives may not require such intense concentration, but perhaps they do require a little more selectiveness.

Sometimes, we can put the phone aside and listen to a song fully. Read a few pages. Take a walk. Pray. Sit with someone and talk without looking at a screen.

Or simply sit quietly. Perhaps that is when something interesting happens.

When the outside noise becomes softer, we may begin to hear something within us.

Our own thoughts.

Our own feelings.

Perhaps even something we had been too busy to notice. Maybe that too is a small form of meditation.

To pause.

To be present.

To listen.

And to be comfortable in our own company.

Perhaps we don’t always need more connection. Sometimes, we may simply need a little more space within ourselves.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 238 | Epic Series 108 | How do I make peace with what I cannot change? | 
A reader wrote to me recently.

She said, “I am 67 now. When I look back at my life, there are some things I still cannot accept. There were family problems that were never resolved. There were people who hurt me and never apologised. There were decisions I made that I still regret. I have tried to forgive, but sometimes the old pain comes back. I keep asking myself, why did all this happen? How do I make peace with things that I cannot change?”

Many of us may have something similar in our lives. It may be a brother with whom we have not spoken properly for years. A child whose choices hurt us. A marriage in which some wounds were never discussed. A friend who betrayed us. A decision we wish we had taken differently. Or simply something someone said twenty years ago that we still remember word for word.

The strange thing about old pain is that the incident may have ended long ago. But inside us, it may still be alive. The person who hurt us may have forgotten it completely.

We have not.

I was reminded of Bhishma.

After the Kurukshetra war, Bhishma lay on a bed of arrows. He had seen almost everything life could show a human being : love, loyalty, betrayal, war, loss and death.

Yudhishthira came to him. The war had been won, but Yudhishthira was not happy. So many people he loved were dead. He had become king, but his heart was filled with questions.

Why did all this have to happen? Why did so many people have to suffer? Could things not have been different?

Even with Krishna beside them, the Pandavas had suffered. Having God with us does not mean that life will never hurt us.

Bhishma did not try to give an answer to every question. He spoke to Yudhishthira about dharma and about how one should live when life does not go according to our wishes.

And when he had said all that he could, Bhishma turned his mind towards Krishna.

There is something very deep in that moment. Bhishma did not need every question answered before he could become peaceful.

Perhaps we don’t either.

I remember a situation where two brothers had not spoken properly for many years because of a property dispute. Both felt they were right. Both were waiting for the other to apologise.

Then one day, one of them became seriously ill.

Suddenly, the property did not seem so important. The old argument did not seem so important. There were only two ageing brothers sitting together, remembering the childhood they once shared.

One of them reached out and held the other’s hand. No one said, “You were right.” No one said, “I was wrong.”

But both began to cry. Sometimes we don’t really want to win the argument. We simply want the person back.

Perhaps this happens in many families. A mother waits for her son to understand her sacrifices. A husband waits for his wife to acknowledge an old hurt. A daughter waits for her father to say the words she always wanted to hear.

And then, sometimes, there is no more time.

That is why, as we grow older, perhaps one question is worth asking ourselves:

How much more of my life do I want to give to something that has already happened?

This does not mean that what happened was right. It does not mean that we should forget. It simply means that we do not want yesterday to keep hurting us every day.

There may be things we can still repair. There may be people we can forgive. And there may be some things that we can do nothing about.

Perhaps those things can be placed at the feet of Krishna.

And then we can turn towards the people who are still with us.

If there is someone we love, let us tell them. If there is someone we have been avoiding, perhaps we can take one small step. If there is someone sitting beside us every day, let us not assume they will always be there.

Because one day, we may look at an old photograph and wish for just five more minutes.

Five more minutes to hear that voice.

Five more minutes to hold that hand.

Five more minutes to say, “I am sorry.”

Five more minutes to say, “Thank you.”

Five more minutes to say, “I love you.”

Perhaps we have those five minutes today. And maybe what we need to do differently tomorrow is not something big.

Maybe it is just one phone call we have been postponing. Maybe it is sitting with our spouse for a few minutes without looking at our phone. Maybe it is calling our brother or sister. Maybe it is hugging our child a little longer.

Maybe it is saying “thank you” to someone whose presence we have taken for granted.

Or maybe it is simply stopping ourselves before saying something hurtful and asking, “Is this really worth hurting someone I love?”

Because we often think that the precious moments of life are the big ones weddings, holidays, achievements and celebrations.

But when we look back, perhaps what we miss most are the simple things.

The morning tea we had together. The familiar voice calling us from another room. The little argument about something completely unimportant. The hand that reached for ours while crossing the road. The person sitting beside us while we watched television.

The ordinary evening when everyone was still there. We may not realise the value of these moments while they are happening.

But one day, these ordinary moments may become the memories we would give anything to have again.

Perhaps that is why Bhishma’s final moments are relevant to us. He could not change what had happened. He could not bring back the people who were gone. He could not rewrite his past.

But he could decide where to place his mind at the end. He chose Krishna. Perhaps we too can slowly learn to leave some things with Him.

We cannot change everything that happened yesterday. But tomorrow morning, we can choose what we do with today.

We can carry the old hurt again. Or we can put a little of it down.

We can continue waiting for someone to understand us. Or we can take the first small step.

We can keep asking why something happened. Or we can ask ourselves, “What can I do with the life that is still in my hands?”

And perhaps, many years from now, when we look back at our own life, what will matter most will not be whether every argument was settled or whether everyone finally admitted that we were right.

It may simply be whether we loved the people who were with us. Whether we said the words that needed to be said. Whether we forgave when we could. Whether we were present while the people we loved were still present.

Because one day, there may be no tomorrow. And on that day, we may not wish for another house, another car, another achievement or another argument to be won.

We may simply wish for one more ordinary day with someone we loved. Perhaps that ordinary day is what we have been given today.

Let us not miss it.

Thought for the day

We may never understand everything that happened in our life.

We may never get every apology. We may never get every answer.

But we can decide what we carry into tomorrow.

Let us leave some questions with Him. Let us put down a little of the old hurt. Let us call someone we love. Let us say the words we have been postponing. Let us hold one hand a little longer.

Because the past has already had its time. Tomorrow, let us give a little more of ourselves to the people who are still here.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 237 | Epic Series 107 | When someone we love is no longer there | 
TFD 235 was about the loneliness that can come when someone we love is no longer with us.

After reading it, one of our readers shared a deeply moving story with me. I am sharing it anonymously because, in a way, this is not just her story. It is a story about love, loss and the strange courage that life sometimes asks of us.

More than fifty years ago, this reader lost her elder brother. He was only 23. After graduating from IIT, he had gone to the US for his master’s. One day, he met with a terrible accident. He survived for five days, but eventually passed away.

His mother was only 41. Her husband was bedridden, so she travelled alone to America for the first time in her life, carrying the hope of seeing her son and bringing him home. But by the time she reached there, he was gone.

Imagine that journey. A mother travelling across the world, holding on to the hope that she would see her son again, only to arrive and discover that she had arrived too late. There are some moments in life for which words are simply not enough. No advice can make them easier. No philosophy can remove the pain.

And yet, what this mother did next stayed with me. With the help of her son’s friends and the Indian consulate, she brought him back home. Then she asked to meet the young man who had been driving the car. Her son was the only one who died among the four friends travelling in that car.

She could have blamed him. She could have shouted at him. She could have asked, “Why did this happen to my son?” Instead, she told him that she understood it was an accident and that she did not blame him.

Why would a mother who had just lost her 23 year old son think about the young man responsible for the accident?

Because she did not want him to carry that guilt for the rest of his life. She was carrying unbearable pain, yet she could still see another human being who was also hurting.

That does not mean she did not grieve. It does not mean she did not cry. It simply means that her pain did not take away her goodness. Perhaps that is a very different kind of strength not the strength to stop crying or to “move on” quickly, but the strength to carry our pain without allowing the pain to destroy the goodness inside us.

This story reminded me of another mother from our scriptures.

Uttara and Abhimanyu

Uttara was the young wife of Abhimanyu. Abhimanyu was barely out of his youth when he entered the Kurukshetra battlefield. He knew the danger, yet he entered the Chakravyuha and fought with extraordinary courage. He did not return.

For Uttara, the war was no longer something happening somewhere on a distant battlefield. The war had entered her home. The young husband with whom she had imagined growing old was suddenly gone. There would be no more conversations with him, no more waiting for him to return, and no more ordinary little moments that we usually do not even notice while they are happening.

And then came another painful truth. Uttara was pregnant. She had lost her husband, but she was carrying his child. And even that child was not safe. Ashwatthama released the Brahmastra with the intention of destroying the Pandava lineage, and the weapon was directed towards the unborn child.

Uttara ran to Krishna and pleaded with Him to protect her baby. Krishna protected the child. The baby survived and was named Parikshit. Years later, Parikshit became the king who listened to the Bhagavatham from Sukadeva.

There is something deeply moving in this story. Abhimanyu was gone. Uttara could not bring him back, and she could not continue the life she had imagined with him. But life had left something precious in her hands his child, a living connection to the husband she had lost.

Uttara had to grieve, but she also had to go on. And this is an important distinction. Going on does not mean forgetting. It means learning to live with the loss without allowing the loss to take away the rest of our life.

Many of us may face this in different forms. Sometimes it is the death of a parent after decades of love. Sometimes it is the loss of a spouse after forty or fifty years together. Sometimes a child moves to another country, and suddenly the house that was once full of noise becomes quiet.

Sometimes a close friend moves away. Sometimes a marriage ends. Sometimes our health changes. Sometimes a little pet dies, and the house suddenly feels strangely empty.

And sometimes we lose something that nobody else can see a dream, a relationship we thought would last forever, or a future we had carefully imagined. Life can take away the very thing around which we had built our picture of tomorrow. The mind then asks, “How will I live without this?”

Perhaps the answer is not to replace what we have lost. Some things simply cannot be replaced. A mother cannot be replaced. A husband cannot be replaced. A child cannot be replaced. A dear friend cannot be replaced. Even a little pet cannot be replaced. And perhaps they are not meant to be.

We do not have to replace them. We have to learn to make a place for their absence in our lives.

In the beginning, the absence can fill the entire room. Every corner reminds us of them. A photograph can bring tears. A familiar song can suddenly hurt. We may even find ourselves turning to tell them something, only to remember that we cannot. The ordinary things we once took for granted suddenly become reminders of what is no longer there.

But slowly, very slowly, something begins to change. The relationship takes a different form. They are no longer physically beside us, but their love remains. Their memories remain. Their values remain. Their voice may still live inside our head. Something they once told us may suddenly guide us.

One day, we may do something kind and think, “This is exactly what they would have done.” We may hear a song they loved, cook something they taught us, repeat something they used to say, or help someone because that is what they always did. And in those moments, we begin to understand that they have not disappeared completely. They have become a part of us.

The reader who shared this story with me told me that even after all these years, her family continued to speak about her brother. The younger generation knew about him. They knew what kind of person he was. His life had become part of the family’s story.

Perhaps that is how some people continue to live. Not through their physical presence, but through the lives they touched. If they were kind, we become a little kinder. If they helped people, we help someone. If they loved music, we sing. If they valued family, we hold our family a little closer. If they made us laugh, we allow ourselves to laugh again. And if they taught us something beautiful, we pass it on.

Their life then continues through us.

Perhaps this is what Uttara’s story quietly teaches us. Life may break the plans we had made. It may take away someone we thought would always be there. It may leave us standing in the middle of a life we never imagined living. But even then, something precious may remain in our hands a child, a relationship, a memory, a value, a responsibility, a purpose, a friend, or sometimes simply another morning.

We cannot always choose what life takes away. But slowly, we can learn to ask another question: “What has life left with me?”

That question can change everything. Instead of looking only at the empty chair, we begin to notice the people still sitting around us. Instead of only remembering what we lost, we begin to feel grateful for what we were blessed to have. Instead of endlessly asking, “Why did this happen to me?”, we slowly begin asking, “What can I do with the life that remains?”

The pain may not disappear, and perhaps it does not have to. Grief is, in some ways, love that has suddenly lost its familiar place to go. But with time, we can give that love a new direction. We can put it into our children, our friendships, our prayer, our music, our work, or into helping someone who is hurting.

We can allow the person we lost to change the way we live. If their life taught us something, we can carry that lesson forward. If their love made us stronger, we can use that strength to make someone else’s life a little easier. In that way, the person we lost continues to touch the world through us.

Perhaps that is how we carry someone forward. We do not leave them behind. We take them with us.

Thought for the day

Perhaps the goal is not to “get over” a loss. Some losses are simply too deep for that. Perhaps the goal is to learn how to carry the loss and still carry on with life—to cry when we need to, to remember without guilt, and to smile without feeling that we are betraying the person we lost.

Slowly, we may discover that life can still hold meaning, even though it is no longer the life we once knew. We may even discover that the loss, while it never becomes something we wanted, has changed us in ways we could not have imagined. It can make us more compassionate, more grateful, more present and more sensitive to another person’s pain.

And if someone we love is still with us today, let us not wait for loss to teach us their value. Let us sit with them a little longer. Let us talk to them, listen to them, hold their hand and tell them what our heart wants to say. Because one day, when we can no longer hold that hand, we may realise that those ordinary moments were actually some of the most precious moments of our life.

We cannot decide how long someone will walk beside us. But while they are walking beside us, let us make sure they know how deeply they are loved. And when they are no longer beside us, may we have the courage to keep walking—not because we have forgotten them, but because we are carrying their love with us.

Perhaps, after some time, we may even understand something that brings both tears and peace: the person we lost is no longer walking beside us, but their love has become part of the way we walk.

And maybe that is one of the most beautiful ways a life can continue.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️
