@@ 236 | Epic Series 106 | Tell God what you want. Then let Him decide | 
This morning, a reader sent me a beautiful question. She told me about something her mother had taught her about prayer. Her mother used to say, “First, thank God for everything. Then pray for the good of everyone.

And when you ask God for something, don’t be too specific. Don’t say, ‘God, I want this particular job.’ Don’t say, ‘God, my child must get this particular opportunity.’ Just say, ‘God, please give us what is good for us.’”

Her mother had taught her this for a simple reason. Something we want very badly today may not be good for us tomorrow. Sometimes we hold on to one wish with all our heart, only to understand much later why God did not give it to us.

But the reader was confused. Today, she often hears people saying something very different. They say we should be very clear about what we want. They say we should ask for exactly what we want. They say we should even manifest it.

So she asked, “When I pray, should I tell God exactly what I want? Or should I simply say, ‘God, give me what is good for me,’ and leave everything to Him?”

I found her question very beautiful because, if we are honest, many of us have stood at this same place.

We have something very close to our heart. We want it badly. We pray for it. We hope for it. And yet, somewhere inside, there is another small voice asking, “What if God has something else in mind for me?”

Her question took me to a beautiful story from the Bhagavatam. The story of Dhruva.

Dhruva was a very young boy. One day, something happened that hurt him deeply. His father, King Uttanapada, was sitting on his throne. Dhruva wanted to sit on his father’s lap. It was such a small wish. He was only a little boy. He simply wanted the love of his father.

But when Dhruva tried to sit on his father’s lap, his stepmother stopped him. She told him that he had no right to sit there. Those words went straight into the heart of the little boy.

Dhruva ran to his mother, crying. His mother tried to comfort him. But the hurt was too deep. Somewhere inside him, a strong thought had taken root. He wanted to become so great that nobody would ever be able to look down upon him again. So Dhruva decided to go to the forest and pray to God.

He was only a child, but his determination was extraordinary. On the way, he met the great sage Narada. Narada saw that Dhruva was very young. He tried to calm him. He told him that anger and hurt should not decide the course of his life. He tried to make him turn back.

But Dhruva would not change his mind. His heart had one clear wish. He wanted to see God.

Narada then showed him how to pray and meditate on the Lord. Dhruva went deep into the forest and began his tapas. Day after day, he became more and more focused. Slowly, he gave up food. Then he gave up water. His mind became completely fixed on the Lord.

Dhruva was asking God for something very specific. He wanted a place of greatness. He wanted a position that nobody could take away from him. He wanted to prove that he was worthy.

And then, after his intense prayer, something wonderful happened. The Lord appeared before him.

Imagine that moment. A little boy who had gone into the forest carrying so much hurt suddenly saw the Lord Himself standing before him. The One whom he had been searching for was now right in front of him.

Dhruva became completely overwhelmed. The wish that had brought him to the forest suddenly looked very small. He had gone to God asking for a kingdom, but now he had found the King of the whole universe. He had gone asking for a place that nobody could take away, but now he had found the One who could never be lost.

Dhruva realised that he had been asking for something very small when something infinitely greater was already before him. His heart had changed simply because he had found the Lord.

The Lord did not have to tell him that his desire was wrong. Just seeing the Lord was enough. Dhruva understood for himself that he had come looking for a small gift, but had found something far greater.

That, to me, is the most beautiful part of Dhruva’s story. Dhruva did not hide his desire from God. He asked for exactly what he wanted. He was clear. He was determined. He prayed with his whole heart. And yet, when he came close to God, his own heart changed.

Perhaps this gives us a beautiful answer to the reader’s question. There is nothing wrong in telling God what we want. If we want a particular job, we can ask. If we want our child to get a particular opportunity, we can ask.

If we want good health, peace, success, a relationship, or some change in our life, we can ask. We don’t have to be afraid of telling God what is in our heart.

Even when we try to manifest something, we are expressing a desire. There is nothing wrong in that. The important thing is what we do after we ask.

Perhaps we can say, “God, this is what I want. I will pray for it. I will work for it. I will give it my best. But You know what I cannot see. If this is good for me, please give it to me. And if something better is meant for me, please give me the wisdom to accept that.”

That one little sentence brings surrender into our prayer. Because sometimes, without knowing it, we become attached not just to what we want, but to the belief that only that one thing can make us happy.

But we don’t know the whole story. We see only today. God sees what we cannot see. We see one door. God sees all the doors.

Sometimes a door closes and our heart breaks. We wonder why God did not give us what we prayed for. Then, months or years later, we look back and quietly say, “Thank God that door closed.”

Sometimes we get exactly what we asked for. Sometimes we get something completely different. And sometimes, what first looked like a disappointment turns out to be one of the greatest blessings of our life.

So perhaps we don’t have to choose between asking and surrendering. We can do both. We can tell God clearly what our heart wants, pray for it sincerely, and work for it honestly. But after doing everything we can, we can leave the result in His hands.

We don’t have to pretend that we have no desires. We don’t have to be afraid of asking God for something specific. We can tell Him everything, just as a child tells his mother everything that is in his heart.

But after telling Him, we can gently place our desire at His feet and say, “God, this is what I want. But You know what I need. I trust You more than I trust my own understanding.”

That is not giving up our dreams. It is simply giving God the freedom to give us something better.

Maybe that is what Dhruva teaches us. We can go to God asking for something. But if we stay close to God, something beautiful may happen. Our prayer may change. Not because God refused to listen, but because being close to Him may slowly change what we think we need.

Perhaps the deepest prayer is not, “God, please give me exactly what I want.” Nor is it, “God, I don’t want anything. You decide everything.”

Perhaps it is something much simpler: “God, this is what my heart wants. I place it before You. If it is good for me, please give it to me. If something better is waiting for me, please give me the heart to recognise it.”

And perhaps, one day, when we look back at our life, we may understand something very beautiful. Some of our greatest blessings were the things we received. But some of our greatest blessings were the things we did not receive.

Because sometimes, what we call a rejected prayer today may turn out to be a protected life tomorrow.

Thought for the day

We can ask God for what our heart wants. There is nothing wrong in having a dream, praying for it, or working towards it.

But after we have asked, perhaps we can leave a little space for God to surprise us.

We may not always get the thing we asked for. We may not understand why. But perhaps we can still trust that the One who sees the whole picture knows what we cannot see.

So today, let us pray like this: “God, this is what I want. Please give it to me if it is good for me. And if You have something better for me, give me the heart to recognise it and the courage to accept it.”

Sometimes, the greatest blessing in our life may not be getting what we wanted.

It may be looking back one day and whispering with a grateful heart, “God, now I understand why You said no.”

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 235 | Epic series 105 | The loneliness we never prepared for | 
Yesterday, one of our readers sent me a message.

“One of my very close friend’s children have settled abroad. His wife passed away recently. He is now living alone. Every day he keeps saying, ‘I am praying to God to take me away.’ I wanted to comfort him… but I had no words.

I am also scared. This can happen to any one of us. How do we face such loneliness?”

Many of us are living longer than the previous generation. Our children are building their lives in different cities and countries. They love us deeply. But they cannot always be physically present.

Then one day, the life partner with whom we shared forty or fifty years is no longer there.

The house is the same.The chair is the same.The cup of coffee is the same.But the silence has become louder than any sound.

What words can possibly comfort such a person?

I always search for answers in Bhagavatham.

The day finally came when Krishna had to leave Vrindavan for Mathura.Akrura arrived with the chariot. The Gopis could not believe what they were seeing.

They ran behind the chariot. Some stood in front of the horses. Some cried uncontrollably. They begged Krishna not to leave. To them, Krishna was not just a person. He was the very reason they smiled, sang, laughed and lived.

How could life continue after He was gone?

Krishna did not stop the journey. Some journeys in life cannot be stopped.

A child has to leave home. A daughter gets married. A son settles abroad. A loved one leaves this world.

Life keeps moving.

But before leaving, Krishna gave them something far greater than His physical presence.

He helped them discover that the deepest relationships are not carried by the eyes. They are carried by the heart.

Slowly, the Gopis stopped searching for Krishna on the streets of Vrindavan.

They started finding Him within themselves. Every tree reminded them of Him. Every song reminded them of Him. Every breath reminded them of Him.

His physical presence had gone. His inner presence had become even stronger.

Perhaps this is one of life’s hardest lessons. When someone is physically taken away from us, life quietly asks us to discover a new way of being with them.

Not through touch. Not through conversation. But through gratitude. Through memories. Through the values they left behind. Through the love that has now found a permanent home inside us.

The loneliness may not disappear. But it slowly changes its nature.

The emptiness becomes remembrance. The tears become gratitude. And the relationship continues… in a different form.

Thought for the day

Maybe the greatest tragedy is not that someone we love is no longer beside us.

The greatest tragedy is when, because they are no longer beside us, we stop living ourselves.

The people who truly loved us never wanted us to count the days until our end. They wanted us to make our remaining days meaningful.

Perhaps the best way to honour those we miss is not by waiting to join them…but by becoming the love, kindness and strength they planted in us, and passing it on to someone who needs it today.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 234 | Epic Series 104 | When does “Enough” arrive? | 
One of the greatest blessings of participating in Namasankeerthanam over the years has been the opportunity to spend time with hundreds of families.

The bhajan may last only a few hours, but the conversations afterwards often stay with me for years.

Very often, someone says with complete sincerity, “I would also love to do something like this… but let me first settle a few responsibilities.”

Another says, “After my children’s education is over.”

Someone else says, “Once their marriage is completed.”

A retired person quietly remarks, “At this age, we never know when a medical emergency may arise. It is better to be careful.”

An elderly grandfather once told me, “I don’t want my children to spend on me. Whatever happens, I want to remain financially independent.”

As I listened to these conversations over the years, I realised something beautiful. None of these words came from a lack of devotion. None of them came from selfishness.

They came from love; love for family, a deep sense of responsibility, and a genuine desire not to become a burden on others.

Before I go any further, may I share one small thought?

Today’s reflection is not about rich people or poor people. It is not about whether someone spends more or saves more. It is not even about money.

It is about a quiet fear that visits almost every human heart, the feeling that perhaps what we have today may still not be enough for tomorrow.

As I reflected on this, my mind went to a beautiful conversation from the Bhagavatam.

King Yadu once met the great Avadhuta. The king was amazed because the Avadhuta owned almost nothing, yet his face radiated extraordinary peace. Curious, he asked,,”Who is your Guru?”

The Avadhuta replied, “I have learnt from twenty-four Gurus in nature.” One of them was the honey bee.

The honey bee tirelessly flies from flower to flower, collecting tiny drops of nectar. Day after day and month after month, those tiny drops slowly become a hive filled with honey.

The bee is hardworking, patient and disciplined. There is nothing wrong with its effort. Yet, before the bees can enjoy the honey they have so carefully stored, a honey collector arrives. With a little smoke, the bees are driven away and the entire hive is taken.

Watching this, the Avadhuta understood something profound. The lesson was not that we should stop saving, nor that planning for the future is unnecessary.

The lesson was far more subtle. If our feeling of security always depends on having just a little more, then “enough” may quietly keep moving further away.

Isn’t this something many of us experience?

When we are young, we worry whether we have enough. When we grow older, we worry whether what we have will continue to be enough.

The object of our worry changes, but the worry itself often remains.

The Bhagavatam never asks us to become careless. It encourages us to work hard, save wisely and fulfil our responsibilities. But it also gently reminds us that no amount of wealth can completely remove the uncertainties of life.

Real security comes not only from what we accumulate, but also from the peace with which we live.

Perhaps every once in a while, we can pause and ask ourselves a simple question: “Am I waiting to feel completely secure before I begin doing the things that truly matter?”

Perhaps it is opening our home for a satsang….Perhaps it is spending more unhurried time with our loved ones…..Perhaps it is deepening our spiritual life….quietly helping someone in need….supporting a noble cause, or simply creating memories that our family will cherish long after we are gone.

Life has a gentle way of reminding us that tomorrow is a gift, not a promise.

The honey bee teaches us a timeless lesson. Save with wisdom. Plan with responsibility. Fulfil your duties with sincerity.

But do not let the search for complete security quietly postpone the opportunities that bring meaning, devotion and human connection into your life.

The Bhagavatam is not asking us to calculate less. It is asking us to fear less.

Perhaps “enough” is not a number that life gives us. Perhaps “enough” is a decision that wisdom helps us make.

And perhaps true wealth is not measured only by what we preserve for tomorrow, but also by what we lovingly put to its highest purpose today.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 233 | Epic Series 103 | The race that quietly steals our life · When love becomes control | 
( _I hesitated before sharing something this personal. But if this touches even one heart caught in the same race, it is worth it_)

There are some moments in life that quietly break our heart. Not because something bad happens, but because we suddenly realise what we have missed.

Last week, I visited the home of one of my very close friends. His eight-month-old granddaughter was playing on the floor. As I sat beside her, she looked straight into my eyes. Then she slowly wrapped her tiny fingers around one of mine and held it tightly.

She kept looking at me, smiled innocently, and soon burst into laughter. I don’t know why, but my heart simply melted.

For those few minutes, nothing else mattered. There was no past, no future, only the joy of being with a little child.

As we travelled back home, a painful thought entered my mind. I could hardly remember such moments with my own children. When they were babies, I was always busy building my career.

There was always another customer to meet, another deadline to achieve, another journey to make. I sincerely believed I was doing it all for my family.

Today, I have many photographs of my children’s childhood. I have videos too. But I have surprisingly few memories of simply sitting beside them, holding their tiny fingers, watching them smile, or listening to their laughter. Then another thought touched my heart. If I had missed those precious moments, someone else had not.

My wife had quietly lived them all. She was there for their first smile, their first steps, their first words, their first day at school, their little victories, their tears and their laughter. While I was busy earning for the family, she was quietly building the family.

Looking back today, I feel she received blessings that money could never buy. Some of life’s greatest treasures are not earned. They are simply experienced, one ordinary moment at a time.

As I reflected on this experience, my mind went to a deeply moving episode from the Bhagavatam.

There lived a noble king named Muchukunda. He was known for his righteousness, courage and unwavering sense of duty. When the devas were engaged in a fierce war against the asuras, they sought his help. Without a second thought, Muchukunda left his kingdom and joined the battle.

The war did not last for a few weeks or months. It continued for an unimaginably long time. Muchukunda kept fighting with complete dedication, never complaining and never asking what it was costing him personally. His only concern was to fulfil his duty.

At last, the devas won the war. Filled with gratitude, they said, “O King, ask for any boon.”

Muchukunda stood silently. His body was exhausted. His mind was exhausted. His heart was exhausted. Then he looked back at the life he had left behind.

Time had quietly moved on.

His parents were no more. His wife was no more. His children were no more. His relatives were no more. The kingdom he had once ruled had changed beyond recognition.

He had spent the finest years of his life fighting battles for others. By the time his duty was over, the people for whom life had meaning were no longer there.

He did not ask for another kingdom. He did not ask for wealth. He did not ask for fame or power.

With folded hands, he simply said, “Please… allow me to sleep.”

The devas blessed him. They granted him deep, uninterrupted sleep in a lonely mountain cave. They also gave him another boon that anyone who disturbed his sleep would instantly be reduced to ashes by his very glance.

Many years later, Krishna deliberately led the arrogant king Kalayavana into that very cave. Mistaking the sleeping king for Krishna, Kalayavana angrily kicked him.

Muchukunda slowly opened his eyes. In that very moment, Kalayavana was reduced to ashes.

As I reflected on this story, one thought would not leave me. Kalayavana did not create the fire. He merely awakened it.

Perhaps burnout is like that. Years of carrying responsibilities… years of postponing life… years of saying, “After this project… after this promotion… after I earn a little more…” slowly become a hidden fire within us.

Then one small request from our spouse, one innocent question from our child, one interruption, one small mistake and the fire bursts out.

The people closest to us are not hurt because they deserve it. They are hurt because they happen to stand closest to our unhealed exhaustion.

Muchukunda’s story is not merely about a king asking for sleep. It is the story of a good man who gave everything to his responsibilities, only to discover that time had quietly taken away moments that could never return. His greatest loss was not his kingdom. It was the years he could never live again.

As I reached home that evening, I did not feel guilty. I felt grateful. Grateful that an eight-month-old baby had taught me a lesson that I had somehow missed for many years.

Life is not made only of achievements. It is made of moments. We cannot relive the moments we have already missed. But we can still protect the ones waiting for us today.

If someone we love asks for five minutes, may we not always answer, “Later.”

Because one day…”Later” quietly becomes “too late.”

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

When love becomes control

There is a scene that quietly plays out in thousands of homes.

For nearly thirty years, a mother lovingly runs her home. She decides what to cook. She knows where every small item is kept. Festivals, family functions, guests, finances… everything passes through her hands.

Her children depend on her for almost every decision. She is not simply managing a house. She is the house. Then one day, her son gets married.

Slowly, without anyone planning it, life begins to change. The daughter-in-law starts cooking. She arranges the kitchen differently. She buys different groceries. She chooses new curtains. She has her own way of raising the children.

The son, wanting peace at home, gently says, “Amma… don’t strain yourself anymore. Please relax. We’ll take care of everything.”

He says it with love. But Amma hears something else. “Maybe they don’t need me anymore.” Nobody intended to hurt her. Yet her heart hurts. From that day, every small change begins to hurt.

“The sambar is too thin.”

“We never kept vessels there.”

“In our days, children were brought up differently.”

Slowly, conversations become corrections. Corrections become complaints. Complaints become distance.

The daughter-in-law begins to feel, “Whatever I do is never enough.”

The mother-in-law begins to feel, “Nobody respects me anymore.”

The son stands helplessly between the two women he loves the most. A home built with years of love slowly loses its peace.

As I reflected on this common family situation, my mind went to a beautiful episode from the Bhagavatam.

There was a mighty vanara named Dvivida. Long ago, during Lord Rama’s time, Dvivida was one of the great heroes.

When the bridge had to be built across the ocean to reach Lanka, he worked alongside the other vanaras. He used his great strength in the service of Dharma. He was respected and honoured for his courage.

Years passed. Lord Rama’s avatara came to an end. A new age had begun. Lord Krishna and Lord Balarama were now protecting the world.

But Dvivida could not accept that life had moved on. Instead of finding joy in the new generation, he became bitter.

He started keeping the company of wicked people. The same hands that once helped build now began to destroy. He uprooted trees. He threw huge rocks into villages.

He frightened innocent people. He disturbed the yajnas of great sages. He even interrupted happy family celebrations and marriages just to create confusion.

The hero who once brought happiness had now become the cause of fear. Finally, the suffering became too much.

Lord Balarama patiently warned him. But Dvivida would not change. At last, Balarama had no choice but to destroy him.

As I reflected on this story, one thought deeply touched my heart. Dvivida was not destroyed because he had become old. He was destroyed because he allowed bitterness to replace gratitude.

Perhaps every family teaches us the same lesson. As parents grow older, they slowly lose authority. But they never lose their importance.

The younger generation may no longer need instructions every day. But they will always need blessings. A daughter-in-law does not want another mother. She wants acceptance.

A mother-in-law does not really want control. She simply wants to know that she is still loved, respected and needed.

When both understand each other’s silent fears…a house becomes a home.

Perhaps the greatest wisdom in growing older is this:

Do not measure your place in the family by how many decisions you make. Measure it by how much peace your presence brings.

Anyone can build a house. But only a loving elder can build harmony.

May we all grow from being the centre of the family…to becoming the strength behind the family.

That is not losing our place. That is becoming the foundation on which the next generation stands.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 232 | Epic Series 102 | Who are we trying to impress? | 
It was a busy sunday morning.

“The guests will be here in half an hour!”

Immediately, the whole house sprang into action.

“Quick… fold those blankets. Put the newspapers away…..Hide those old buckets…..Bring out the new tea set…..Use the good plates. And please… keep those medicine strips inside the drawer.”

Father wiped the dining table one more time. Mother carefully arranged the sofa cushions. The children were reminded not to leave their toys in the hall. Within a few minutes, the house looked completely different.

Just then, little Rahul walked into the kitchen carrying his favourite steel tumbler. His mother smiled. “No, kanna… not today. Guests are coming. We’ll use the glass tumblers.”

Soon, the guests arrived. The house was filled with cheerful conversation. Tea was served in beautiful cups. Snacks were neatly arranged.

Looking around, the guests said, “What a lovely home! So neat! So beautifully maintained!”

Everyone smiled. After they left, life quietly returned to normal. The glass tumblers went back into the cupboard. The special plates disappeared. The old steel tumbler came back into Rahul’s hands.

As he sat drinking his milk, Rahul looked at his mother and innocently asked, “Amma… why don’t we use all these nice things every day?”

His mother laughed. “These are for guests, kanna.”

Rahul thought for a moment. Then he asked another question. “Amma… are we trying to make our guests happy… or are we trying to make them think we live like this every day?”

The room suddenly became silent.

Sometimes, children ask the simplest questions, and they quietly touch places where even adults hesitate to go.

Perhaps all of us do something similar. Not only with our homes. With our lives.

We carefully choose what others should see. We hide our worries, our failures and our fears. We wear a smile even when our heart is tired. We hesitate to ask for help because someone may think we are weak. Slowly, our image becomes more important than our reality.

The Bhagavatam tells us about King Paundraka, the ruler of a small kingdom.

He was respected by his people and ruled his kingdom well. If he had simply remained the king he truly was, history would probably have remembered him as a good ruler.

But Paundraka had one weakness. He loved praise.

Every day, his ministers stood before him and said, “Maharaja, why should Krishna alone be called Vasudeva? You are equally great. You look just like Him. You deserve the same honour.”

At first, Paundraka laughed because he knew they were exaggerating. But the praise continued every single day.

The same words…The same applause…The same admiration…Slowly, something changed inside him.

One day he no longer thought, “My ministers are praising me.”

He began to think, “They are right. If I am the real Vasudeva, I should also look like Vasudeva.”

He called the finest craftsmen in his kingdom. “Make me look exactly like Krishna.”

The craftsmen carefully carved two extra wooden arms and tied them to his shoulders. Jewellers copied Krishna’s ornaments. He carried a conch, a discus and a mace. He dressed like Krishna and stood before a mirror, admiring himself.

The ministers applauded loudly. Sadly, not one of them loved him enough to tell him the truth. Not one person said,

“Maharaja..You do not have to look like Krishna. You only have to be yourself.”

Soon, Paundraka became so convinced that he sent a royal message to Krishna. “You have been using my name and my symbols. Lay down your conch, your discus and your mace. I alone am the true Vasudeva.”

When Krishna heard the message, He did not become angry. He smiled. How could the original feel threatened by a copy?

How could truth become smaller because someone tried to imitate it. Krishna quietly rode to Paundraka’s kingdom.

The battle began. Krishna did not argue. Truth rarely needs long explanations.

As the battle unfolded, one by one, everything that Paundraka had borrowed began to disappear. The fake weapons could no longer protect him. The wooden arms broke and fell to the ground. The borrowed ornaments lost all meaning.

Within moments, the grand image he had spent years creating simply vanished.

There stood Paundraka. Not the Vasudeva he imagined himself to be. Not the person others had praised. Just Paundraka…exactly as he really was.

Perhaps that was the saddest part of the story.

He had spent so much of his life trying to become someone else that he had forgotten the joy of simply being himself.

As I reflected on this story, I couldn’t help wondering how different we really are from Paundraka.

Most of us don’t wear wooden arms. But many of us wear invisible ones.

We spend more than we can afford just to keep up an image. We hide our struggles because we don’t want relatives to worry or worse, to judge us. We pretend our family is perfect. We pretend we are always strong. We post smiling photographs while carrying silent worries that nobody knows about.

Slowly, we become tired not because life is difficult, but because pretending is.

Krishna did not remove Paundraka’s wooden arms to insult him. He removed the burden of living a borrowed life.

Paundraka’s greatest mistake was not that he wanted to become great. It was that he forgot the beauty of simply being himself.

Perhaps Krishna is asking each of us the same question today. “Are you spending your life becoming a better person… or simply becoming better at looking like one?”

Every home has a cupboard that holds the “good cups” and “good plates” for guests.

Sadly, many of us have another cupboard inside our heart. That is where we keep our real self.

Our fears. Our struggles. Our tears. Our ordinary life.

Whenever the world knocks on our door, we quickly close that cupboard and bring out the polished version of ourselves, the version that looks successful, happy and in control.

The world may admire that person. But Krishna is not waiting to meet that person.

He is waiting to meet the real you.

The day we stop performing for the world and start living honestly before Him, our heart becomes lighter. We discover a peace that applause can never give, because it comes from knowing that we no longer have to impress anyone.

We only have to be the person Krishna lovingly created us to be.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 231 | Epic Series 101 | Who is waiting for your cup of coffee? | 
Every evening, just before six, Meenakshi would make two cups of filter coffee. She would pour them into their favourite steel tumblers and place them on the small table near the window. One cup was for her. The other was for her husband. This had been their little routine for almost thirty years.

Those twenty minutes together were the happiest part of her day. They would talk about work, laugh over small incidents, discuss the children, or sometimes simply sit quietly, enjoying each other’s company.

As life became busier, that little routine slowly began to disappear. Every evening her husband would come home, smile and say, “Just five minutes… let me finish one office call.” Five minutes became twenty.

Then came a few WhatsApp messages, followed by one more email. Some evenings he picked up a spiritual book and said, “Let me finish this chapter. Then we’ll have coffee.”

Meenakshi never complained. She simply reheated the coffee. The next day, the same thing happened. Then again. Weeks quietly became months.

One evening, he came home and looked around. “Meenu… where’s my coffee?”

She smiled gently and said, “It’s in the kitchen.”

Surprised, he asked, “You didn’t bring it today?”

She looked at him with great affection and softly replied, “The coffee can always be reheated… but the person waiting to drink it with you slowly stops waiting.”

He stood there in silence. That one sentence stayed with him through the night. He realised that every day he had been giving his best attention to work, messages and even good things… while the person who loved him the most quietly adjusted, without asking for anything.

Perhaps many homes are like this.

The people who love us the most rarely ask for expensive gifts. They are not waiting for us to solve all their problems. Most of the time, they are simply waiting for us to listen, to smile, to sit with them, even for a few minutes.

The Bhagavatam tells us a beautiful story.

One day, Krishna and His cowherd friends were playing in the forests of Vrindavan. After hours of running behind the cows, laughing and playing, everyone became hungry. Nearby, a group of great Vedic scholars were performing a grand yajna.

The sacred fire was blazing. The air echoed with Vedic chants. Every ritual was being performed with perfect precision.

Krishna smiled and said to the boys, “Go and ask them for some food.”

The boys happily ran to the scholars. Folding their little hands, they said, “Krishna is nearby. We have all become hungry. Could you please give us something to eat?”

The scholars heard them. But no one looked up.

One continued chanting. Another carefully poured ghee into the sacred fire. A third turned the pages of the scriptures. The boys stood there for a few moments, hoping someone would smile and say, “Come, children. Sit down.”

Nobody did. Slowly, they turned around and walked back. Their hands were empty, and so were their hearts.

Krishna looked at them and smiled. “Now go to their wives.”

The boys ran to the homes of the scholars. The women were busy with their daily work. One was rolling chapatis. Another was stirring milk. One was serving food. Another was grinding spices.

The boys repeated the same words. “Krishna is nearby… and He is hungry.”

Everything changed.

One woman left the chapati half rolled. Another forgot the milk on the stove. Someone quickly filled a basket with rice. Another packed butter, curd, fruits and sweets.

Not one of them asked, “Has the yajna finished?” or “What will our husbands say?”

Love did not stop to calculate. Love simply ran.

Holding the baskets close to their hearts, they hurried towards Krishna. Their only fear was not that someone might stop them. Their only fear was, “What if Krishna finishes eating before we reach Him?”

When they finally reached Him, Krishna welcomed them with a warm smile and accepted their offering with great love.

Later, the scholars came to know what had happened. Only then did they realise what they had missed.

They knew every mantra. They knew every ritual. They knew every rule.

But when hungry children came asking for food in Krishna’s name, they never even looked into their eyes.

Their knowledge had filled their minds. Love had filled the hearts of their wives.

That day, the scholars understood a lesson that no book alone could teach.

Knowledge that does not make us kinder… has not yet reached the heart.

Thought for the Day

Tomorrow, someone may quietly wait for you.

Your spouse may want to share a cup of coffee. Your father may be waiting to tell you the same story once again. Your mother may simply want you to sit beside her. Your child may say, “Can you see what I made?” None of them are asking for a great sacrifice. They are simply asking for a few moments of your attention.

When that moment comes, pause before saying, “Just five minutes.”

The message on your phone can wait. The television can wait. Even tomorrow’s work can wait for a little while.

But a heart that keeps waiting… slowly learns to stop asking.

The scholars missed Krishna not because they lacked knowledge, but because they were too busy to notice someone who came asking in His name.

Perhaps Krishna still comes to us in the same way. Not always through a temple. Not always through a prayer.

Sometimes He comes through a gentle voice that says, “Will you sit with me for a few minutes?”

If we miss that moment, we may also miss Him. Before the coffee becomes cold… before the waiting becomes silence… before love quietly stops asking…

Sit down. Keep your phone aside. Look into the eyes of someone who has been waiting for you.

Those few minutes may become the most beautiful prayer you offer to Krishna today.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 230 | Epic Series 100 | When life suddenly stops going according to our Plan | 
Almost every family has a simple dream.

Not a dream of becoming rich. Just a dream that one day…Life will become a little easier.

We work. We save. We sacrifice. We postpone.

Always telling ourselves, “One day… we’ll finally have time to live.”

……We will travel.

…. We will visit temples.

…..We will spend more time together.

…..We will restart our morning walks.

…. We will learn music.

…. We will meet old friends.

After all these years of responsibility, life will finally become peaceful.

Then…Just when that day seems to be within reach…

Life quietly changes the plan. An ageing parent can no longer manage alone…..A spouse falls sick……A child faces an unexpected crisis…… A loved one suddenly becomes dependent on us.

Without any warning, our days begin to revolve around hospital visits, medicines, blood tests, doctor’s appointments and endless phone calls.

Weeks become months. Months become years. Slowly, the life we had imagined begins to fade away. We don’t complain because we love our family. But somewhere deep inside, a silent question appears. “Is this how the rest of my life is going to be?”

Many people never say these words aloud. But countless hearts quietly carry them every day.

Is there a way to accept such unexpected responsibilities… without becoming bitter?

The Mahabharata answers this question through someone whose name many people have never even heard.

Not Bhishma….Not Arjuna…..Not Krishna.

A young princess named Sukanya. One day, Sukanya was walking through the forest with her friends. It was an ordinary day filled with laughter and play.

She noticed two tiny glowing lights inside an anthill. Curious and innocent, she gently poked them with a small thorn.

In that one moment…Her entire life changed. Those glowing lights were not insects. They were the eyes of the great sage Chyavana, who had been sitting in such deep meditation that an anthill had grown over his body.

The sage was blinded. Sukanya immediately realised what had happened.

Fear….,Shock…..Guilt.

She could have run away. She could have denied it. She could have blamed someone else.

Instead, she did something very rare. She accepted responsibility.

When life placed before her a burden she had never chosen, she chose courage over escape.

She married the aged, blind sage and devoted herself to caring for him with patience, respect and quiet love.

Not because life was fair. Not because she had no choice. But because she chose to transform an unexpected responsibility into an act of loving service.

Years passed.

Pleased by her unwavering devotion, the Ashvini Kumaras restored Sage Chyavana’s youth and eyesight.

What had once seemed like the end of all her dreams quietly became the beginning of unexpected grace.

Perhaps that is why the Mahabharata remembers Sukanya.

Not because her life was easy. But because she refused to let an unexpected responsibility make her heart bitter.

Is it not this the story of many of us today?

Life may not have asked us to care for an aged sage. But it may have quietly asked us to care for an ageing parent……To stand beside a spouse who is unwell…..To support a child going through a difficult phase.

Or to shoulder responsibilities we never imagined would become ours.

We often feel these responsibilities are taking life away from us.

Perhaps they are giving us something even greater.

A heart that is becoming more patient. More compassionate. More selfless. Sometimes, God changes our plans…

Not because He wants to reduce our happiness. But because He wants to enlarge our heart.

Thought for the Day

If life has unexpectedly placed someone in your care…

An ageing parent…A spouse who is unwell…A child going through a difficult phase…

Don’t spend today asking, “Why has my life become like this?”

Instead, ask, “How can I make this person’s day a little lighter?”

Sit beside them for ten uninterrupted minutes. Listen without looking at your phone.

Serve their meal with warmth instead of treating it as another task. Hold their hand when they are afraid.

One day…

The hospital visits will end. The medicines will stop. The room that now needs your attention may become silent.

And on that day, we will not wish we had watched one more movie…Taken one more vacation…Or attended one more meeting.

We will only ask ourselves, “When God gave me the privilege of caring for someone who needed me… did I do it with love?”

The people who need us today will not remain with us forever.

Perhaps God has quietly entrusted them to us…Not to interrupt our plans…But to enlarge our heart.

Like Sukanya, we may not have chosen this responsibility. But we can always choose how we live it.

And years from now…That choice may become the most beautiful chapter of our life.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 229 | Epic Series 99 | The Hidden Gift Inside Difficult Times | 
Sri Yatiraja Mutt Jeeyar Swamiji had come to our neighbour’s flat during this Chaturmasya period. I was fortunate to have His darshan and receive His Anugraha bhashanam .

During His interaction with everyone, He lovingly encouraged us to regularly do Kunti Stuti.

His words stayed with me. Today, let us reflect on Kunti Stuti, the beautiful story behind it, and what it teaches us in our daily life.

The Mahabharata war had finally come to an end.

The Pandavas had regained their kingdom. Their long years of exile, humiliation and suffering were over. Peace had finally returned.

Lord Krishna was now preparing to leave for Dwaraka.

The Pandavas and Queen Kunti gathered to bid Him farewell. Before Krishna left, He lovingly asked Kunti if she wished to ask Him for any boon.

Anyone in her place would have prayed for a peaceful life, good health, prosperity and freedom from future troubles.

But Kunti’s prayer surprised everyone. With folded hands she prayed,

“O Krishna, please let difficulties come to us again and again. During every hardship, You were always with us. We remembered You every moment. We depended on You completely. But now that life has become comfortable, I fear we may slowly forget You.”

What an extraordinary prayer !

Kunti was not asking for suffering. She had already seen enough of it.

She had lost her husband at a young age. She had raised her children amidst constant danger. She had watched them face injustice, exile, humiliation and a devastating war. No mother would ever wish for such pain again.

Then why did she pray like this?

Because she had discovered something very precious.

Every difficulty had made her heart softer. Every challenge had reduced her ego. Every helpless moment had made her remember Krishna more deeply.

She realised that while suffering brought her closer to God, comfort quietly pushed Him to the background.

Isn’t this true in our own lives?

When everything is going well, we become busy. Prayer becomes shorter. We postpone reading. We delay spending time with our family.

We assume tomorrow will always be like today. Then suddenly something happens.

A medical report worries us…..A misunderstanding arises at home….A child faces a problem….Someone speaks harshly to us….A financial expense comes unexpectedly.

Suddenly we become more thoughtful. We pray with greater sincerity. We value our relationships more. We realise what truly matters.

The problem did not just disturb our life. It also woke us up.

Perhaps that is why difficulties often become turning points in our life. Not because they are pleasant…But because they help us see what comfort sometimes hides.

Thought for the Day

The next time something goes wrong…

Before saying, “Why is this happening to me?”

try these three simple steps.

(1) Pause before reacting.

Don’t immediately complain, blame or become angry.

(2) Ask yourself: “What is this situation trying to teach me?”

It may be teaching patience… humility… gratitude… or understanding.

(3) Do one positive thing.

Offer a short prayer. Speak gently instead of reacting. Forgive someone. Help someone. Or peacefully accept what cannot be changed.

A problem becomes a burden only if we carry it with anger.

The same problem becomes a teacher if we carry it with awareness.

Perhaps God has not sent the difficulty to punish us…Perhaps He has sent it to polish us.

Sri Gurubhyo Namaha.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 228 | Epic Series 98 | When the mind carries us away… | 
When the mind carries us away…

In the last TFD, we asked ourselves, “Am I protecting my values… or just my ego?” Sometimes our ego stops us from taking the first step towards someone we love. But there is another kind of struggle.

Sometimes nobody has moved away from us. Our own mind has carried us away from what is actually happening.

Imagine a husband waiting for his wife to come home. She usually reaches home by 7. It is 7:30. No call. He calls her. No answer. At first, it is only a small worry. “Maybe she is stuck in traffic.”

But then the mind starts asking questions. “Why hasn’t she called? Her phone is always on. What if something has happened?”

Another ten minutes pass. He calls again. Nothing. His heart starts beating faster. He gets up and walks around the room. He checks his phone every few seconds.

His breathing becomes shallow. His chest feels tight. His hands begin to tremble. He tells himself, “Calm down. Nothing has happened.” But the mind asks back, “How do you know?”

That is enough. Now the mind starts showing him an accident. Then a hospital. Then doctors. Then all the terrible things that could happen. He knows he is thinking too much. But he cannot stop.

And then, at 8:05, the door opens. His wife walks in. She is perfectly fine. Her phone battery had died. He looks at her. There is relief. There is irritation. There is embarrassment. And perhaps even tears.

Because for those thirty-five minutes, his body was sitting in his living room, but his mind had taken him somewhere terrible.

Haven’t we all experienced some version of this? A delayed phone call. A medical report. A short message from someone we love. A problem at work. A child who doesn’t answer the phone.

Something happens. The mind fills in the blanks. And soon, we are suffering from something that has not happened.

This reminded me of the story of Trinavarta in the Bhagavatam.

One day, Mother Yashoda was holding little Krishna when Trinavarta appeared as a terrible whirlwind. Dust filled the village. Nobody could see clearly. The storm became so violent that Trinavarta lifted Krishna high into the sky. Yashoda could not see her child. She could not reach Him. She could not control the storm.

But then something extraordinary happened.

As Trinavarta carried Krishna higher, Krishna became heavier and heavier. The demon could no longer carry Him. He struggled for breath. His strength disappeared. And finally, the whirlwind collapsed. Krishna was safe.

What does it mean for us to become “heavy” when our mind becomes a whirlwind? It does not mean becoming serious. It does not mean suppressing our feelings. It does not mean telling ourselves, “I should not be afraid.”

It means stopping the mind from running away with us.

So the next time panic starts rising, let us do something very simple. First, stop what we are doing and sit down. Put both feet firmly on the floor. Don’t walk around trying to solve everything.

Then breathe slowly. Take a gentle breath in and let the breath out slowly. Do this several times. We don’t have to force a very deep breath. Just make the breathing slow and steady.

Then look around. Say to yourself quietly, “I am sitting in my room. My feet are on the floor. I am safe at this moment.” Look at the chair. Look at the wall. Look at the window. Hold something in your hand. Feel the floor under your feet.

This brings the mind back from the frightening world it has created to the world that is actually around us.

Then separate facts from fears. Ask, “What has actually happened?” Not “What could happen?” Not “What if?” Only, “What do I know right now?”

In our example, the husband knew only this: “My wife has not reached home and I cannot contact her.” That is the fact. “She has met with an accident” is a fear.

So he should act on the fact, not on the fear. Call again. Check with someone if needed. Take the next sensible step. But don’t sit there and mentally live through an accident that may not have happened.

That is what making the mind “heavy” means.

Bring the mind back to the body. Bring it back to the room. Bring it back to the facts. Bring it back to the next small action.

And if there is nothing more to do right now, say, “I have done what I can. The rest I will deal with when it comes.” Then leave it.

This is very different from saying, “Don’t worry.” We are not fighting the fear. We are refusing to feed it.

The mind may say, “What if something terrible happens?” We don’t need to answer that question. We can simply say, “If it happens, I will deal with it then. Right now, it has not happened.”

That is becoming heavy.

The whirlwind may still be blowing. But it is no longer carrying us away.

Thought for the Day

The next time my mind starts running faster than life itself, I want to remember Trinavarta.

Stop. Sit. Breathe slowly. Feel the feet. Look around. Ask what is actually true. Do only the next thing that needs to be done.

Not tomorrow’s problem. Not ten imaginary problems. Just this moment. Just this fact. Just this next step.

Perhaps that is how we become heavy enough to stay rooted. And perhaps the most comforting thought is this:

I don’t have to solve my whole life when I am frightened. I only have to come back to this moment.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 227 | Epic Series 97 | Am I protecting my values… Or Just my ego? | 
In the last few TFDs, we reflected on how our hidden desires, old habits, assumptions and emotions quietly shape our life.

Maharaja Khatvanga then reminded us that it is never too late to begin changing. As I reflected on these beautiful lessons, another question came to my mind.

What if I sincerely want to do the right thing… but somewhere along the way, I become so attached to my own principles that I slowly start hurting the very people I love?

I think many of us have experienced this.

A small misunderstanding happens at home. Maybe the husband says something in anger. The wife replies sharply. Both feel hurt.

That night, dinner is quiet.

The next morning, life goes on. Lunch boxes are packed. Children go to school. Office calls continue. Groceries are bought. Bills are paid.

Everything looks normal. But something beautiful has quietly disappeared.

The easy smile.The casual conversation. The warmth. Deep inside, both want things to become normal again.

But another voice quietly begins to speak. “Why should I be the first to say sorry? I didn’t make the mistake. If I give in now, it will become a habit. It is a matter of principle.”

The same thing happens between a father and son.

Or two brothers. Or two sisters. Or close friends.

Both remember the happy days. Both miss each other. Both hope the other person will take the first step.

A birthday passes. Then Deepavali. Then another birthday. The phone remains silent. Months become years.

One day, we don’t even remember what the original argument was about. But we remember the distance.

As I reflected on this, I remembered the life of Bhishma in the Mahabharata.

As a young prince, he was known as Devavrata. He loved his father deeply. When his father wished to marry Satyavati, her father placed one condition. Only the son born to Satyavati should become the future king.

Devavrata could have argued. After all, the throne rightfully belonged to him. Instead, out of love for his father, he gave up his claim to the kingdom.

Then another thought came to him. “What if one day my own children claim the throne?”

To remove even that possibility, he took a vow that he would never marry and never have children.

It was such an extraordinary sacrifice that the world remembered him forever as Bhishma.

Years passed.

That same vow which was once an expression of love slowly became a bond he could not step out of.

When Draupadi was humiliated, he suffered……When the Kurukshetra war came, he suffered again.

More than anyone else, Bhishma knew the pain of being unable to do what his heart wanted to do.

Then a thought touched me.

Most of us will never take a vow like Bhishma. But perhaps we have all taken smaller ones.

“I won’t call first.”

“If they really care, let them come.”

“After what they said, I am done.”

We never say these words aloud. Sometimes we don’t even realise we have made these promises to ourselves.

But slowly, they begin to decide our relationships.

The phone is in our hand…but we don’t dial.

The message is typed…but we don’t send it.

Not because we don’t care. But because we have become loyal to a decision we made when we were hurt.

Days pass. The hurt becomes smaller. The silence becomes bigger. Then one day, I realised something that pierced my heart.

Perhaps I was not protecting my principle. I was protecting the image I had created of myself.

The relationship was waiting. It was my pride that was waiting even longer.

Thought for the Day

The next time I tell myself, “It is a matter of principle…”

I want to pause for a moment.

Not to ask, “Who is right?”

But to ask, “What does this relationship need from me right now?”

If one phone call…one gentle message…one sincere “I’m sorry”…or simply, “Shall we talk?”

can bring life back into a relationship…perhaps taking the first step is not losing.

Perhaps it is the quiet courage to reach out first, instead of waiting for the other person.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 226 | Epic Series 96 | How long will I keep saying… “One Day”? | 
Guru Purnima greetings to all.

Today we bow with gratitude to the Guru, not merely because he teaches us something new, but because he helps us see what has always been true. A Guru does not simply fill our mind with knowledge. He awakens us from the illusion that there is always more time.

In the last few TFDs, we reflected on how life slowly reveals what is hidden inside us. Saubhari Muni showed us our hidden desires. Kamsa showed us our old patterns. Sudama showed us the stories we quietly tell ourselves. Virabhadra reminded us to pause before our emotions begin speaking for us.

Even after understanding all this… why do I still postpone changing?

I think many of us have said these words at some point in our lives.

“Once this project is over…”

“Once the children settle down…”

“Once I retire…”

“Once my health becomes better…”

“Then I will spend more time with Bhagavan.”

“Then I will read the Bhagavatam.”

“Then I will do more seva.”

We genuinely mean every word.

But life has a way of giving us one more responsibility… and then another. Before we realise it, months become years, and “one day” quietly becomes “someday.”

Let us listen to the story from Bhagavatam about Maharaja Khatvanga.

Khatvanga was a great king and a fearless warrior. When the devas were struggling in a fierce battle, they sought his help. He fought bravely on their side and helped them win.

Filled with gratitude, the devas said, “You have done so much for us. Please ask for any boon. We will gladly grant it.”

Most of us know what we might have asked for.

A longer life.

Better health.

More wealth.

Success for our family.

Freedom from problems.

But Khatvanga asked only one question. “How much longer do I have to live?”

The devas replied, “Only one muhurta… about forty-eight minutes.”

Imagine hearing those words.I don’t know what I would have done.

Perhaps I would have felt shocked. Perhaps I would have thought, “Only forty-eight minutes? There is still so much left undone. I wish I had lived differently.”

But the Bhagavatam tells us something extraordinary.

Khatvanga did not spend even one minute regretting the years that had gone by. He did not ask for more time. He did not blame anyone. He did not waste those precious moments thinking about yesterday.

Instead, his mind became completely clear.

The kingdom could wait…. His wealth could stay behind…..His titles no longer mattered.

Only one thing mattered. He turned his mind completely towards Bhagavan. With total sincerity and complete focus, he used the little time that remained.

In that one muhurta, he attained the highest goal of life.

Reflecting on this story, one thought touched my heart.

Perhaps my biggest problem is not that I don’t have enough time.

Perhaps my biggest problem is that I keep believing I will have more time later.

That one thought changes everything. Every day, I postpone something that I know is important.

A prayer…..A few minutes of Nama…..Reading one page of the Bhagavatam…Meeting an old friend…..Doing one small act of kindness.

I tell myself, “I will do it tomorrow.”

But tomorrow quietly keeps moving away.

Thought for the Day

On this sacred Guru Purnima, perhaps the greatest offering we can make to our Guru is not another promise that we will begin someday.

It is to begin today. We cannot begin yesterday. We may not get tomorrow.

But today is completely in our hands.

One page…One prayer…One act of seva…One heartfelt chant of His Name.

Because perhaps…Today is the “one day” we have been waiting for.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 225 | Epic Series 95 | When Does My Anger Stop Being Under My Control? | 
In the last few TFDs, we reflected on how life reveals what is already hidden inside us. Saubhari Muni discovered a hidden desire. Kamsa revealed an old pattern. Sudama showed us how easily we can believe the stories our own mind creates. As I reflected on these beautiful lessons, another question came to my mind.

If my desires can quietly take over me… if my old habits can quietly take over me… can my anger also quietly take over me without me even realizing it?

I think every one of us has experienced this.

Someone says something that hurts us deeply. It may be at home, at work or even over the phone. We don’t react immediately. But the conversation keeps playing in our mind.

We remember every word they spoke. We think of all the things we should have said. Even while eating, driving or trying to sleep, the same incident keeps coming back. Slowly, the hurt becomes anger.

Then another voice quietly starts speaking inside us. “I can’t leave it like this. They should know how much they hurt me. I have to give them a fitting reply.” At that moment, it feels as though we are making a wise decision. But are we really?

The Bhagavatam made me think deeply about this through the story of Daksha’s sacrifice.

Daksha repeatedly insulted Lord Shiva. Sati could not bear to see her husband humiliated again and again. Unable to bear the pain, she gave up her life in the sacrificial fire. When the news reached Lord Shiva, His grief was beyond words.

The Puranas then describe a powerful scene. Lord Shiva pulled out a lock of His matted hair and threw it to the ground. From it arose the mighty Virabhadra. He entered Daksha’s sacrificial arena like a storm. The sacrifice was destroyed. Daksha was beheaded. What began as an insult ended in widespread destruction.

As I reflected on this story, one thought stayed with me. The Puranas are certainly not telling us that Lord Shiva lost control. Through this powerful picture of Virabhadra, they are gently holding up a mirror to our own mind.

Virabhadra made me think of the many forms my own anger takes. A harsh WhatsApp message. A painful email. Words spoken in the heat of the moment. A decision taken only to hurt someone back.

As long as my anger remains inside my mind, I still have a choice. I can pause. I can remain silent. I can wait. But the moment I give my anger a voice through my words or actions, it begins creating consequences that I can no longer control.

One angry sentence leads to another. One argument pulls in the whole family. People who had nothing to do with the problem also get hurt. Long after my anger has disappeared, the wounds created by my words may still remain.

Then I realised something very simple.

When anger is only a thought, I can still change it.

The moment it becomes words, it begins changing the world around me. And I may not like what it changes.

Thought for the Day

The next time anger tells me, “Reply now.”

I want to pause for a moment and ask myself, “Will these words reduce my pain… or will they simply spread it?”

If the answer is, “They will only spread it,” perhaps silence for a little while is not weakness.

It is wisdom.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 224 | Epic Series 94 | Why did I stop meeting people? | 
One day, while scrolling through our contacts, an old friend’s name appears. Our face lights up. “It’s been so long… I should really call him.” Our finger even moves towards the Call button.

Then, almost instantly, another conversation begins inside our mind. “His life has gone so well… Mine has not. What will I talk about? It has been so many years. It will be awkward now. I will call him another day.” We quietly lock the phone.

A few days later, there is a family function. We genuinely want to go. But the same inner voice returns. “Everyone has done well in life. They will ask about me. They will compare me with others. What will they think when they see me now?” So we politely make an excuse and stay back.

Looking back, it feels strange. Nobody told us not to come. Nobody rejected us. Nobody spoke a harsh word. Yet somewhere along life’s journey, we quietly built a wall around ourselves… and then convinced ourselves that everyone else had built it.

In the last two TFDs, we reflected on how life reveals what is already hidden inside us. Saubhari Muni discovered a hidden desire. Kamsa revealed an old pattern of fear. As I reflected on those stories, another question touched my heart.

If life can reveal my hidden desires and hidden fears, can it also reveal the way I quietly see myself?

Here is the story of Sudama which gave me some insight.

Sudama and Krishna had studied together in the same Gurukulam. They learnt together, laughed together and shared everything like brothers. But life took them in very different directions. Krishna became the King of Dwaraka, while Sudama remained a poor Brahmin. There were days when his family hardly had enough food to eat.

One day, Sudama’s wife gently said, “Why don’t you go and meet Krishna?” Sudama loved Krishna with all his heart. Yet he hesitated. Not because he doubted Krishna’s love, but because he doubted himself.

“How can I go like this? Look at my clothes. What can I possibly offer the King of Dwaraka?”

After searching the whole house, they found only a few handfuls of beaten rice. His wife tied them in an old piece of cloth and placed them lovingly in his hands.

As Sudama walked towards Dwaraka, I often wonder what was happening inside his heart.

“Will Krishna remember me?”

“Will the guards even allow me inside?”

“Will people laugh when they see this tiny gift?”

With every step towards the palace, perhaps Sudama felt smaller… while Krishna’s love remained exactly the same.

Then came one of the most beautiful moments in the Bhagavatam.

The moment Krishna saw Sudama, He ran towards him. He embraced him with great love, seated him beside Himself and washed his dusty feet with His own hands.

Sudama quietly tried to hide the small bundle of beaten rice because he felt ashamed. But Krishna lovingly took it, opened the old cloth and ate the beaten rice with such joy, as though it were the grandest feast in the world.

Sudama never asked for help. Krishna never spoke about Sudama’s poverty. Love needed no explanation.

When Sudama returned home, his little hut had become a beautiful palace. Krishna had understood everything… even before Sudama asked.

As I reflected on this story, one thought pierced my heart.

Sudama’s poverty never stood between him and Krishna. Only Sudama’s opinion about himself did.

Then another question quietly arose within me.

How many beautiful relationships have I slowly walked away from… not because people stopped loving me… but because I had already decided what they would think of me?

Without asking them…Without meeting them…Without giving them a chance to love me.

Perhaps many relationships do not become distant because love disappears.

They become distant because assumptions quietly replace conversations.

Thought for the Day

Today I learnt something very simple.

Whenever I think, “They won’t value me… They must have forgotten me… They will judge me…” I want to stop and ask myself,

“Did they really say this… or did I say it on their behalf?”

Many times, I may realise that I never heard their voice.

I only heard my own fears… speaking in their name.

That one question can change a relationship. The next time I hesitate to call an old friend, attend a family function or reconnect with someone after many years, I want to remember Sudama.

Krishna never changed. Only Sudama’s picture of Krishna had changed.

Perhaps the people I am afraid to meet have not changed as much as the story I have created about them.

One phone call…One visit….One heartfelt conversation.

Sometimes, that is all it takes to discover that love was waiting on the other side… all along.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 223 | Epic Series 93 | Why do I keep reacting the same way… again and again? | 
In yesterday’s TFD, we reflected on how our hidden desires come out when life tests us. The fish did not create the desire in Saubhari Muni. They simply revealed what was already inside him.

After writing that TFD, I found myself thinking about something else.

If our hidden desires can remain inside us for years… can our hidden reactions also remain hidden inside us?

I am sure many of us have experienced this.

An argument happens at home. After it is over, we feel bad. We tell ourselves, “Next time I will stay calm. I will listen patiently. I won’t react like this again.” We genuinely mean it.

A few peaceful days go by. Then another disagreement comes. Within a few minutes, the same words come out of our mouth… the same emotions take over… and the same hurt is created.

Later, when everything becomes quiet, we sit alone and wonder, “Why does this keep happening? I really wanted to change.”

The people may be different. The situations may be different. Sometimes even many years have gone by.

Yet somehow, my response remains the same.

The Bhagavatam has a beautiful answer through the story of Kalanemi.

Kalanemi spent his life opposing Lord Vishnu. His mind was filled with fear, hatred and the constant desire to fight the Lord. In the end, Lord Vishnu destroyed him.

But the Bhagavatam tells us that in his next birth, he was born as Kamsa.

Everything on the outside had changed. He had a different body, a different name, a different family and a different kingdom.

But one thing had not changed…..His way of thinking.

One day, Kamsa heard a prophecy. “Devaki’s eighth child will be the cause of your death.”

They were only a few words. But those words completely took over his mind. Instead of trusting God, he trusted his fear…..Instead of accepting what he could not control, he tried to control everything around him.

He imprisoned his own sister. He killed her innocent children. He began to suspect everyone.

The prophecy did not create those reactions. It simply revealed what was already inside him.

Reflecting on this story.

How many times do I do exactly the same thing?

Someone disagrees with me… I become defensive.

Someone points out my mistake… my ego gets hurt.

Someone forgets to appreciate me… I immediately feel ignored.

The people are different. The situations are different. But my reactions are often the same.

Then one thought suddenly gave me a lot of peace.

Maybe I don’t have to ask, “Why is this person always like this?”

Maybe I need to ask, “Is this really about the other person… or about me?”

That one question changes everything. Instead of looking at the other person, I begin to look at my own mind.

Instead of blaming the situation, I begin to understand the pattern inside me.

Then every difficult person becomes a teacher. Every disagreement becomes an opportunity. Every argument becomes a mirror.

The next time I feel like reacting, perhaps I don’t need to speak immediately.

Perhaps I only need to pause for a few seconds and ask, “If I react in the same way again… what inside me will remain the same?”

That one pause may not change the other person. But it will slowly change me. And every time I choose a better response, my old pattern becomes a little weaker.

One day, without even noticing it, I won’t just be handling the situation differently…I will have become a different person.

Thought for the Day

We cannot stop life from testing us. But every test shows us something about ourselves.

The day we stop asking, “How do I change this person?”

and begin asking, “Is this really about the other person… or about me?”

…our transformation quietly begins.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 222 | Epic Series 92 | Have I really changed …or have I simply never been tested ? | 
In yesterday’s TFD, we saw how God often answers our prayers through people and situations that we may not expect.

But even after receiving God’s guidance, one challenge still remains.

Our own mind.

Many times, we honestly feel that we have changed. We think we have overcome our anger, our desires or our old habits. But have we really changed… or have we simply never been tested?

Let us listen to the story of Saubhari Muni narrated in Bhagavatam.

Saubhari Muni wanted nothing from the world. He wanted only God. He knew that the mind gets distracted by what it sees and hears, so he searched for a place where nothing could disturb his meditation.

He chose a place that nobody would even think of.

The bottom of a river.

There were no people… no conversations… no celebrations… no attractions. Just silence. Years passed, and his meditation became deeper and deeper. Anyone who saw him would have thought, “Here is a sage who has completely conquered his mind.”

Then one day, he slowly opened his eyes. Right in front of him, he saw two fish swimming happily together. A little later, he noticed their tiny baby fish swimming around them.

It was such an ordinary sight. The fish were simply living their life. But something happened inside the sage.

A quiet thought entered his mind. “They look so happy. I have spent so many years alone. I have never experienced family life.”

He immediately pushed the thought away and closed his eyes. But now the mind had changed. The same thought returned again and again. What began as a passing thought slowly became a strong desire.

Finally, he got up from the river and went to King Mandhata. “I wish to marry one of your daughters,” he said.

The king was puzzled. “Which of my daughters would agree to marry an old sage?” So he told Saubhari Muni, “Go and meet them. If any one of them chooses you, I have no objection.”

Using his yogic powers, Saubhari Muni transformed himself into a handsome young prince. The moment the princesses saw him, something unexpected happened. Every one of the fifty princesses wanted to marry him.

Not wanting to disappoint any of them, he married all fifty. But one desire soon led to another.

Now each wife wanted him to spend all his time with her. So he built fifty beautiful palaces. Then, using his yogic powers, he took fifty different forms and lived in all the palaces at the same time, so that every wife felt, “He is always with me.”

Years passed.

One day, Saubhari Muni quietly looked back at his own life.

A painful truth suddenly became clear. “The fish did not create this desire. The world did not change me. The desire was already inside me. It was simply waiting for the right moment to come out.”

Reflecting on this this story,

I asked myself….

How many times have I thought I had changed, when all I had really done was stay away from the situation?

How many times have I said, “I have overcome my anger,” when I simply had not met a difficult person?

How many times have I said, “I have given up sweets,” when they simply were not in front of me?

How many times have I said, “I have become detached,” when I had simply stayed away from temptation?

Perhaps staying away from temptation is a wise beginning. But it is not the destination.

Our mind can never remain empty. If we take away one desire without giving it something better, it quietly finds another desire to hold on to.

That is why our elders constantly encourage us to spend time in Nama, satsang and seva. Slowly, the mind begins to enjoy them.

As that joy grows, many worldly pleasures begin to lose their attraction. The world has not changed. The temptations are still there. But the mind has found something more beautiful.

Just as a child happily drops a small toy after receiving something much better, the mind also slowly lets go of lower pleasures when it discovers a higher joy.

That is real transformation.

Thought for the Day

Avoiding temptation can protect us for some time.

Finding a higher purpose can change us for a lifetime.

When the mind is filled with the joy of God, there is very little room left for the attractions of the world.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏
