@@ 221 | Epic Series 91 | Am I Waiting for God’s Answer… Or Missing It? | 
I was talking to an elderly gentleman about life, choices etc. . During our conversation, he said something which drew my attention.

He said, “When I look back at my life, I don’t think my biggest mistakes happened because nobody guided me. They happened because I didn’t listen. God sent many people to guide me at the right time. Some advised me. Some warned me. Some corrected me. But I was looking at the person… not at the message.”

After a small pause, he smiled and said, “I now feel God never stopped answering my prayers. I simply didn’t like the people He chose to answer them through.”

The more I thought about them, the more I felt they were true for many of us.

Many of us have prayed like this: “Bhagavan… I don’t know what to do. Please show me the way.” Then we wait. We expect the answer to come through a respected Swamiji, our Guru, an elderly person in the family, a close friend or maybe through a miracle. Without even knowing it, we have already decided how God should answer our prayer.

The Mahabharata tells us a beautiful story that teaches this lesson.

After many years of deep penance, Lord Vishnu appeared before Sage Utanka and offered him a boon. The sage asked for only one thing. “Whenever I become thirsty while travelling in the desert, please make sure I always get water.” The Lord smiled and gladly granted his wish.

Some time later, Utanka was crossing a vast desert. The sun was blazing. The hot sand burnt his feet. There was not a tree in sight. His throat had become dry. Every step was becoming more difficult. He looked all around him. There was no water anywhere. He silently remembered the boon that Lord Vishnu had given him.

Just then, a hunter appeared. His clothes were dirty. A few hunting dogs followed him. Across his shoulder hung an old leather bag filled with water. Looking at the tired sage with kindness, he said, “Maharishi, you look very thirsty. Please drink some water.”

Utanka looked at the hunter. Then he looked at the leather bag.

His mind had already made its decision.

“How can I accept water from a hunter carrying it in a leather bag?”

He politely refused. The hunter requested him again. Utanka refused again.

The hunter pleaded with him one last time. Utanka still refused.

The hunter smiled gently… and disappeared.

A little later, Lord Vishnu appeared before the sage.

Utanka said, “Prabhu, You promised that I would get water whenever I was thirsty. But nobody came.”

The Lord smiled and said, “Utanka, I kept My promise. The hunter who came to you was Indra himself. The water he offered was not ordinary water. It was Amrita, the nectar of immortality. You rejected My gift because you judged the person who brought it.”

What a beautiful lesson for all of us.

Many times, we pray for God’s guidance. But we also decide how that guidance should come. So when the answer comes through someone younger than us… someone we don’t agree with… a criticism that hurts us… a delay that disappoints us… or even a closed door… we don’t even stop to think that God may be speaking to us.

Before we reject an advice… before we become upset by a criticism… before we complain about a delay… let us pause for just a moment and ask ourselves one simple question.

“Could this be God’s answer… even though it is not the answer I was expecting?”

That one question can change the way we look at people. It can change the way we look at problems. And sometimes… it can help us recognise God’s hand in places where we never thought of looking.

Thought for the Day

The next time we pray for guidance, let us not only look up towards God.

Let us also look around.

Because sometimes…we are not waiting for God’s answer.

We are simply missing it.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 220 | Epic Series 90 | Am I simply filling my days… or am I changing myself? | 
Yesterday, through the life of King Yayati, we reflected on a simple question. “How much is enough?”

The day our heart sincerely answers that question, another question quietly follows. “Now that I have enough… what should I do with the rest of my life?”

Many of us think we already know the answer. “When I retire, I will finally have time.”

During our working years, life is genuinely busy. Office work, deadlines, family responsibilities, children’s education, ageing parents and financial commitments leave very little time for ourselves. We keep telling ourselves, “One day, when all this is over, I will spend more time with God.”

Then retirement arrives. The office disappears. But something surprising happens.

Our days become full once again.

Morning walks, slokam classes, poojas, discourses, bhajans, temple visits, pilgrimages, WhatsApp messages, YouTube videos, family gatherings, doctor appointments…

Every day is occupied.

There is nothing wrong with any of these. In fact, many of them are noble and spiritually uplifting.

Some even continue watching the stock market every day, trying to grow money that is already more than enough for the rest of their lives. If you gently ask them why, many smile and honestly say, “Otherwise… what else will I do?”

That simple answer stayed with me. Perhaps the problem was never a lack of time.

Perhaps the mind has never learnt how to simply be with itself. It only knows how to keep itself occupied.

This is where Narada Maharishi gently awakens us through the beautiful story of King Puranjana in the Bhagavatam (Puranjana Upaakyaanam)

Puranjana spent many years searching for a place where he could live happily. One day he found a magnificent city. It was beautiful, safe and filled with everything he could desire. There he met a graceful princess. They married, built a family and settled into a comfortable life.

His kingdom prospered. His family grew. His responsibilities increased. Every day there was something to do, someone to meet, a problem to solve or a joy to celebrate.

Life was full. Years quietly became decades.

Then, as it comes to every one of us, old age entered his life. His body slowly became weak. The pleasures that once excited him no longer satisfied him. One day, Time knocked on his door.

Only then did Puranjana realise something that pierced his heart. He had spent his whole life taking care of his kingdom…

But had hardly spent any time taking care of the person living inside that kingdom.

Narada’s story is not really about an ancient king.

It is about us.

Every day I take care of my body, my family, my home, my finances and my responsibilities.

But do I spend even a few quiet minutes taking care of my own mind?

Not by keeping it busy…But by observing it…Correcting it…Purifying it…And gently offering it to God.

Perhaps that is the real purpose of retirement. Not to fill our calendar with different activities…

But to finally begin the one work we postponed all our life… The work of transforming ourselves.

Thought for the Day

All our possessions, achievements and even our relationships are precious gifts from God. They remain behind when we leave this world.

What goes with us is the transformed mind we offer back to the Giver.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 219 | Epic Series 89 | How much is enough? | 
A few days ago, someone asked me, “What are you doing these days?”

I smiled and said, “I spend most of my time doing Namasankeerthanam.”

He laughed and said, “No… I’m asking seriously. What do you actually do?”

I smiled again and gave him the same answer.

He looked at me for a few moments and quietly said, “You are lucky.”

His words stayed with me for a long time.

Am I really lucky? Or have I simply reached a stage in life where I have started asking myself a different question…How much is enough?

When we are young, we run to complete our studies. Then we run for a good job. After that comes a better salary, a bigger house, our children’s future, more savings, and better investments. There is nothing wrong with any of these. They are all part of life.

But somewhere along the way, “need” quietly becomes “just one more.” And before we realise it, we are running again.

Will this “just one more” ever end?

The Bhagavatam answers this beautifully through the life of King Yayati.

King Yayati was a great emperor. He had wealth, power, comfort and everything that most people dream of. But because of a curse, he suddenly became old while he still longed to enjoy the pleasures of youth.

Unable to accept old age, he went to his sons with an unusual request. “Will one of you give me your youth and take my old age?” Every son respectfully declined except his youngest son, Puru. Out of love for his father, Puru gladly agreed.

Young once again, Yayati thought all his unfulfilled desires would finally come to an end. He enjoyed every pleasure he wished for. Days became years. Years became centuries.

But something unexpected happened. His desires did not become smaller. They became even stronger.

That was the turning point in his life.

He realised that desire cannot be satisfied by fulfilling it. Just as pouring ghee into a fire only makes it burn more brightly, feeding our desires only creates new desires.

Yayati immediately returned his son’s youth, accepted his old age with gratitude, and turned his mind towards God.

When I reflected on this story, I realised that King Yayati’s struggle is not just the story of an ancient king.

It is my story. Perhaps it is our story. How often have I told myself, “Just one more…”

Just one more achievement….,Just one more purchase…,,Just one more milestone…,,The finish line quietly keeps moving.

Maybe peace does not begin when we finally have everything we want.

Maybe peace begins when the heart quietly says, “What God has already given me is enough.”

Thought for the Day

Life does not become lighter because we own more.

Life becomes lighter when the burden of “just one more” quietly leaves our heart.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 218 | Epic Series 88 | I only wanted to help… so why did they get hurt? | 
In many homes, we see elderly parents living by themselves.

Their children love them deeply. They call often. They make sure their parents have enough money, medicines, and everything they need.

Yet, many parents quietly feel lonely. Not because their children don’t love them. But because they miss their time… their presence… and their companionship.

It made me think.

How often we do something with love… but leave behind a wounded heart.

A father works long hours because he wants to give his family a better life. Years later, his children don’t remember the sacrifices. They only remember that their father was never at home.

A mother keeps correcting her daughter because she wants the very best for her. Slowly, the daughter stops sharing her heart. She feels she is never good enough.

A husband keeps giving advice because he genuinely wants to help. But his wife feels he is always finding fault with her.

A friend speaks the truth very honestly because he wants to help. But the other person goes home carrying hurt instead of hope.

As I reflected on these situations, one question came to my mind.

How can good intentions sometimes leave behind wounded hearts?

Let me share a beautiful story from the Bhagavatam.

King Bhagiratha performed severe penance to bring the sacred river Ganga from heaven to earth. He did not do it for himself. He wanted to liberate his ancestors and bless the entire world. His prayer was pure. His intention was filled with compassion.

At last, Ganga descended from heaven. Her waters rushed across the earth with tremendous force. Wherever she flowed, she brought life, purity, and hope.

But on her way, something unexpected happened.

Her powerful waters entered the place where Sage Jahnu was performing a sacred yagna. In a few moments, everything he had carefully prepared was washed away.

Ganga never meant to disturb him. She never wanted to insult him. She was simply flowing towards her noble purpose. Yet, someone was deeply hurt because of her.

Jahnu Muni became angry. With his great yogic power, he swallowed the entire river in a single gulp. Suddenly, the mighty Ganga disappeared.

The Devas were shocked. They came to Jahnu and pleaded with him. They explained why Ganga had come to earth and how many lives depended on her. Jahnu’s anger slowly melted. He released Ganga through his ear. From that day, Ganga came to be lovingly known as Jahnavi, the daughter of Jahnu.

When I reflect on this story, I realised the story was not really about Ganga. It was about us.

How often have I said,

“But I didn’t mean it that way.”

“My intention was good.”

Perhaps it was. But did the other person feel respected?… Did they feel understood?… Did they feel valued?… Did they feel loved?

That day, I learnt a lesson I don’t want to forget.

My intention belongs to me. My impact belongs to the other person.

A loving heart is beautiful. But a heart that makes others feel loved is even more beautiful.

Thought for the Day

Good intentions are only the beginning.

Love becomes complete only when the other person can feel it.

That is where real love begins.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 217 | Epic Series 87 | Why does every blessing become a new worry? | 
This morning, our HariNamadwar team travelled about two hours from Bangalore to sing bhajans at a senior citizens’ home.

We went there to sing. But God had planned to teach me something.

As we sang the Lord’s Name, the whole place became full of joy. Many of the elderly people were smiling, clapping and gently dancing from their seats. Their bodies had become old, but their love for God had not.

After the bhajan, one elderly gentleman slowly came to me. His eyes were full of tears. He held both my hands and softly said, “Thank you… You have made my day.”

I asked him, “Sir, how old are you?”

He smiled and said, “Ninety-three.” He told me that he had retired many years ago from a good government job.

On the way back home, I could not forget his face.

It was full of peace.

I kept thinking…Ninety-three years is a long life. There must have been a time when he worried about getting a job… doing well at work… earning enough… buying a house… raising children… their studies… their marriage… his health… and his retirement.

Every new blessing brought a new worry.

But today, none of those things brought tears to his eyes.

The Lord’s Name still did.

That made me ask myself…

If one day all the things I worry about today will become so small, why am I losing my peace over them today?

That question reminded me of a beautiful story from the Bhagavatam.

King Chitraketu had almost everything. He had wealth. He had power. He had respect. But he had no child.

That one pain never left his heart. After many years, Sage Angira blessed him with a son. Before leaving, he said, “This child will bring you great joy… and great sorrow.”

The king heard only the first part. When the baby was born, the whole kingdom celebrated. The king must have felt, “Now my life is complete.”

But one day, out of jealousy, the child was poisoned. The king held his little son and cried.

His wealth could not help him. His power could not help him. Nothing could take away his pain.

Seeing his grief, Sage Angira came back with Sage Narada. By their grace, the child’s soul returned for a few moments.

Everyone thought the child would run to his father.

Instead, the child quietly asked, “Who is my father?”

The whole palace became silent. Then the child said,

“In my many births, I have had many fathers, many mothers and many families. We meet for a short time, like travellers resting in the same place. Then each of us goes the way God has planned.”

After saying this, the soul left again.

As I thought about this story, I remembered the tears of the ninety-three-year-old gentleman.

Long ago, King Chitraketu cried because he was holding tightly to God’s gift.

Today, this elderly devotee cried because he was holding on to God.

What a beautiful difference. The story is not telling us to love our family less. It is telling us not to forget the One who gave them to us.

Our children are God’s gift. Our parents are God’s gift. Our health is God’s gift. Our home is God’s gift.

Everything we have is God’s gift. Let us love these gifts with all our heart. But let us hold them with open hands. One day, every gift will stay here.

Only the Giver will be with us forever.

That ninety-three-year-old gentleman reminded me of this today.

After a whole lifetime of work, money, success, happiness and sorrow…

What brought tears to his eyes was not his job. Not his house. Not even his achievements. It was the Lord’s Name.

Thought for the Day

Perhaps peace begins the day we stop saying, “This is mine forever.”

And start saying, “Thank You, Lord, for this beautiful gift. I will enjoy it with love for as long as You let me keep it.”

Because one day, every blessing will stay behind.

Only our relationship with God will go with us.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 216 | Epic Series 86 | What will my children remember about me? | 
A few days ago, after a bhajan, a small group of us were sitting together and chatting. Someone casually asked, “What is the greatest inheritance we can leave our children?”

People spoke about education… good values… financial security… owning a house… leaving enough money so that life becomes easier for them.

The conversation ended there.

But on my way back home, one thought refused to leave me.

When I am no longer here… what part of me will still be living inside my children?

One day, they may open my cupboard. They may fold my clothes, go through my files, and decide what to keep and what to give away. Even the things I carefully protected for so many years will one day belong to someone else.

But what will they carry with them… for the rest of their lives?

Will they remember my love… or only my advice?

Will they remember that I was always busy… or that I always had time for them?

Will they remember my patience… or my anger?

Will they inherit only my money… or also my faith, my values, and my kindness?

As these thoughts stayed with me, I remembered a moving incident from the Bhagavatam.

Once, the Devas were in great danger. A mighty demon named Vritrasura had become so powerful that none of them could defeat him. They prayed to the Lord for help.

The answer they received must have shocked them. They were told that the only weapon powerful enough to defeat Vritrasura could be made from the bones of a great sage named Dadhichi. There was no other way.

Imagine how difficult it must have been for the Devas to approach him. What could they possibly say?

“Great Sage… we have not come to ask for your blessings. We have not come to ask for your wisdom. We have come to ask for your body.”

If someone asked me that today, I honestly don’t know what I would say. I would think of my wife… my children… my unfinished dreams… the people who depend on me. I might even ask, “Is there no other way?”

But Dadhichi’s response was beyond ordinary.

He smiled.

He said, “This body is only a temporary gift. One day I have to leave it anyway. If it can protect many lives before that day comes, what greater purpose can it have?”

He then sat in deep meditation and peacefully gave up his body.

From his backbone, the Vajra was made. With that weapon, Vritrasura was defeated, and countless lives were saved.

His body disappeared. But his life became unforgettable.

That story has stayed with me.

Dadhichi did not ask, “How long can I keep what I have?”

He asked, “How much good will remain after I am gone?”

That made me look at my own life differently.

Like many parents, I have often wondered, “How much should I leave for my children?”

But perhaps there is a more important question.

What am I leaving inside my children?

Will they inherit my worries… or my trust in God? Will they inherit my complaints… or my gratitude? Will they inherit my fear… or my courage? Will they inherit my possessions… or my values?

One day, my children may divide everything I own. But they will spend the rest of their lives living with everything I have placed inside them.

Perhaps…that is the only inheritance that never loses its value.

Thought for the Day

We spend much of our life thinking about what we will leave for our children.

Let us also spend some time thinking about what we are leaving in our children.

That is the legacy that lives long after we are gone.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 215 | Epic Series 85 | Can I still trust God… when He says “No”? | 
Yesterday, I wrote about waiting.

Waiting for a medical report… an interview result… a loved one to recover. Many of you wrote back saying, “That is exactly where I am today.”

This morning, a close friend from my bachelor days forwarded me a message that I have not been able to forget.

It was written by a wife who had just lost her husband, a deeply pious soul who had touched and inspired countless lives through his Upanyasams and spiritual discourses.

As I read her words, I could almost see the scene before my eyes. A husband lying on a hospital bed. A wife sitting beside him, watching every breath. Children rushing back from another country. Doctors walking in and out. Every time a doctor entered the room, a little hope would rise.

“Maybe today there will be good news.”

For twenty long days, the family hardly slept. They prayed continuously. Vishnu Sahasranamam… Lalita Sahasranamam… Mahamrityunjaya Mantra… Friends prayed. Relatives prayed. Hundreds, perhaps thousands, joined in prayer. But there was only one request.

“Please… don’t take him away from us.”

But one afternoon, he quietly breathed his last.

I put my phone down for a few moments. Not because I had finished reading, but because I simply could not continue. Suddenly, I realised… this was not only her story. It is the story of almost every family.

Perhaps not in a hospital. But somewhere in life.

We have all prayed for something with all our heart. A child to recover. A marriage to survive. A job to come through. A loved one to be spared. We prayed with complete faith… and yet life unfolded differently.

Those are the moments that shake us the most. Not because we stop believing in God, but because we don’t understand Him.

The Mahabharata gives us a similar moment. Abhimanyu was surrounded on the battlefield. Krishna had the power to save him, yet He did not. Arjuna’s heart broke. No explanation could remove that pain. But Krishna never left Arjuna’s side.

That made me wonder if we have misunderstood the purpose of prayer.

We often think prayer is meant to change God’s decision. Perhaps prayer is also meant to prepare our heart.

That family believed they were praying to save the life of someone they loved. Perhaps those very prayers were also quietly preparing them to bear the pain of letting him go.

Sometimes prayer changes our circumstances. Sometimes it changes us. And when life cannot be changed, perhaps changing us is the greater miracle.

One day, every one of us will receive a phone call we never wanted.

One day, every one of us will stand beside a hospital bed.

One day, every one of us will have to say goodbye to someone whose hand we never wanted to let go.

When that day comes, we will discover that faith is not the certainty that everything will happen the way we want.

Faith is the courage to whisper through our tears,

“Lord, I do not understand You today… but I still trust You.”

Thought for the Day

We often go to God asking Him to change our circumstances. Many times, He first prepares our heart.

Because one day, our prayers may not change the path that life takes…

But they can change the way we walk that path.

And perhaps…

That is the greatest miracle of all.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 214 | Epic Series 84 | Can I be peaceful… even when I don’t know what tomorrow holds? | 
Yesterday, my wife and I went for our annual Master Health Check-up. My wife is a doctor. My son is also a doctor. So I thought it would be just another routine day.

It wasn’t. One test after another…Blood test…X ray, ECG…Treadmill…ultrasound Scan…audiometry ..dental.. ophthalmic.. Doctor’s consultation…

Finally, everything was over. The receptionist smiled and said, “Please wait. Your final report will be ready shortly.”

Those few minutes felt surprisingly long. I looked around. Everyone was waiting. Some were talking. Some were scrolling through their phones. Some were smiling.

But I wondered…

Behind every smile, was there a silent prayer? “Please let everything be normal.”

Then a thought struck me.It is not the tests that make us anxious.It is not even the report.It is the not knowing.

Our mind finds it very difficult to live with uncertainty. That is why we suffer even before life gives us an answer.

King Parikshit faced the greatest uncertainty of all. He knew that in seven days, his life would end. Imagine what must have gone through his mind. “What will happen to my family?”…… “Why did this happen to me?”……”Can anyone save me?”

He had a kingdom. He had wealth. He had power. But none of them could remove his fear.

For seven days, Sage Sukadeva sat beside him. Interestingly, he never told the king how to escape death. Instead, he slowly freed him from the fear of death.

He filled the king’s mind with the stories of the Lord… His compassion… His love… His presence in every stage of life.

Day by day, something beautiful happened. The king stopped thinking about how long he had to live. He became absorbed in how deeply he could live. On the seventh day, Sukadeva gave his final teaching.

He said, “My dear King, do not keep thinking, ‘I am going to die.’ That fear comes because you have forgotten who you really are.”

By then, Parikshit was no longer the frightened king who had begun listening seven days earlier.

When Takshaka came, the snake could bite only his body. Fear had already left his heart.

Yesterday, our reports were reassuring, and we came home with gratitude.

But the visit left me with a question that I have been carrying since then.

Can I remain peaceful only when the report is normal? Or can I remain peaceful even while I am waiting?

Perhaps all of us are waiting for something today.

A medical report…..An interview result…..A court decision…..An exam result…..A phone call……A loved one to recover.

Life cannot always remove the waiting. But it can teach us how to wait.

Thought for the day

When life keeps you waiting, don’t allow your mind to write tomorrow’s story.

Stay with today. Do what you can. Leave the rest in God’s hands.

Very often, the waiting changes us… even before the answer arrives.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 213 | Epic Series 83 | What is it that bends us inside? | 
A few days ago, I was waiting outside a wedding hall at chennai , simply watching people arrive.

It is amazing how much we can observe when we are not in a hurry.

A young girl stepped out of a car. Before entering the hall, she looked at her reflection on her phone, adjusted her hair, straightened her dress, checked herself once again… and only then walked in.

A little later, an elderly gentleman arrived. He looked around, noticed a group of influential people talking, hesitated for a moment, and quietly chose a chair in a corner instead.

Then I noticed someone I know well. He is intelligent, experienced, and highly capable. Yet whenever there is an opportunity to speak or take responsibility, his usual response is, “No… let someone else do it.”

As I watched them, another thought came to me.

How different are they from me?. How many times have I worried about how I looked?

How many times have I remained silent because I wasn’t sure how my words would be received?

How many times have I avoided singing loudly, raising my hands during bhajans, volunteering, or trying something new because I didn’t want to look awkward?

Perhaps we have all done this.

None of those people had a bent back. But each seemed to be carrying something invisible.

One was carrying concern about appearance……Another was carrying concern about status……Another was carrying fear of being judged.

Our bodies may stand straight. Yet inside, many of us walk with a hunchback.

The Bhagavatam introduces us to Kubja, a maidservant in King Kamsa’s palace. She was physically bent in three places. Every day she carried fragrant sandalwood paste to decorate the cruel king.

When Krishna entered Mathura, Kubja saw Him. Something within her instantly recognised Him. Without hesitation, she offered Him the precious sandalwood paste she had been carrying for Kamsa.

It was a courageous act. The sandalwood belonged to the king. Giving it away could have brought severe punishment.

But in that moment, love became greater than fear.

Krishna accepted her offering with affection. Then He gently placed His feet on hers, lifted her chin, and straightened her body.

The miracle did not begin when Krishna touched her.

It began the moment she stopped living for Kamsa and started living for Krishna. Until then, she carried her finest fragrance for a tyrant. From that moment, she offered her very best to the Divine.

That changed everything.

Perhaps that is our story too.

Many of us spend our best energy trying to impress people, protect our image, and earn approval…..We carefully choose our words…..We hide our weaknesses….,We constantly wonder…”What will people think?”

That invisible burden slowly bends us from within.

The day we stop living for people’s approval and begin offering our best to a higher purpose, whether it is God, service, or simply doing what is right, we begin to stand straight again.

Thought for the Day

The heaviest burden we carry is not on our shoulders…..It is the need to be accepted by everyone.

Offer your best to God…..Let people think what they want.

You will discover a freedom that no compliment can give and no criticism can take away.

Dhanyosmi. 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 212 | Epic Series 82 | Who am I… when nobody is watching? | 
After our bhajan on Sunday, a few people came and spoke to me. One appreciated the singing. Another thanked me for the TFD messages. Someone said, “Sir, your voice is so soothing.”

As I drove home, a quiet question arose within me. “If one day all these compliments disappeared… who would I be?”

If I could no longer sing… if people stopped recognising me… if nobody appreciated me… would I still know who I am?

Another thought came to me. During many bhajans, I have noticed that as the devotion builds, some people forget themselves. They clap, raise their hands, sing joyfully, and even dance before the Lord with childlike innocence.

Others quietly remain seated. They sing softly or simply observe.

Neither is right nor wrong. We all express devotion differently.

But I sometimes wonder… what holds many of us back?

Is there a quiet fear within that whispers, “What if I look silly?” Perhaps many of us carry that invisible burden.

When someone asks, “Who are you?” we usually answer with our role: I am a doctor… an engineer… a businessman… a teacher… I am retired. These are true. But are they really who we are?

Slowly, our profession, achievements, reputation, and what others think of us become our identity. That is why losing a job, facing failure, or not being appreciated can feel like losing ourselves.

The Bhagavatam beautifully illustrates this.

The Gopis loved Krishna deeply. One day, while they were bathing in the Yamuna, Krishna gathered their clothes and climbed a nearby tree.

He was not interested in their clothes.

He was inviting them to come to Him without anything to hide behind - without pride, status, labels, or the identity they clung to. Just with love.

As I reflected on this, I realised that I too wear many invisible clothes - the need for appreciation, respect, usefulness, and a good image.

Perhaps Krishna was gently removing not their clothes, but everything that stood between their hearts and His.

Maybe that is His invitation to us too.

To let go of the labels we cling to and discover that our true identity is not in what we do, what we own, or what others think of us. It is simply that we belong to Him.

Thought for the Day

The world knows us by our name, profession, achievements, and image.

God knows us by our heart.

Perhaps true freedom begins the day we stop worrying about how we look before people…

and become comfortable standing just as we are before God.

Dhanyosmi. 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 211 | Epic Series 81 | When will I find my Guru? | 
The day before yesterday , we saw that it is never too late to change.

Today, let us look at another question. Many people may never say it aloud, but they carry it quietly in their hearts.

“I long to grow spiritually. I pray every day. I read good books. But I have still not found my Guru. When will God bless me with the right Master?”

The Bhagavatam tells us a beautiful story.

One day, King Yadu met a young saint walking alone in a forest. He owned nothing, yet his face shone with joy and peace.

The King bowed to him and asked, “Who is your Guru?”

The saint smiled and said, “I have learnt from twenty-four Gurus.” The King was surprised.

“Twenty-four? Who are they?”

The saint pointed to the ground beneath his feet. “The earth is one of my Gurus. People dig it, stamp on it and throw dirt on it. Yet it quietly gives us food, water and a place to live. It taught me patience.”

He looked up as the breeze touched his face. “The wind is another Guru. It passes through a garden and also through a dirty place. But it does not carry either with it. It taught me to live in this world without becoming attached.”

Then he pointed to a tiny spider. “It spins its own web and later pulls it back. Watching it, I realised that many of the problems that trap me are webs woven by my own mind.”

Finally he said, “One of my Gurus was a woman named Pingala. She waited the whole night for someone who never came. At last she gave up waiting. The moment she let go of her expectations, her mind became peaceful. That night, she became my Guru.”

King Yadu stood in silence. He realised that this saint had a rare gift. He never stopped being a student. So God never stopped sending him teachers.

As I reflected on this story, one thought touched my heart.

We all pray for the blessing of a true Guru. There can be no greater blessing in life.

But perhaps, while we are waiting to meet our Guru, God is quietly preparing us. Every prayer…Every scripture…Every experience…Every lesson…is making us a little more ready.

Thought for the Day

Pray that God blesses you with a true Guru. And while you wait…pray for one more thing.

“Lord, give me the humility to learn every lesson You are teaching me today.”

A heart that never stops learning…is a heart that is ready to receive the Guru’s grace.

Dhanyosmi. 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 210 | Epic Series 80 | Have I wasted my life? | 
Yesterday, we saw that even good people sometimes go through painful situations. Life is much bigger than what we can see. We see only one page. God sees the whole book.

Today, let us look at another question. It is a question that many people carry silently in their hearts.

“I am already sixty… or seventy…..I have made too many mistakes…..I chased money and forgot my family…..I lost my health because of my habits…..I hurt the people who loved me…..I wasted so many years…….Is it too late for me?”

Many people may never ask this question aloud. But deep inside, they wonder whether their life can still change.

Our scriptures tell us the touching story of Ajamila.

As a young man, Ajamila was honest, kind and deeply spiritual. Everyone believed he would live a noble life. But one wrong friendship slowly changed everything. One bad decision led to another. Slowly he lost his values, neglected his family, and spent many years chasing worldly pleasures. The young man who once walked towards God slowly walked away from Him.

Years became decades.

When Ajamila was eighty years old, he lay on his deathbed. For the first time, he realised that his life was about to end. Filled with fear, he saw the messengers of death approaching him. In that moment, he cried out loudly, “Narayana!”

He was not calling God. He was calling his youngest son, whose name was Narayana.But something extraordinary happened.

The moment the holy name “Narayana” came from his lips, the messengers of Lord Vishnu appeared and stopped the messengers of death. Ajamila was not taken away. Instead, he was given another chance to live.

That one moment changed everything.

He understood the grace that had been shown to him. This time, he did not waste it. He spent the rest of his life remembering God, living a righteous life, and finally attained liberation.

The story is not teaching us that we can do anything we want and remember God at the last minute. That is not its message at all.

Its message is far more beautiful.

For eighty years, Ajamila’s life was moving in one direction. Everyone would have thought, “His story is over.” But God did not look only at his past. God looked at the moment his heart turned.

How often we imprison ourselves with these words…

“I have always been like this.”

“I can never change.”

“It is too late now.”

But who told us that?. One decision to forgive can heal years of anger…..One decision to seek help can begin freedom from addiction……One sincere apology can heal a broken relationship……One decision to return to God can change the direction of an entire life.

We cannot rewrite yesterday. But we can write a different tomorrow. That is the hope that Ajamila’s life gives every one of us.

Thought for the Day

Never let your past decide your future.

Your mistakes may explain where you have been. They do not decide where you can go.

God is not waiting for a perfect past. He is waiting for a willing heart.

As long as you are breathing…Your story is not over.

Dhanyosmi. 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 209 | Epic Series - 79 | I have tried to live a good life… then why is life so unfair? | 
Yesterday, we spoke about loving without attachment. Some of you wrote saying, “That gave me peace.”

But today, let us look at another question. A question that silently troubles many good people.

”I have never cheated anyone…..I have worked honestly all my life…….I have helped people whenever I could…….Then why am I going through so much pain?”

A sudden serious illness…A court case…A business loss…A family problem

Sometimes we even look around and wonder, “The people who cheat seem to be happy. Why is life so hard only for me?”

Our scriptures tell us a beautiful story.

There once lived a king called Nriga. He was famous for his kindness. He donated millions of cows to poor people and learned scholars. People loved him. Everyone said, “What a noble king!”

One day, something happened that nobody expected. A cow that King Nriga had already donated quietly wandered back into his cowshed. No one noticed.

Later, by mistake, that same cow was gifted to another scholar. Soon, two men came before the king.

One said, “This cow is mine.”

The other said, “No. It was given to me.”

King Nriga was shocked. He folded his hands and said, “Please forgive me. It was an honest mistake.”He even offered thousands of cows in exchange for that one cow.

But the matter could not be settled.Because of that one mistake, King Nriga was cursed.

After his death, he was born as a huge lizard and remained trapped inside a deep, dark well.Not for a few days. Not for a few years. For thousands of years.

Can you imagine that?

A king who had spent his whole life doing good…Now sitting alone in darkness…Unable to come out…Waiting.

Finally, one day, Lord Krishna came there. He lifted the giant lizard out of the well. At once, the curse ended.

King Nriga got back his divine form. He bowed to Krishna with tears in his eyes and went to the higher worlds.

When we hear this story, one question naturally comes to our mind. “How can this be fair?” That is exactly the same question we ask in our own life. “Why did this happen to me?”

The story gives us a very deep answer. We see only one small part of life. God sees the whole journey. Life is not only about what we did yesterday or last year. It is a much bigger account.

Sometimes, a person who has lived a good life may still go through unexpected suffering. Not because God has forgotten them. Not because all their goodness has gone to waste.

But because life may be quietly settling an old account that we know nothing about.

King Nriga’s millions of good deeds were never lost. But one small unfinished account also had to be settled. Only then was he finally free.

Perhaps many of us are also carrying some old accounts we do not remember. So when life feels unfair, let us not become bitter.

Let us not stop doing good. Because every good deed is still adding light to our life.

Thought for the Day

When life asks you to go through a painful season, do not keep asking, “Why me?”

Instead ask, “What is life helping me complete?”

We see only one page. God sees the whole book. So keep doing good. Keep walking with faith.

One day, everything will make sense.

Dhanyosmi. 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 208 | Epic Series -78 | If I stop worrying… does it mean I love them less? | 
Yesterday, we spoke about an important lesson.

If we keep solving every problem for our children, they may never become mentally strong. Just like a butterfly becomes strong only by struggling out of its cocoon, our children also need to face some struggles in life.

Some of you wrote back after reading that message.

Almost every parent asked the same question. “I understand I should not solve all their problems. But how do I stop worrying? They are my children. If I don’t worry about them, who will?”

I couldn’t answer that question immediately. I simply sat with it. Because I realised…this is not just a parent’s question. It is the question of every loving heart.

A husband worries about his wife……A wife worries about her husband…….Children worry about their ageing parents……..

Parents worry about their grown-up children……,.The people may be different. But the worry is the same.

So where does loving care end…and where does painful attachment begin?

A beautiful story from our scriptures answered that question for me.

Long ago lived King Bharata. He ruled a great kingdom. Later, he gave up everything and went to the forest to spend his life in meditation.

His mind became peaceful.His heart became calm. One day, he found a tiny deer whose mother had died. He picked it up with great love. He fed it. Protected it. Cared for it.

There was nothing wrong in what he did. Helping the helpless is always noble. But slowly… something changed.

Every day he thought a little less about God…and a little more about the deer. If the deer disappeared for some time, he became restless.

“Has it eaten?”

“Is it safe?”

“Has a wild animal attacked it?”

The deer was no longer in his hands.It had entered his mind.

The scriptures say that when King Bharata breathed his last, his final thought was the deer. Because his mind was completely attached to it, he was born as a deer in his next life.

The story is not telling us not to love. It is asking us a much deeper question.

Has my love become so anxious that I have lost my own peace?

Many of us do not have a deer at home. But many of us have one living inside our mind.

It may be a son working in another city…..A daughter studying abroad…..A spouse travelling alone……An elderly parent living by themselves.

We check the phone again and again. We imagine problems that have not even happened. Slowly, we lose today’s peace because of tomorrow’s fear.

Let me ask you something.

Has worrying ever prevented an accident?….Has worrying ever changed another person’s destiny?

No.

Calling them helps.Supporting them helps. Praying for them helps. But worrying only steals our peace… without protecting them.

Perhaps that is why the Bhagavad Gita teaches us to do our duty sincerely, but not carry the burden of controlling life.

Our children are not our possessions……Our parents are not our possessions……Even our spouse is not our possession……They are precious lives entrusted to our care for some time.

We can love them deeply. We can stand beside them. We can pray for them every day.

But we cannot live their life for them.

Thought for the day

Today, whenever you catch yourself worrying about someone you love, try this simple practice.

Ask yourself, “Is there anything I can do right now?”

If the answer is Yes, do it. If the answer is No, stop worrying.

Offer a small prayer. “I have done my part. Dear God, now they are in Your care.”

That is not giving up. That is trusting. And there is a beautiful difference.

Love with all your heart. Care with all your strength. But leave the burden to God.

That is love without attachment.

Dhanyosmi. 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 207 | Epic series -77 | How do I make my grown-up child mentally strong? | 
A mother sent me this question.

She wrote, “My daughter worries too much. She keeps thinking about the same problem again and again. Whatever I say, she feels I don’t understand her. I want to help her. But I don’t know what to do.”

As I read her message, I thought…This is not only her question. This is the question of almost every parent.

We all want to protect our children. We all want to remove their pain.

But can we?

The mother then shared something beautiful.

She said, “I first listen. Then I help her think. I don’t want to solve her problems. she should find her own answers.”

Then she wrote one line that touched my heart. “We should not cut open the cocoon to help the butterfly.”

How true ! If we cut open the cocoon…The butterfly may come out easily. But it will never become strong enough to fly.

Then she told me about her own mother. Her mother faced many problems in life. But she never gave up. She always trusted God.

Without giving big advice…Without giving long lectures…She taught her daughter how to face life. Then suddenly, one thought came to my mind.

Maybe this mother has already answered her own question.

Children do not become strong only by listening to us. They become strong by watching us. They watch how we face problems. They watch how we speak when life is hard. They watch whether we lose hope… or keep going.

Our life teaches more than our words.

Then another thought came. Maybe her daughter is not weak. Maybe she is only tired. There is a big difference.

Even a strong person feels weak when the mind becomes tired. At that time, people are usually not waiting for advice. They are waiting for someone to understand them.

Someone who will simply say, “I am here. Tell me what is in your heart.”

There is a beautiful lesson in the Bhagavad Gita.

Before Lord Krishna spoke to Arjuna…He first listened. Arjuna cried. He shared his fear. He shared his confusion.

Krishna did not stop him. He listened first. Only then did He guide. Maybe that is the secret. Listening is not a small thing. Listening itself is the beginning of healing.

Maybe this is why our children sometimes don’t accept our advice. Not because they don’t love us. Not because they don’t respect us.

But because their heart is quietly saying, “Please understand me first… then help me.”

That one sentence changed the way I look at parenting. Maybe our children do not need parents who have all the answers. Maybe they simply need parents who are a safe place……A place where they can cry without feeling ashamed……A place where they can fail without being scolded……A place where they can sit quietly until their courage comes back.

Because no one becomes strong when others keep solving every problem for them. People become strong when they slowly discover,

”This problem is big… but I am bigger.”

And many times…They discover that only because someone stood beside them… believed in them… and never gave up on them.

Maybe that is one of the greatest gifts a parent can ever give.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 206 | Epic Series - 76 | Have you noticed… some problems become bigger, the more we try to solve them? | 
Have you noticed… some problems become bigger, the more we try to solve them?

There are some battles that begin in the morning…and continue even after we go to bed.

You wake up thinking about your health…,…You drive to work thinking about your business……You eat lunch thinking about your son’s future…….You come home thinking about your daughter’s marriage…….You lie down at night thinking about money.

The mind never gets a holiday. The problem may be outside. But the battle continues inside.

Day after day.Month after month.Year after year. Have you ever felt this?

“I am trying so hard… but somehow I am only becoming more tired.”

Our ancestors told a beautiful story just for moments like this.

Most of us know the story of Gajendra Moksham.

A mighty elephant named Gajendra entered a beautiful lake with his family. The water was cool. The day was peaceful. Without warning, a crocodile caught his leg.

Gajendra did what any one of us would have done. He fought. He pulled with all his strength. He refused to give up.

He believed, “If I try harder… I can get out.” The struggle continued. Not for a few minutes. Not for a few hours. For a very long time. Then something strange happened.

The stronger elephant slowly became weaker. The smaller crocodile slowly became stronger.

Why?

Because they were fighting inside water. That was the crocodile’s strength.

Is it not that exactly how life sometimes feels?

The more we worry…the more tired we become. The problem remains.

The more we try to control another person…the more the relationship slips away.

The more we fear growing old…the faster every grey hair catches our attention.

The more we replay yesterday…the less we live today.

Without realising it…our mind keeps fighting battles in places where it can never win.

Then came the turning point.

Gajendra did not suddenly become stronger. The crocodile did not suddenly become weaker. The situation had not changed. Only one thing changed.

Gajendra stopped believing that he could control everything by his own strength.

What does that mean for us? It does not mean we stop trying.

It does not mean we stop working. It does not mean we stop taking treatment, earning a living, or caring for our family. It simply means learning the difference between effort… and control.

I cannot control how someone replies…..I can work sincerely. I cannot control every result…..I can take care of my health. I cannot stop time……I can guide my children. I cannot live their lives for them.

Many of our worries begin the day we start carrying responsibilities that were never really ours. Perhaps that is why peace does not come from doing less. Peace comes from carrying less.

Maybe every morning we can ask ourselves just one simple question. “What is truly in my hands today?”

Do that sincerely. Give it your best. Then quietly let go of everything else. Not because it does not matter. But because worrying has never been the same as solving.

Perhaps this is the quiet wisdom hidden inside Gajendra Moksham.

The elephant was not saved because he stopped making an effort. He was saved because he stopped fighting a battle that no amount of personal strength could win.

Thought for the Day

Life becomes lighter the day we stop trying to control everything. Do your duty with all your heart. Accept that some things will unfold in their own time.

And protect your peace for the battles where your effort can truly make a difference.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 205 | Epic Series - 75 | Could I have completely misunderstood someone… for forty years? | 
It sounds impossible, doesn’t it?

How can two people live under the same roof for forty years, share the same joys and sorrows, raise children together, grow old together… and still misunderstand each other?

Yet, that is exactly what happened to an elderly couple.

People admired them. They had been married for more than forty years. Their children were well settled, and their grandchildren filled their home with laughter. Whenever relatives visited, they would quietly say, “What a wonderful couple! God has truly blessed them.”

But no one knew the silent story hidden inside that home.

One afternoon, the wife was cleaning an old wooden cupboard. Tucked away behind a few dusty files was a small notebook. It belonged to her husband.

She smiled to herself. “Since when did he start writing?”. Out of curiosity, she opened it.

Most pages contained only a few simple thoughts. Then one sentence caught her attention.

“Every evening after coming home from work, I wished she would sit beside me for just ten minutes. But she always looked busy. I never wanted to disturb her.”

She read it once. Then again.

The notebook slowly slipped from her hands. She sat down on the floor, unable to move. Her mind travelled back over four decades.

Every evening, after her husband returned from work, she quietly remained in the kitchen. She had always believed she was doing the loving thing.

“He has worked so hard all day,” she would tell herself. “He must be tired. Let him relax. If I go and sit beside him now, I may only disturb him.”

So she stayed away. Not because she didn’t love him. But because she loved him.

And every evening, her husband sat alone in the hall. Not because he wanted to be left alone. But because he silently hoped she would come and sit beside him for just a few minutes. She stayed away because she cared.

He waited because he cared. Both loved each other deeply. Both misunderstood each other.

For forty long years. That night, after everyone had gone to bed, she quietly walked towards him and placed the notebook in his hands.

He looked at it. Then he looked at her. With tears rolling down her cheeks, she asked softly, “Is this really true?”. He nodded. “All these years… you wanted me to sit beside you?”

He nodded again. She whispered, “Then why didn’t you tell me?” He smiled gently. “Because… I thought you already knew.”

She held his hand tightly and began to cry. “And I stayed away… because I thought you wanted to be left alone.”

For a long time, neither of them spoke. There was nothing more to explain. It was never a marriage without love. It was a marriage that had quietly lived with one misunderstanding.

As I reflected on this story, one question kept troubling me.

How many homes are living this very story today?

A husband quietly concludes, “She doesn’t care about me anymore.”

A wife silently feels, “He doesn’t understand me.”

Parents think, “Our children have changed.”

Children think, “My parents never really understood me.”

A brother assumes his sister has forgotten him.

A sister believes her brother no longer needs her.

Years pass. The silence grows. The assumptions become stronger. The distance becomes deeper. Then one unexpected conversation…

One forgotten letter…One hospital visit…One final goodbye…

And suddenly people discover that the love was never missing. Only the understanding was.

Our ancestors captured this truth beautifully through a touching incident from the childhood of little Krishna.

One afternoon, Krishna was sitting on the soft grass with His cowherd friends. It was lunchtime. Each child had brought a simple meal from home. One happily offered Krishna a piece of his half-eaten fruit. Another shared a handful of curd rice from his own leaf. Someone else lovingly gave Him a small piece of butter.

Krishna accepted everything with joy. He laughed with them, teased them and ate from their hands just like any innocent village boy.

Watching this scene, Lord Brahma was deeply puzzled.

“Can this really be the Supreme Lord?” he wondered. “How can God sit on the ground like an ordinary child? How can He eat food that others have already tasted?”

The problem was not what Brahma saw. The problem was the picture of God that he had already created in his own mind.

So Brahma decided to test Krishna. Using his divine power, he quietly hid every calf. Then he also hid every cowherd boy. “Now the truth will be revealed,” he thought.

Nearly a year later, Brahma returned. He expected frightened parents. He expected confusion everywhere.

Instead, he saw children laughing, calves running and mothers smiling. Everything looked exactly as it had before.

He was stunned. “How can this be? I hid every child and every calf. Then who are these?”

As he stood there in complete disbelief, something beyond imagination happened. Every child began to shine. Every calf began to shine. Then, in a moment that left Brahma speechless, every one of them revealed the form of Lord Vishnu. Brahma saw countless universes shining within each form. He realised that the little boy he had tried to test was none other than the Supreme Lord Himself.

His pride melted away. He fell at Krishna’s feet. Not because Krishna had changed. Krishna had always been the same.

It was Brahma’s understanding that had changed. Isn’t that exactly what happens in our own lives?

Most of our pain does not come from what people actually do. It comes from the meaning our mind gives to what they do.

“I know my husband.”

“I know my wife.”

“I know why she said that.”

“I know what he meant.”

The day we become completely certain that we know another person, we stop asking. We stop listening. We stop discovering.

Like Brahma, we become prisoners of our own assumptions.

Thought for the Day

Many relationships do not become beautiful because people change.

They become beautiful the day we become humble enough to say, “Perhaps I have misunderstood this person.”

One honest conversation can heal years of silence. One sincere question can remove years of hurt. And one moment of genuine understanding can bring two hearts together again.

Perhaps the person you think has stopped loving you…

Has simply been loving you in a language you never learnt to recognise.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 204 | Epic Series-74 | Why did things become worse after I tried to make them better? | 
Today, let me tell you about two families I know.

When I first heard their stories, I had the same question they had. Maybe… you too have asked this question sometime in your life.

The First Family

Four brothers and a sister met one sunday morning. Their parents were no more. The only thing left was to divide the family property.

Before anyone spoke, the eldest brother said, “Whatever we do today, let us do it with love. We should all remain one family.”

Everyone nodded. For a few minutes, everything went well. Then one brother quietly said, “Anna… before we divide the property, can I ask one question?”

The room became silent. “All these years… who stayed with Appa when he was in hospital?”

Nobody answered. After a few seconds, another brother said, “And who spent for Amma’s treatment?”

The sister slowly wiped her eyes. She said, “Nobody knows what I gave up after my marriage.”

The property papers were still lying on the table. But nobody was looking at them anymore.

Old pain…Old sacrifices…Old disappointments…Everything that had been sleeping quietly for years suddenly woke up. That evening, everyone went home without dividing the property.

One brother said before leaving, “We came here to make peace… Why did everything become worse?”

The Second Family

It was their fortieth wedding anniversary. Their children surprised them with a dinner outside. For the first time in many years, the husband and wife were sitting alone.

No office calls. No grandchildren. No hurry. Just the two of them.

The husband smiled and said, “We have spent forty years together.”

Then he laughed and asked, “Tell me honestly… have I ever hurt you?”

She smiled. For a few moments, she said nothing. Then she looked out of the window and said softly,

“There are a few things I never had the courage to tell you.”. One memory led to another.

“I waited for you on many birthdays.”

“I wanted you beside me when Appa passed away.”

“I smiled in front of everyone… but many times I cried alone.”

The husband held her hand. He said quietly, “I never knew you carried all this in your heart.”

When they reached home, there were tears in both their eyes. The children thought, “What a beautiful anniversary!”

Only the husband and wife knew what had happened. As they went to bed that night, both wondered, “We wanted to celebrate our life together… Why did so much old pain come out?”

These two families were asking the same question. “When our intention was good… why did so much pain come out?”

Our ancestors answered this question thousands of years ago through the beautiful story of Samudra Manthan.

The Devas and the Asuras wanted only one thing. The nectar of immortality. So they began churning the Ocean of Milk. But something unexpected happened.

The nectar did not come first.The very first thing that came out was Halahala, a deadly poison. Only after the poison came many precious treasures. Only at the end did the nectar appear.

What a wonderful picture of life!

Whenever we sincerely try to make something better, we begin to churn it. A family. A marriage. Our own heart.

And when the churning starts…what has been lying hidden for years begins to come out.

Old hurt. Old anger. Old disappointment. Old fear.

The churning did not create them. They were already there. It simply brought them into the light. That is why the property meeting became painful. Not because talking was wrong.

But because old wounds had never healed. That is why the anniversary dinner ended in tears. Not because the marriage became worse.

But because hidden pain finally found a safe place to come out. This is where many of us make a mistake.

The moment the poison appears, we stop the churning.

We say, “We should never have discussed the property.”

“I should never have asked that question.”

“Everything was better before.”

But was it really better? Or was the pain simply lying quietly underneath?

Then comes the most beautiful part of the story.

When the poison came out, Lord Shiva did not panic. He did not throw it back. He did not run away. He held it in his throat until the danger passed. That is the wisdom we need in our own homes.

When old hurts come out, we don’t have to answer every word. We don’t have to win every argument. We don’t have to decide everything that day.

Sometimes, the greatest wisdom is to hold the moment. To listen. To understand. To give each other’s pain some space. Only then can the healing continue. Only then can the nectar appear.

Thought for the day

The next time your sincere effort brings out old pain, don’t be afraid. Don’t think, “Everything has become worse.”

Instead, gently ask yourself, “Has the hidden poison finally come out?”

If the answer is “yes”…don’t stop the churning. Because the poison is not the end of the story. Very often… it is the first sign that the nectar is on its way.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 203 | Epic Series - 73 | When the people we love make us feel small… | 
When the people we love make us feel small…

Yesterday we saw little Narada.

Life suddenly took away the only person he had. He accepted what he could not change and moved forward with faith.

Today we meet another little boy. His pain was very different. This is a pain many of us have quietly carried. Sometimes, the deepest hurt does not come from strangers. It comes from the people whose love we long for the most.

A child feels a brother or sister is loved more. A husband feels his efforts are never noticed. A wife feels nobody values all that she silently does every day. An elderly parent feels forgotten after spending a lifetime for the family.

At work too, someone else gets the appreciation, the promotion or the credit, while we wonder, “What about me?”

These moments leave a wound inside us. We may not speak about it. But we keep asking ourselves,

”Am I not good enough?”

This was the pain of little Dhruva. Dhruva was only five years old. One day, he happily ran to sit on his father’s lap. Before he could, his stepmother pushed him away. She said he did not deserve that place.

The little boy ran to his mother, crying. His mother could not change the king. She could not change the stepmother. She could not erase what had happened.

But she stopped her son from spending his whole life waiting for the love of people who had already rejected him. That changed everything.

Instead of sitting with his hurt, Dhruva chose to grow. Instead of trying to prove himself to people, he began a journey to discover his real worth.

That journey finally led him to the Lord. In just six months, he had the darshan of Lord Vishnu.

The little boy who was denied a place on his father’s lap received a place that even great kings could never dream of.

Life teaches us a beautiful lesson here.

Many of us spend years trying to win someone’s approval. We hope that one day they will appreciate us…respect us…accept us…But even if that day comes, the happiness does not last.

Someone else’s opinion can never become the foundation of our self-worth. Real peace begins when we stop asking, “Who values me?”

Instead, we ask, “Am I becoming a better person than yesterday?”

Thought for the Day

Rejection can make us bitter. Or it can make us better.

The choice is ours. Dhruva chose to grow. That is why the world remembers him even today

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 202 | Epic Series - 72 | Can our faith stand when life changes ? | 
Most of us carry a silent fear. We rarely talk about it. We pray every day for our family.

“May my children be happy.”

“May my spouse be healthy.”

“May my parents be safe.”

That prayer comes from love. There is nothing wrong with it. But along with that prayer, another thought quietly sits inside us. “What if something goes wrong?”

When our child is travelling, we wait for that one message. “Reached safely.” If our husband or wife is late, we look at the phone again and again. If someone we love is unwell, we keep hoping the next test report will be normal.

If our son or daughter is going through a difficult time in life, we keep wondering, “Will everything become alright?” This is how all loving hearts are. None of us can say, “Nothing will ever happen.”

We wear a seat belt. We buy health insurance. We save money for difficult days. Not because we are negative. But because we know that life is uncertain.

Spiritual life is also like that. Faith is not believing that nothing painful will ever happen. Faith is trusting God even if life takes a turn we never expected.

The Bhagavatam gives us a beautiful example.

Before he became the great Sage Narada, he was a small boy. His mother was his whole world. One evening, while she was milking a cow, a snake bit her. She died instantly. In one moment, everything changed.

Narada did not pretend that nothing had happened. He did not run away from reality. He accepted it. Not because it was easy. But because he believed that God had not stopped caring for him.

That trust gave him the strength to walk forward. The little boy later became the great Sage Narada.

Acceptance does not mean we stop loving. Acceptance does not mean we stop praying. We continue to do both.

But deep inside, we also know that every person we love is finally in God’s hands, not ours. That understanding does not make our love smaller. It makes our faith stronger.

Thought for the Day

We cannot protect our loved ones from everything. We cannot control every tomorrow.

But we can slowly build a heart that says, “Whatever life brings… I will do my best… and I will continue to trust God.”

That quiet trust is called Viswaasam. (விசுவாசம் / விஸ்வாசம்)

And that is one of the greatest strengths we can have.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 201 | Epic Series - 71 | What if the person who bothers you the most Is actually helping you? | 
Have you noticed something?

Most of our unhappiness does not come from big problems. It comes from ordinary people we meet every day.

At home, someone finds fault with almost everything you do. If you keep quiet, they ask why you are quiet. If you speak, they ask why you spoke. Sometimes you wonder, “Is there any answer that will make them happy?”

At work, someone else gets the credit for your hard work. Your boss remembers only your mistakes. A junior does not listen. A senior does not notice you.

A friend whom you helped is nowhere to be seen when you need help.

A relative says something that hurts and then smiles and says, “I only said it for your good!”

Sometimes, nobody says anything at all. They don’t call. They don’t reply. They don’t invite you. They don’t ask what you think. Slowly, you start feeling that you don’t matter.

And just like that…

The peace you had in the morning quietly packs its bags and leaves. Then the mind gets very busy. It keeps playing the same scene again and again.

“We should have said this.”

“No… we should have said that.”

By bedtime, we have given five wonderful replies. Unfortunately, only inside our head!

The funny part is…

The other person is sleeping peacefully. We are the ones still holding the meeting in our mind.

Many of us pray, “God, please take this person away from my life.” But maybe God is smiling and saying, “I am not trying to remove that person. I am trying to improve you.”

This is what we learn from the Bhagavatam.

There was a powerful demon called Vritrasura. Everyone thought he was the bad man. Even King Indra fought against him. But there was a surprise. Vritrasura was a great devotee of the Lord. While fighting, he was praying to the Lord. He did not hate Indra. He was not worried about winning. He only wanted to finish his karma and go back to the Lord.

What looked like a battle…Was actually helping him reach God.

Maybe it is the same in our life.

Maybe difficult people are not in our life by mistake. Maybe God has kept them near us for a reason.

Think about going to a gym. If all the weights are made of sponge, nobody will become strong. Heavy weights make the muscles grow. Life is like that too.

Nice people make us comfortable. Difficult people make us stronger.

They show us our anger. Our pride. Our ego. Our need for appreciation. Our expectation that everyone should understand us.

Without such people, we may happily think we are already patient, humble and loving.

Many times, the real fight is not with the person standing in front of us. The real fight is with the ego sitting quietly inside us.

The day we stop asking, “How can I change this person?”

and start asking,

“What is this person teaching me?”, something beautiful begins to happen.

Slowly…

We change. And when we change, the whole world begins to look a little different.

The biggest victory is not proving someone else wrong. The biggest victory is keeping our peace, whatever others may do.

Thought for the Day

The people who trouble us the most may not be stopping our journey.

They may be the very people God is using to make us a little wiser… a little humbler… and a little closer to Him.

@@ 200 |  | A Few Quiet Minutes for the Mind | 
Today marks the 200th Thought for the Day.

Looking back, I do not feel a sense of achievement. I only feel deep gratitude.

This journey has happened one morning at a time, guided by a Grace far greater than any one of us.

Every question, every doubt, and every life situation shared has given us a chance to pause, think, and discover the timeless wisdom in our epics. More than anything else, these 200 mornings have taught me one simple truth.

The biggest battle in life is rarely outside us. It is inside our own mind.

No matter who we are or what stage of life we are in, every mind carries a burden.

A parent worries about a child. An elderly person quietly waits for a call from someone far away. Someone is worried about health. Another is anxious about money or an uncertain future. Someone else is carrying the pain of rejection, a broken relationship, an old hurt that refuses to go away, or the silent weight of loneliness.

Our situations may be different, but the thoughts running through our minds are often the same.

The problem is not that these thoughts come. That is part of being human. The real question is, what are we feeding our mind every day?

Without even realizing it, our mind is constantly fed by news, social media, endless opinions, comparisons, criticism, worries, fears, and distractions. Just as our body becomes what we repeatedly eat, our mind slowly becomes what we repeatedly think.

That is why these few quiet minutes every morning matter. They do not remove life’s problems or give instant solutions. But before the world fills our mind with noise, they help us fill it with something better.

They invite us to look at life through the wisdom of our epics and ask ourselves, “Is there another way of looking at this?”. Sometimes, that one question is enough to replace anxiety with acceptance, reaction with reflection, bitterness with understanding, and helplessness with hope.

Real change rarely happens in one big moment. It happens through small changes in the way we think. One good thought today, another tomorrow, and then another. Slowly, without even noticing it, our thinking begins to change. And when our thinking changes, our experience of life changes too.

Just as we make time every day to feed our body, we also need a few quiet minutes to feed our mind. If these reflections have helped you pause before reacting, worry a little less, forgive a little sooner, or carry life’s burdens a little more lightly, then this journey has already served its purpose.

Thought for the day

As we complete 200 TFDs, my simple prayer is this: may we continue to feed our minds with thoughts that make us calmer, kinder, wiser, and stronger.

Because a peaceful mind does not come from a life without problems. It comes from a mind that is fed with the right thoughts every day.

Dhanyosmi. 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 199 | Epic Series - 70 | Why Do We Keep Postponing God? | 
Yesterday we saw something beautiful. When life throws us into the deepest pit, Krishna does not stand far away. He comes down to lift us up.

But today let us ask a different question.

If Krishna is so loving… why do so many of us keep postponing Him?

It is not because we don’t believe in Him. It is not because we don’t love Him. We simply tell ourselves, “This is not the right time.”

“Let this project finish.”

“I have responsibilities. I have EMI”

“Let my children settle down.”

“Once I retire, I will have more time.”

“Right now life is too busy.”

Many of us think devotion needs a lot of free time, strict discipline and complete peace of mind. We imagine that we have to leave everything else behind before we can truly come closer to God.

So we keep postponing Him.

But have you noticed something? The work never ends. Responsibilities never end. The desire to earn a little more never ends. You can even see people in their eighties still worrying about money, still counting every rupee, still thinking about one more investment or one more business opportunity.

There is nothing wrong with earning money or fulfilling our responsibilities. Krishna never asked us to run away from life. But somewhere along the journey, we quietly pushed Him to the end of our priority list.

“We will come to You later.”

Then the Srimad Bhagavatam tells us a story that completely changes the way we look at God.

There was a woman named Putana. She did not come to worship Krishna. She did not come with flowers or Tulasi. She did not even come to see Him. She came with only one intention to kill Him.

She smeared deadly poison on her breast, dressed herself like a loving mother and carried baby Krishna in her arms.

Everyone expected Krishna to punish her. But Krishna saw something no one else saw.

For a few moments, she held Him like a mother. She offered Him her breast like a mother. Her intention was evil, but Krishna chose to notice that tiny resemblance of motherly affection.

Instead of remembering her poison, He remembered that one small act. Instead of punishment, He gave her liberation (moksha).

Can you imagine such compassion?

That is why Krishna is called Bhakta-vidheyan. He allows Himself to be won over by even the smallest expression of love. He is not waiting for perfect devotees. He is searching for even the smallest reason to shower His mercy.

If Krishna could accept even Putana’s false act as a reason to give her liberation, why do we think our sincere but imperfect devotion is not enough?

We think we have to become spiritually perfect before coming to Him. Krishna is waiting even for one small step.

One Tulasi leaf.

One heartfelt prayer.

One sincere “Krishna.”

One moment of remembering Him before we begin our day.

That is enough to melt His heart.

Why then should we postpone Him until retirement? Why wait until life becomes easier? That day may never come. But today… this very moment… belongs to Him.

Thought for the Day

Do not wait for the perfect time to come closer to Krishna. He is not asking for everything you have.

He is only waiting for one small offering of love.

Start today.

Because even one sincere step towards Krishna is enough for Him to take a thousand steps towards you.

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️
