@@ 198 | Epic Series - 69 | Why Does Happiness Never Stay for Long? | 
Perhaps the greatest struggle in life is not that we are unhappy…It is that we keep believing,

“Just one more thing… and I will finally be at peace.”

Just one more promotion.Just one more salary hike. Just one more house.Just one more problem solved. Just until my children settle. Just until life becomes a little more secure.

Our mind quietly convinces us that happiness is waiting on the other side of the next achievement. So we work harder. Sacrifice more. Pray more. Wait patiently.

And one day…

It finally happens. For a while, life feels complete. We smile more. We sleep better. We tell ourselves,

“Finally… this is what I was waiting for.”

But then…

Without any warning…The joy quietly slips away. Nothing has changed. The promotion is still there. The house is still there. The family is still there. Yet something inside us starts searching again…

Another goal. Another dream. Another destination.

Have you ever wondered… Why does happiness never stay?

Thousands of years ago, the Bhagavatham answered this question through the beautiful story of Kardama Muni and Devahuti.

Kardama Muni was a great sage who lived with almost nothing. Devahuti was a princess who willingly gave up every royal comfort to marry him. For years, she lovingly served him without complaint.

Moved by her sacrifice, Kardama Muni decided to reward her. Using his divine powers, he created a magnificent aerial palace filled with unimaginable beauty, luxury and comfort.

They travelled the worlds, enjoyed pleasures beyond imagination and experienced everything that people dream of.

In one lifetime…

They had experienced both extremes. A life with almost nothing…And a life with almost everything. Yet after experiencing both…They discovered an astonishing truth. Neither poverty…Nor luxury…Could give lasting happiness.

Only when their hearts turned towards God did they discover the joy that never faded.

What is the Bhagavatham teaching us?

It is not asking us to stop dreaming. It is not asking us to give up our family. Or our career. Or life’s blessings. It is simply reminding us not to expect these blessings to do what they were never created to do.

A promotion can make us happy. A beautiful home can make us happy. A loving family can make us happy. But none of them can keep us happy forever.

That is why, after every achievement, the mind quietly begins searching for the next one. Because what we truly long for is not another achievement…We long for a happiness that never leaves.

Thought for the Day

There is nothing wrong with chasing your dreams.

But remember…

You are not really chasing a promotion.Or a bigger house. Or greater success. You are chasing the lasting happiness you hope they will bring.

The Bhagavatham gently reminds us…

Lasting happiness is not waiting at the end of the next achievement. It begins the day God becomes the centre of our life. Then every blessing becomes something to enjoy…

But none of them becomes something we depend upon to feel complete.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

Ps: The epic series brings simple life lessons from Srimad Bhagavatham to help us reflect on our day-to-day life .

@@ 197 | Epic Series - 68 | When We Feel Too Broken for God | 
In our yesterday’s reflection, King Parikshit received the most frightening news of his life.

”You have only seven days left.”

Perhaps many of us have never heard those exact words. But almost all of us have faced moments that shook our world.

A medical report….,A failed business….A broken relationship……A mistake that still haunts us…..Or simply… a season in life where everything seems to be going wrong.

Something strange happens during such moments. We don’t just lose hope. We also lose the courage to look towards God.

A quiet voice inside says,

“Maybe I brought this upon myself.”

“Let me first fix my life.”

“Let me become a better person.”

“Only then will I deserve God’s help.”

Have you noticed? We often feel we must become clean before we can go near God.

Thousands of years ago, before answering King Parikshit’s question about life and death, Sage Sukadeva addressed this hidden fear first. He began the Bhagavatham with an unusual story.

The story of Varaha Avatar

The Earth had been dragged deep into the dark waters by the demon Hiranyaksha. She could not free herself. The deeper she sank, the more helpless she became. Now comes the question.

Why did Lord Vishnu choose to appear as a boar?

Could He not have come as a king? Or as a radiant celestial being?

The Bhagavatham is teaching us something extraordinary. A boar never hesitates to enter mud. It does not stand at the edge worrying about getting dirty. It dives straight in. That is exactly what the Lord did.

He did not wait for the Earth to come out of the mud. He did not ask her to become clean first. He Himself entered the darkness. He fought the demon. He gently lifted the Earth on His tusks and restored her to safety.

What an answer to our own life!

The Lord became a boar because our rescue mattered more to Him than His comfort. He has no fear of our failures. No disgust for our mistakes. No hesitation because our life is in a mess.

When everyone else steps back…

He steps in. Perhaps that is why Sage Sukadeva began the Bhagavatham with Varaha. Before teaching King Parikshit how to face death…He first wanted him to know this:

“There is no place where God’s grace cannot reach.”

Thought for the Day

Never think you must become worthy before seeking God.

If He was willing to become a boar just to enter the mud and lift the Earth…

He will certainly enter the darkest moments of your life to lift you too.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 196 | Epic Series - 67 | When Life Suddenly Changes | 
It was supposed to be just another health check. The doctor looked at the reports… paused for a few moments… and softly said,

“I am sorry… the tests don’t look good. We need to discuss the next steps.”

The drive back home was unusually silent. Neither husband nor wife knew what to say.

The television was on…But nobody watched it. Dinner was served…But nobody was hungry. The grandchildren came running with their stories from school. Everyone smiled for a moment…But deep inside, the mind was somewhere else.

Late that night…

One lay awake staring at the ceiling. The other quietly turned to the other side… pretending to be asleep… so the tears would not be seen.

Only that morning, life had seemed so normal.Just one medical report…And suddenly, the mind was flooded with questions.

“Will I recover?”

“How much time do I have?”

“What will happen to my family?”

“Have I really lived my life well?”

Many of us may face such moments in one form or another.

Not only because of illness. Sometimes it is retirement. Sometimes the loss of a loved one. Sometimes an unexpected accident. Sometimes a sudden financial crisis. Life has a way of reminding us that nothing here is permanent.

Thousands of years ago, a great king faced exactly such a moment.

King Parikshit was a noble and righteous ruler. He was loved by his people and ruled with fairness. One day, while tired, thirsty and exhausted after a hunt, he unknowingly made a mistake. In a moment of weakness, he insulted a sage who was deep in meditation.

When the sage’s young son heard about it, he became angry and cursed the king.

”Within seven days, you will die from the bite of a serpent.”

Imagine hearing those words. Only seven days left. Parikshit had the power to fight back. He had soldiers, wealth, influence and the finest physicians. He could have searched every corner of the kingdom for a way to escape.

He could have blamed the sage. He could have spent his last seven days in fear and anxiety. But he did something that has inspired millions for thousands of years.

He accepted that death could not be avoided. He handed over his kingdom to his son. He walked to the banks of the holy Ganga. Great sages gathered around him.

Then Parikshit asked one question that became the very heart of Srimad Bhagavatham:

”What should a person do when he knows that life is coming to an end?”

For the next seven days, Sage Sukadeva narrated the Bhagavatham. Those seven days did not merely prepare Parikshit to face death. They helped him discover what it truly means to live.

Perhaps that is why Bhagavatham remains relevant even today. It reminds us that none of us knows whether we have seven days, seven years or seventy years. But all of us have today.

Thought for the day

We cannot choose how long we will live. But we can choose how we live from this day onward.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 195 | Epic Series - 66 | Who Am I Really Bargaining With? | 
A question from a reader:

“Whenever life becomes uncertain… I start making promises to God.

“If my son gets this job… I will walk barefoot to Tirupati.”

“If my wife’s surgery goes well… I will sponsor annadhanam.”

“If I get promoted… I will shave my head at Palani.”

For years, I thought this was devotion. But recently… one question disturbed me. Am I praying… or am I bargaining?

Let us enter another home. Lakshmi’s husband suffered a massive heart attack and was rushed to the ICU. Standing outside…helpless…she folded her hands and cried.

“God… if he comes home safely… I will offer all my gold bangles to You.”

By God’s grace…he recovered. Lakshmi happily fulfilled her vow. Life slowly became normal again.

Two years later… her only son met with a serious road accident. Once again , she stood before the same deity. This time she prayed,

“God… if my son survives… I’ll shave my head.”

As soon as she said those words…she became silent. A thought came to her.

“Last time… I offered my bangles.”

“Today… I am offering my hair.”

“If another problem comes tomorrow… what will I offer then?”

And then…a question she had never asked before.

“Have I slowly started believing that I must give God something… before He helps me?”

She stood there quietly.For the first time…she asked for nothing. She simply said,

“God… I don’t know what You have planned. But please… don’t let my fear decide how I pray.”

She walked out of the temple. Her problem had not disappeared. But something inside her had. Her fear.

Now let us go to the Mahabharata.

When Draupadi was humiliated in the royal court…she first held on tightly to her saree with one hand…and called Krishna with the other. She was still trying to save herself.

Still trying to hold on. The miracle did not happen then. Only when she let go completely…raising both hands in total surrender…did Krishna’s endless grace flow. What changed? Not Krishna. Her surrender became complete.

The Insight

Perhaps…we have misunderstood many of our vows. God never said,

“Give Me this… then I will help you.”

It is our frightened mind that says,

“Maybe I have not offered enough.”

Fear keeps increasing the offering. Faith keeps increasing the trust. A coconut…then a fast…then a pilgrimage…then gold…then our hair…

The offering keeps becoming bigger. But perhaps…God was never looking at the offering. He was looking at the heart.

Thought for the Day

There is nothing wrong in making a vow. But ask yourself one simple question.

Am I offering this because I love God…or because I am afraid God may not help me otherwise?

One comes from love. The other comes from fear.

And God has always responded…not to the size of the offering…but to the depth of our faith. That… is sacred.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

PS: The Epic Series connects our everyday struggles with timeless wisdom from our epics.

@@ 194 | Inside Us Series -36 | The Beautiful Burden of Each Other | 
The other day, a close friend looked exhausted. I asked him what happened.

He sighed. “My teenage son came home from a party at 2:00 AM. My wife and I spent half the night staring at the ceiling. The moment we heard the front door click shut, we finally fell asleep.”

The next morning he told his son how worried they had been.

The son looked puzzled. “But I was fine! Why didn’t you sleep? Nobody told me you were awake.”

My friend turned to me and asked, “Where did we go wrong?”

The Secret World of Gen Z

We see this in many homes today.

A relative arrives, settles into the living room, and starts chatting. Meanwhile, the children remain in their rooms, immersed in their screens.

It is easy to conclude, “This generation has no empathy.” But perhaps something else is happening.

Gen Z is not heartless. They are growing up in a completely different world. For them, the internet is not a tool, it is part of life itself. They are constantly managing friendships, conversations, trends, studies, and emotions across multiple digital spaces.

So when a guest arrives or a parent is worried, they may genuinely not notice. Their familiar defence, “But nobody told me!” is often not an excuse. It is simply their reality.

The Beautiful Puzzle of the Past

Thirty or forty years ago, life felt like one giant team sport. Homes were full of grandparents, cousins, neighbours, and unexpected visitors. In fact, neighbours often walked into each other’s homes so naturally that the doorbell sometimes felt optional.

Today, even if the neighbour lives twenty feet away, we first call on the intercom and ask, “Are you home?” before walking across. There is nothing wrong with that. Boundaries matter. Privacy matters.

But somewhere along the way, a habit quietly changed. Earlier, we lived together. Today, we coordinate together.

If a neighbour fell sick, someone appeared with soup. If there was a wedding, relatives arrived to help. If there was a crisis, people showed up before they were called.

Empathy was not taught. It was observed, practised, and absorbed.

The Cost of “My Life”

Modern life has given us wonderful gifts individuality, privacy, freedom, and choice. But every gift carries a price.

Sometimes we ask, “How are you?” while already looking at our phones, secretly hoping the answer is “Fine.”

We have become wonderfully connected and strangely unavailable at the same time.

In protecting ourselves from intrusion, we may have also reduced opportunities for connection.

A home can slowly become a place where people share an address, but not always their lives.

Moving Closer Again

We cannot turn back the clock, nor should we. But perhaps we can remember something timeless.

Human beings were never meant to be isolated islands. We are designed for connection. Maybe we don’t need grand lectures. Maybe we simply need to notice a little more.

Tonight, perhaps we can knock on that bedroom door, sit beside our child, look them in the eye, and ask, “How was your day?”

And while they answer, let us leave our own phones in the other room. Because true warmth does not require changing the world. It only requires helping one person feel seen.

And perhaps that is the beautiful burden and privilege we carry for one another.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 193 | Inside Us Series - 35 | The Pattaani That Exposed Me | 
The other day, I made a serious tactical mistake.

At around 4:30 PM, I quietly entered the kitchen like a secret agent on a classified mission. My target? One lonely pattaani (green pea) that had escaped from lunch.

Just as my fingers touched it, a voice floated in from the next room. “That is approximately 2 calories. Has it been logged?”

I froze. My wife was standing there with the calm confidence of a seasoned detective.

Welcome to my new life.

Recently, my better half, with great love and even greater determination, enrolled both of us under a fitness coach. Along with that came the HealthifyMe app, a smartwatch, calorie tracking, meal photos, and enough data analytics to run a small country.

Every meal must be photographed before consumption. Every step is counted. Every heartbeat is monitored.

I am convinced that if I merely think about a gulab jamun, the app sends an alert to my coach.

The other day, my watch vibrated because I had been sitting too long. Imagine that.

For 57 years, nobody objected to my sitting. Suddenly, a gadget on my wrist has become my mother.

Something strange has happened over the last few weeks.I no longer see food.I see numbers.

A idli is no longer a idli. It is 60 calories. A dosa is not breakfast. It is 120 calories. A spoon of molagaipodi is no longer a condiment. It is a financial liability.

Even when I pass a sweet shop, I don’t see sweets anymore. I see future gym sessions. But hidden behind all this comedy was an uncomfortable truth.

For years, I lived under the comforting illusion that a little extra here and a little extra there did not matter. One biscuit. One handful. One spoon. One small indulgence.

The problem is that life itself is built from “one small” choices. The body we have today is often the accumulated result of thousands of tiny decisions that seemed insignificant at the time. The app simply held up a mirror. It did not judge me.

It merely showed me the arithmetic I had been negotiating with for years. And arithmetic, unfortunately, is immune to excuses.

Then came the insight.

Most of us look at fitness as a punishment. We see the trainer as the villain and the dessert as the hero. But what if we have the story completely backwards?

What if every healthy choice is not a sacrifice, but an investment?

As we grow older, ageing is inevitable. But ageing gracefully is not automatic. The ability to walk without pain, climb stairs without effort, travel freely, think clearly, and remain independent is built one meal and one habit at a time.

Suddenly, fitness stopped feeling like deprivation. It started feeling like self-respect.

And for this realization, I owe a debt of gratitude to my wife. What I initially saw as surveillance, she saw as care. What I thought was a restriction was actually an invitation to take responsibility.

She was not trying to control my food. She was trying to protect my future. Sometimes, the people who love us see dangers that we are busy negotiating with.

And sometimes, their persistence becomes our blessing.

So yes, the pattaani remained uneaten. The calories remained unlogged. And I quietly walked away from the kitchen smiling.

Though I must confess…

I still suspect the app is watching me.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️😊

@@ 192 | Inside Us Series - 34 | The Blind Spot We All Have | 
Yesterday, I watched a young waiter carry a cup of hot coffee through a crowded cafe. He carefully placed it before a gentleman who was busy on an important phone call.

The gentleman picked up the coffee without looking at him. No smile. No eye contact. No acknowledgement. Just coffee received, and life moved on.

The waiter quietly walked away. The entire incident lasted barely five seconds. Yet it stayed with me much longer.

Have you noticed something about us?

We immediately notice when people fail to appreciate us. But we rarely notice how often we fail to appreciate others. If someone does something extraordinary for us, we remember it for years.

But the people who quietly make our lives easier every day slowly become invisible.

Sound familiar?

The meal appears on our table. We notice if the salt is slightly less. We rarely notice the love, effort, planning, cooking and cleaning that brought it there.

The driver gets us safely through traffic. We step out and walk away.

The security guard opens the gate every day. One day he is absent, and only then do we realise he existed.

Someone serves us. Someone supports us.Someone helps us. Someone cares for us. And after a while, we stop seeing them.

Not because we are bad people. Simply because we get used to their presence. And what we get used to, we stop noticing.

Perhaps that is our blind spot. We see the mistake. We miss the effort. We see what is missing. We miss what is being given.

A child shows a drawing. A spouse serves a meal. A colleague quietly helps. A worker does his job sincerely.

Most of them are not looking for rewards. They are simply hoping someone noticed. After all, is that not what every human heart wants?

To feel seen. To feel valued. To feel that their presence made a difference. Sometimes a single sentence can do that.

“Thank you for waiting.”

“Thank you for helping.”

“Thank you for taking care of this.”

“Thank you. I noticed.”

Such small words. Yet they can brighten an entire day. Maybe even an entire life. The truth is this. None of us walks alone. Behind every comfortable day stand countless invisible hands.

People whose names we may never know. People who spend a small piece of their life making ours easier.

The tragedy is not that they are invisible. The tragedy is that we often realise their value only after they are gone.

Let us not wait for absence to create appreciation. Let us not wait for loss to create gratitude.

The next time someone serves you a cup of coffee… Look up. Smile. And let them know they were seen.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️😊

@@ 191 | Epic Series - 65 | The Secret Charioteer | 
Yesterday, we spoke about placing our burdens down on the train of life. What a comforting thought that is.

For a few moments, we imagine setting aside the heavy suitcase of worries, fears, expectations, regrets, and unanswered questions. We breathe a little easier, our shoulders relax, and the heart feels lighter.

And then life does what life often does. The lights suddenly go out. The train is still moving, but now it is racing through darkness. The tracks ahead seem uncertain and questions begin to arise.

Am I on the right path? Am I making the right decisions? What if I make a mistake? What if I trust and things still go wrong? Without even realizing it, we reach for the suitcase again, not because the burden became lighter, but because uncertainty became heavier.

The human dilemma

We want surrender, but we also want guarantees. We want faith, but we also want visibility. We want to trust, but we are afraid of the dark. And this is where the role of a Guru is often misunderstood.

A Guru is not someone who takes away our freedom or asks us to stop thinking. Nor is a Guru someone who lives our life for us. A true Guru does something far more profound, they help us see what we cannot see by ourselves.

The Mahabharata gives us one of the most beautiful illustrations of this truth.

On the eve of the great war, Arjuna stood in his chariot facing the greatest crisis of his life. His mind was confused, his heart was torn, and his convictions had collapsed.

The mighty warrior who had conquered countless enemies could no longer conquer his own doubts. His bow slipped from his hands and, for perhaps the first time in his life, he did not know what was right.

That was the moment something extraordinary happened.

Arjuna did not discover Krishna that day. Krishna had always been there beside him, walking with him, protecting him, and silently guiding him. But Krishna became the Guru only when Arjuna finally admitted, “I am confused. I do not know. Please guide me.”

That moment changed everything. Not the battlefield, not the circumstances, and not the challenges before him. What changed was clarity. And when clarity changes, life changes.

Perhaps that is why a Guru is often compared to light. Light does not walk for us, carry us, or force us. It simply reveals. The path was always there, the obstacles were always there, and even the destination was always there. We simply could not see them clearly.

Many people ask, “Do I need a Guru?” Perhaps the better question is, “Am I willing to be guided when I can no longer see clearly?”

For every one of us eventually reaches moments when knowledge falls short, experience offers no answers, and the intellect alone cannot untangle the knots of the heart.

In those moments, grace often arrives in the form of guidance. Sometimes through a person, sometimes through a scripture, sometimes through a life-changing conversation, and sometimes through a single sentence that enters the heart at exactly the right time.

The form may vary, but the light is the same.

And here is the beautiful secret. You do not have to anxiously search for that light. Life has a mysterious way of bringing the right guidance when the seeker is genuinely ready.

Just as Krishna was already seated beside Arjuna long before the Gita was spoken, the guidance meant for us is often much closer than we imagine.

Perhaps the real journey is not finding the Guru. Perhaps the real journey is becoming ready to listen.

Are we?

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 190 | Epic Series - 64 | Beyond the To-Do List | 
In the last reflection, we saw that peace does not come from finishing every task on our list. Peace comes when we are fully present in the moment.

But then a question arises. How do we do that? Our mind is always running. Even while eating, we think about work. Even while talking to family, we think about tomorrow. Even while praying, we think about our problems.

So how do we slow down?

The answer is a beautiful word from our scriptures: Sharanagathi - Surrender.

Many people misunderstand surrender. They think surrender means giving up. It does not. Surrender is not weakness. Surrender is trust.

Imagine you are travelling in a train with a heavy suitcase. Would you keep the suitcase on your head for the entire journey?

Of course not.

You place it on the floor. The train is carrying both you and your luggage. Yet this is exactly what many of us do in life.

The train of life is moving. God is carrying us. But we still keep carrying the weight of yesterday’s regrets and tomorrow’s worries.

We keep asking:

“What will happen?”

“Will everything be okay?”

“How will I manage?”

Surrender simply means putting the luggage down. Do your best. Then leave the rest.

The Ramayana gives us a beautiful example.

When Vibhishana left Lanka, he left behind everything he knew : his home, his family, his position and his comfort. He came to Sri Rama with empty hands. He did not come with demands. He did not come with a plan. He simply fell at Rama’s feet and said: “I am yours.” That is surrender.

In that moment, all his fear disappeared. The future was no longer his burden. It became Rama’s responsibility.

We may not be Vibhishana. But every day gives us a chance to practice the same surrender.

When you drink your morning tea, just drink your tea. Don’t drink tomorrow’s worries with it.

When your child speaks to you, just listen. That moment will never come back.

When you pray, pray. When you walk, walk. When you eat, eat.

Life becomes beautiful when we stop living everywhere except where we are. Surrender does not mean we stop planning. It simply means we stop worrying. We do what we can. We trust God with what we cannot.

And slowly, the mind becomes lighter. The heart becomes calmer. The present moment becomes sweeter.

Thought for the Day

Surrender is not saying, “I give up.” It is saying, “I trust.”

Put down your mental luggage for a few minutes today. Trust the One who is carrying the train.

You may discover that the peace you were searching for was already waiting for you in this very moment

Dhanyosmi 🙏🙇‍♂️

@@ 189 | Epic Series - 63 | The Spiritual Checklist | 
In the last reflection, we spoke about Sabari. She never worried whether she had enough money, comfort, or preparation.

She had nothing perfect in her life. But she had something deeper : pure love and full presence. She didn’t follow a rulebook. She didn’t follow a system . She didn’t do things “correctly” by any formal standard.

She simply picked fruits, tasted them with love, and waited. That is all. And because her heart was completely there… God came to her.

Today’s real life

We are also sincere people. Every day , We do morning puja, We light lamps properly, We go to temples, We chant slokas, We listen to spiritual talks, we learn and sing bhajans .

We do everything we are supposed to do. We complete our list. We tick all the boxes.

But still…

somehow the mind is not peaceful. Something inside still feels incomplete.

The quiet truth

It is not because we are doing something wrong. It is because something important is missing. We have learned the steps of devotion…but we are missing the feeling of devotion.

What actually happens inside us

Every morning, during puja : Hands are offering flowers, Mouth is chanting mantras. But the mind is somewhere else. Thinking about work. Thinking about money. Thinking about family. Thinking about problems. Sometimes even replaying old conversations.

So the puja finishes.Everything is done “properly”. But inside, we feel the same as before. Because we were not fully there.

A gentle realization

We often treat devotion like an exam: “If I complete everything correctly, I will get peace.”

But peace does not come from completing steps. It comes from being present while doing them. God is not watching our checklist. He is simply waiting for our attention.

The simple shift

We don’t need to change our life. We don’t need to add more rituals. We don’t need to learn anything new. Only one small shift is needed:

From “I am doing puja” to “I am here in this moment.”

Thought for the day

We are not lacking devotion. We are only distracted while being devoted.

Even one moment of full presence is enough to change the feeling of the entire day.

Not perfection. Just presence.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 188 | Epic Series - 62 | Will It Be Enough? | 
In our last reflection, we spoke about the grace of stepping back and letting the next generation take the wheel.

But there is a silent fear that often prevents us from doing so. A question that visits almost everyone at some stage of life:

How much is enough for me to retire?

Will what I have saved be sufficient for the rest of my life?

The numbers may differ from person to person, but the anxiety is remarkably similar. We keep calculating. We keep planning. We keep wondering whether our basket is full enough.

This reminded me of a touching episode from the Ramayana.

For many years, Sabari lived alone in a small hut deep in the forest. Her guru had told her that one day, Lord Rama would visit her. She had no wealth, no possessions worth mentioning, and no assurance about her future.

Yet every morning she would gather wild berries and keep them ready for Rama. Fearing that some might be sour, she would taste each one and set aside only the sweet berries.

Years passed.

Her hair turned grey. Her body became weak. Still, she waited. And one day, Rama arrived. The Lord who could have chosen the palaces of kings instead chose the humble hut of Sabari. With tears of joy, she offered her simple basket of berries. Rama accepted them with delight and love.

Whenever I reflect on Sabari, one thought touches me deeply.

By worldly standards, she had very little. Yet she never spent her life worrying about what she lacked. She focused on what she could offer. Her security came not from the size of her possessions, but from the depth of her faith.

Perhaps that is a lesson many of us need today.

Planning for the future is wise. Saving for retirement is responsible. But beyond a point, our worry is no longer about money. It is about trust.

We forget that the same grace that carried us through childhood, education, career, marriage, parenthood, successes and setbacks has not suddenly disappeared. As we grow older, life often simplifies itself. Our needs become fewer.

What we seek is not more possessions, but more peace. Not a larger house, but a calmer mind. Not greater wealth, but greater contentment.

Thought for the Day

The question is not whether we will ever have “enough.”

The deeper question is whether we can recognize when we already do.

True security comes less from the size of our savings and more from the confidence that the same Divine hand that brought us this far will continue to guide the journey ahead.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

PS: The same principle applies to our children and careers. We often wonder whether we have done enough, given enough, or achieved enough. If we have acted with sincerity and given our best, perhaps it is time to stop counting and start trusting.

@@ 187 | Epic Series - 61 | Learning to Let Go | 
Last week we reflected on the blessing of having someone walk beside us through life’s journey.

Today, I would like to reflect on another phase of life that many of us eventually encounter.

There comes a time when the children who once held our fingers begin to walk on their own.

For years, parents are at the centre of the family. They make decisions, solve problems, provide support and quietly carry responsibilities that nobody else even notices. Then, almost without warning, life changes.

The children grow up. They start earning. They make their own choices. They build families of their own. And somewhere deep within, a question can arise, “Do they still need me the way they once did?”

This reminded me of a touching incident from the Mahabharata.

After the great war, Dhritarashtra and Gandhari had lost everything. Their sons were gone. Their kingdom was gone. They were living in Yudhishthira’s palace, dependent on the very people who had defeated them. The old king found it difficult to let go of the past.

One day, Vidura came to him and spoke with great compassion. He reminded Dhritarashtra that every stage of life has its own purpose. There comes a time when one must stop looking backward and start looking inward. The words were difficult to hear, but they carried wisdom.

That very night, Dhritarashtra and Gandhari quietly left the palace and departed for the forest to spend the rest of their lives in spiritual pursuit.

Whenever I think about this episode, one lesson stands out. Much of life’s suffering comes not from change itself, but from our resistance to change.

A teacher feels happy when the student no longer needs constant guidance.

A gardener rejoices when the sapling becomes a strong tree.

A bird does not feel rejected when its young ones leave the nest.

In the same way, when our children become independent, it is not a sign that we have become less important. It is a sign that we have fulfilled our responsibility well.

Perhaps the role of parents changes with time. In one phase, we lead. In another phase, we support. And eventually, we bless. The wisdom of growing older lies in knowing when to make that transition gracefully.

Thought for the Day

One of life’s greatest joys is not in being needed forever.

It is in seeing those we nurtured stand confidently on their own feet and knowing that we played a small part in helping them get there.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

PS : The same lesson applies in our careers. When younger colleagues step up and take centre stage, we may sometimes feel less relevant. But their growth is often a reflection of the experience, guidance, and foundation we helped build.

@@ 186 | Epic Series -60 | Walking Together | 
Today happens to be a special day for me personally.

By Bhagavan’s grace, my wife and I complete another year of our life together.

As I sat quietly and looked back, I felt grateful. Not for the number of years that have passed, but for the person who walked with me through those years.

One thing I have realized is that a spouse is much more than a husband or wife. They become a friend when we need someone to talk to. A guide when we are confused. A teacher when we need to learn. A critic when we make mistakes.

And sometimes, they become our memory card, reminding us of all the things we forgot. Most importantly, they become someone who stands by us when life is not going our way.

Thinking about this reminded me of a beautiful incident from the Mahabharata.

During their exile, the Pandavas went through many difficulties. Among them, Draupadi had every reason to be upset. She had lost her comforts, her kingdom, and had suffered great humiliation.

The main reason for all this was Yudhishthira’s mistake at the gambling table. Yet Draupadi did not walk away. She questioned him. She expressed her pain. She disagreed with him. But she stayed with the family and shared their hardships.

At the same time, Yudhishthira never ignored her pain. He knew he had made mistakes. He accepted the consequences and continued to respect her feelings.

Whenever I think about this episode, one lesson stands out. Relationships are not tested when everything is going well. Anyone can smile and walk together when life is comfortable.

The real test comes when problems arrive. When health fails. When money becomes tight. When plans do not work out. When life takes an unexpected turn.

In every long relationship, there will be times when one person carries more of the burden. One adjusts more. One sacrifices more. One remains patient while the other struggles. And later, the roles may reverse.

The problem begins when we start keeping score.

“I did more.”

“I sacrificed more.”

“I adjusted more.”

The moment we start counting, happiness quietly starts leaving. Strong relationships are built on gratitude, not accounting. They grow when we appreciate the many small things that the other person does every day.

A kind word.

A caring phone call.

A cup of coffee.

A silent understanding.

A helping hand when we need it most.

As the years go by, one realizes that the greatest blessing in a relationship is not perfection. It is presence.

To know that there is someone who continues to stand beside us through good times and bad. That itself is a great blessing.

On this special day, my heart is filled with gratitude to Bhagavan and to my wife, who has walked beside me through every season of life as a friend, guide, supporter and companion.

Thought for the Day

A relationship is not successful because two people stayed together for many years.

It is successful because they stayed together through many ups and downs without letting go of each other.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 185 | Epic Series - 59 | When Success Quietly Turns Into Ego | 
One of the biggest challenges in life is that we can easily notice ego in other people, but rarely in ourselves.

We can immediately say,

“He has become arrogant.”

“She doesn’t listen to anyone.”

“He thinks he knows everything.”

But when the same thing starts happening within us, we often don’t notice it. That is because ego never announces its arrival. It enters quietly.

There is a beautiful story from the Bhagavatam that teaches this lesson.

Indra, the king of the Devas, was powerful, respected and worshipped by everyone. Over time, he became accustomed to the praise and honour he received.

Slowly, without realizing it, he began to feel that he deserved all of it.

Then one day, the people of Gokulam followed Krishna’s advice and worshipped Govardhana Hill instead of performing their usual worship for Indra. Indra was deeply offended. His pride was hurt.

In anger, he unleashed heavy rains and powerful storms upon Gokulam. The rains continued relentlessly. The people were frightened and helpless.

Krishna simply lifted Govardhana Hill and sheltered everyone beneath it. At that moment, Indra understood a painful truth. The problem was never the people of Gokulam. The problem was his own ego.

Success had entered his head. His position had become bigger than his humility. His power had become bigger than his wisdom. His achievements had become bigger than his gratitude. And that is where his downfall began.

If we are honest, this does not happen only to Indra. It happens to many of us.

A successful businessman may start believing, “Without me, this company cannot run.”

A parent may think, “Everything in this family happens because of me.”

A knowledgeable person may feel, “I already know enough.”

A senior employee may assume, “Nobody can teach me anything new.”

The danger is not these thoughts themselves. The danger is what follows. We stop listening. We stop learning. We stop appreciating others. We stop noticing our own mistakes. That is often the first sign that ego has quietly entered our lives.

What makes ego dangerous is not that it makes us look bad. It makes us blind. Blind to our weaknesses. Blind to our mistakes. Blind to the contributions of others. And when we stop seeing our mistakes, we stop growing.

So how do we protect ourselves from ego?

Not by thinking less of ourselves. But by remembering that there is always more to learn.

A few simple questions can help:

“Who helped me reach where I am today?”

“What can I still learn from others?”

“When was the last time I accepted a mistake without defending myself?”

Gratitude weakens ego. Listening weakens ego. Learning weakens ego.

The day we believe we have nothing more to learn, ego has already taken charge. This is what makes Indra’s story so inspiring. His greatness did not lie in never making a mistake.

His greatness lay in recognizing it, surrendering to Krishna, and correcting himself.

The same applies to us. Ego is not our biggest problem. Refusing to recognize it is. The wisest people are usually the most humble. The more successful they become, the more grateful they become. Because they understand that no success is achieved alone.

Behind every achievement stand parents, teachers, mentors, colleagues, friends, well-wishers and, above all, Bhagavan’s grace.

Thought for the Day

The day we stop learning, ego starts growing. The day we start learning again, wisdom returns.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

*P.S.: The “Epic” series draws wisdom from our ancient scriptures and connects it to situations we encounter in everyday life

@@ 184 | Epic Series - 58 | The Question Nobody Could Answer | 
Most people are carrying some worry.

“What if I lose my job?”

“What if my health becomes worse?”

“What if my children don’t do well?”

“What if my savings are not enough?”

“What if something goes wrong tomorrow?”

The mind keeps creating fears about a future that has not yet arrived.

There is a beautiful incident in the Mahabharata.

One day, the Pandavas were wandering in the forest. They became very thirsty. One by one, four brothers went in search of water. They found a beautiful lake.

But a mysterious voice warned them: “Answer my questions before drinking the water.”

Ignoring the warning, they drank the water and fell unconscious. Finally, Yudhishthira arrived. He listened. The voice belonged to a Yaksha.

The Yaksha asked many questions. One question was very simple. Yet it is one of the deepest questions ever asked.

“What is the greatest wonder in this world?”

Yudhishthira replied: “Every day people see others dying. Yet each person believes he himself will live forever.” The Yaksha was pleased. The answer was correct.

What touches me most is this

We all know life is uncertain. We know nothing in this world is permanent. We see changes happening around us every day.

Yet we spend so much energy worrying about things that may never happen.

A person worries about retirement twenty years away.

Another worries about a disease he does not have.

Someone worries about losing respect.

Someone worries about losing money.

Someone worries about what others think.

The mind keeps running into an imaginary future. And in the process, it misses today’s peace. The truth is that nobody has control over tomorrow. Not kings. Not billionaires. Not scholars. Not ordinary people.

Then what do we actually control?

Only today’s actions. Today’s effort. Today’s attitude. Today’s prayer. Today’s kindness.

Yudhishthira understood something that many of us forget. Life was never meant to be controlled. It was meant to be lived.

When we keep staring at tomorrow, we stop seeing the blessings of today. The future will arrive when it arrives. Until then, today’s responsibilities are enough.

Thought for the Day

Most of our fears come from trying to control a tomorrow that has not yet come.

Peace begins when we do our best today and leave tomorrow to God.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 183 | Epic series -57 | The Boy Nobody Noticed | 
There is a beautiful story in Srimad Bhagavatam that many of us may have heard, but few pause to reflect upon deeply.

Long before he became the great sage Narada, he was just an ordinary little boy. No royal birth. No famous family. No special education. In fact, the Bhagavatam says he was the son of a maid servant.

Every year, during the rainy season, a group of great saints stayed near his village.

The little boy quietly watched them. He would bring water. Clean their vessels. Serve them without being asked.

Most importantly, he simply sat near them and listened. The sages probably did not even realise the impact they were having on him.

To them, he was just a small boy helping around. To him, they were changing his life.

One day, the saints left. The boy returned to his ordinary life. Then tragedy struck. His mother died unexpectedly. In a single moment, the only person he had in the world was gone.

Imagine the loneliness.No relatives. No support system. No future plan. No shoulder to cry on. Just a young boy standing alone before life.

Yet something remarkable happened. Instead of becoming bitter, he remembered the saints. He remembered their words. He remembered their example. The kindness they had shown. The values they had lived. The seeds they had unknowingly planted in his heart.

Those memories became his strength. That forgotten little boy eventually became Narada Maharishi , one of the greatest devotees known to our scriptures.

What touches me the most is this.

The sages never knew what they had accomplished. They probably left thinking they had merely spent a few months in a village. But their influence continued long after they had gone.

In our daily life, something similar happens all the time.

Many parents worry, “My children are not listening.”

Teachers wonder, “Are my students learning anything?”

Managers feel, “My team doesn’t value what I do.”

Grandparents think, “The younger generation is not interested.”

But human hearts work differently. People may forget our advice. They may forget our lectures. They may even forget our words.

But they rarely forget how we made them feel.

Years later, a son may remember how calmly his father handled a crisis.

A daughter may remember the sacrifices her mother made silently.

An employee may remember a manager who trusted him when nobody else did.

A student may remember a teacher who believed in her when she doubted herself.

At the time, these moments seem insignificant. Yet they quietly shape lives. Just like the saints who never knew they were shaping Narada.

The truth is that we often underestimate our influence. We think impact comes from grand speeches, major achievements or dramatic actions.

But many lives are transformed through small acts of kindness, patience, integrity and compassion. Someone is always watching. Someone is always learning. Someone is always carrying a memory of us.

The question is, What memory are we leaving behind?

Thought for the Day

The greatest influence we have on others is often the influence we never realise we had.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

P.S.: The “Epic” series draws wisdom from our ancient scriptures to reflect on our day-to-day life.

@@ 182 |  | The Wedding Ends in a Day. The Lessons Last for Life. | 
The Wedding Ends in a Day. The Lessons Last for Life.

Recently we attended a beautiful traditional wedding.

The Vedic rituals were profound. The sacred fire, the mantras, the blessings from elders, the Saptapadi - everything reminded us that marriage is not just a contract but a sacred journey.

But as I watched the wedding unfold, another thought crossed my mind.

What are we teaching our children through weddings?

Most parents today tell their daughters:

"Study well."

"Build your career."

"Stand on your own feet."

"You are equal to anyone."

And rightly so. But then comes the wedding.

Suddenly the same daughter sees her parents in a completely different role.

The bride's family is worried whether every guest is comfortable. They are checking if the food is perfect. They are apologising if the coffee is slightly cold.

They are anxious that every expectation of the groom's family must be met.

The groom's family may not even be demanding any of this. But the social script has already been written. Everyone simply plays their assigned role.

Now imagine what goes through the mind of a young girl. She may not say anything.

But she notices everything.

She wonders:

"If we are all equal, why are my parents carrying so much of the burden?"

At the same time, what is the son learning?

We tell him:

"Respect women."

"Treat your wife as an equal partner."

But during the wedding he sometimes sees one family constantly serving and another family constantly receiving.

Again, nobody intends harm. But children learn more from what they observe than from what they are told.

This creates a strange contradiction.

Our words teach equality. Our actions sometimes teach hierarchy.

A humorous example comes to mind.

Before marriage, both the bride and groom are called "modern youngsters."

After marriage, the bride is expected to become traditional in one day, while the groom is allowed to remain modern!

No training programme in the world can achieve such a transformation overnight.

The real impact appears later. The wedding gets over. The daughter enters her new home.

The mother-in-law carries expectations shaped by her generation. The daughter-in-law carries memories shaped by hers.

One is thinking:

"This is how we have always done things."

The other is thinking:

"Why should things be done this way?"

Neither is completely wrong. Neither is completely right.

Yet friction begins.

Interestingly, many family conflicts do not start after marriage.

The seeds are often planted during the wedding itself.

The daughter remembers what she saw her parents go through. The son remembers what society silently rewarded.

The parents wonder why the next generation thinks differently. The answer may be hiding in plain sight.

Children are always watching.

Every ritual teaches something. Every custom teaches something. Every gesture teaches something.

The question is:

Are we teaching dignity for all, or importance for some?

Traditions are precious and should be preserved.

But every generation also has a responsibility to separate sacred values from outdated social habits.

A wedding should leave behind blessings, gratitude and mutual respect.

Not invisible scars. Not silent resentment. Not a feeling that one family was above the other.

When two families meet as equals, the marriage starts with joy.

When one side feels lesser, the marriage starts with baggage.

And baggage is rarely listed in the wedding invitation.

The best inheritance we can give our children is not wealth, property or jewellery.

It is the example of treating every human being with dignity and respect.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️😊

@@ 181 | Inside us series -34 | How to Share the Taste of Faith | 
Last week we had a bhajan concert organised for children and an elderly gentleman spoke to me with sadness.

He said, “Every morning I sit in my puja room and pray. It gives me so much peace. But my 22-year-old grandson is not interested at all. If I ask him to pray, he asks, ‘Why should I do it? What is the proof?’ .

Sometimes I feel our tradition may stop with my generation.”

Many parents and grandparents feel the same today.

Long ago, Nammazhwar taught that we should involve our family in spiritual life so that devotion continues from one generation to the next.

Sri Ramanujacharya also encouraged his followers to keep this beautiful tradition alive and pass it on to others.

But today many families are facing a challenge. The younger generation is not against spirituality. They are against doing things without understanding them.

Let us look at a simple conversation.

Father: “Tomorrow is a special day. Wake up at 5 AM and join me for prayers.”

Son: “Why 5 AM? I have work tomorrow. Can I pray later?”

Father: “Don’t ask questions. This is what our ancestors did.”

Son: “That doesn’t explain why.”

And the conversation ends. The father feels hurt. The son feels forced. Both are unhappy.

The father’s intention is good. He wants to share something valuable. But the child sees only rules, not the beauty behind them.

Today, simply saying “Do this because elders said so” is often not enough. Children want to understand. And that is okay.

Real spirituality is not about fear. It is about peace, strength, love and wisdom.

So how can we help youngsters develop interest?

First, explain the benefit.

Instead of saying: “Just chant because it is good.”

Try saying: “When your mind is stressed, chanting can help you become calm and focused. Try it for a few minutes and see how you feel.”

Experience teaches better than lectures.

Second, share stories and feelings.

Spirituality is not only rituals. It is also love, kindness, gratitude and courage.

Tell children how prayer helped you during difficult times. Tell them inspiring stories of saints and great devotees. Stories touch the heart more than instructions.

Third, be an example.

This is the most important one.

Children watch us carefully.

If we pray every day but are always angry, worried or fighting, they may wonder:

“What is the use of all this prayer?”

But if they see that spirituality makes us peaceful, patient and caring, they become curious.

They start asking:

“What gives my parents this strength?”

That curiosity is the beginning of faith. We cannot force a plant to grow by pulling it. We can only give it water, sunlight and care.

In the same way, we cannot force spirituality into our children. We can only create an environment where it grows naturally.

Let us give them understanding instead of fear.

Let us give them experience instead of pressure.

Let us give them love instead of lectures.

When spirituality is shared with warmth and wisdom, the next generation will not reject it. They will embrace it happily and carry it forward.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️😊

@@ 180 | Inside Us Series - 33 | The Small Thought That Hijacks Our Happiness | 
One uncle recently told me something very honestly.

He said: “Sir… my body is perfectly fine till 10 AM. Then suddenly one small pain comes near my chest…

Immediately my mind opens Google, writes my will, conducts my funeral and sends me to heaven before lunch.” We both laughed.

But quietly… this is the daily life of millions of people.

One small body sensation…

One delayed phone call…

One unusual facial expression from somebody…

One medical test…

One small mistake…

And suddenly inside the mind:

“What if something is wrong?”

“What if something bad happens?”

“What if my life gets spoiled?”

Most people think anxiety comes from situations. Actually, many times anxiety comes from the story we secretly create inside our own head.

Same event.

Different mind.

Different suffering.

One person gets headache: “Maybe I didn’t sleep properly.”

Another person:

“This is how brain tumour starts.”

Within 5 minutes, heartbeat increases…

Hands sweat…

Mind panics…

Sleep disappears…

Family also gets tension.

See what happened?

The headache did not create suffering first. The thought about the headache created suffering.

Our mind is like that one overacting relative in every family.

If somebody does not pick up the phone:

Normal mind:

“Maybe they are busy.”

Anxious mind:

“Something terrible has happened.”

If husband says:

“We need to talk tonight.”

Peaceful mind:

“Okay.”

Anxious mind:

“Marriage is over. Property division has started.”

Poor fellow husband only wanted to discuss electricity bill.

The problem is…

Many people do not even realise thoughts are silently controlling their emotions and behaviour the whole day.

Thought becomes fear.

Fear becomes tension.

Tension changes behaviour.

Behaviour creates more problems.

Then the mind says:

“See? I told you life is dangerous.”

This cycle keeps running automatically. Slowly happiness gets kidnapped by imaginary disasters that never even happened.

And the saddest part?

The body starts suffering for problems created mainly inside the mind.

One doctor beautifully said:

“Many people are not dying from disease. They are dying daily from fear of disease.”

So what can we do?

First , stop believing every thought immediately. Not every thought is truth. Mind is also a machine. Sometimes it gives wrong notifications.

Just because your mind says:

“Something terrible will happen…”, does not mean it is a prediction from God. It is only one thought passing through the brain.

Second, ask one simple question:

“What proof do I actually have right now?”

Not:

“What if?”

Only:

“What is the reality now?”

This one question alone can calm half the panic in life.

Third , do not feed fear continuously.

Some people check BP 14 times a day .Google symptoms every hour. Read hospital stories in WhatsApp groups.

Then ask:

“Why am I anxious?”

That is like pouring petrol and asking why fire is increasing.

And finally…

Learn to sit with discomfort without immediately creating disaster stories.

Every small pain is not danger.

Every silence is not rejection.

Every uncertainty is not catastrophe.

Many times life is simply moving normally. The mind is the one doing cinema shooting in the background.

Real peace begins when we slowly learn this powerful truth:

“Thoughts are not commands.

Thoughts are not always facts.

And I do not have to obey every fear my mind produces”

The day a person understands this deeply… Half the unnecessary suffering inside life quietly reduces.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️😊

@@ 179 | Inside Us Series - 32 | The Weekly Phone Call and the Empty House | 
Recently, a reader from abroad sent me a deeply emotional message.

He wrote:

“We call our parents in India every single Sunday without fail. We take care of all their needs proactively and we ensure they live with absolute comfort . We keep asking them to come and stay with us in America. But somehow… every phone call ends with sadness.”

Then he wrote one line that quietly broke my heart:

“Every time we disconnect the call, my parents softly say ,’The house feels very empty after you all left…’”

Today, this silent emotional pain is entering thousands of homes across the world.

Parents in India feel emotionally lonely. Children abroad quietly carry guilt. And the most painful part is…

Both sides genuinely love each other deeply. This is not a story about bad children. This is not a story about unreasonable parents.

This is a story about two generations silently struggling inside completely different worlds.

For many elderly parents, old age slowly changes life in ways younger people often cannot fully understand.

Their world becomes smaller. Friends stop visiting. Festivals become quieter. The body slows down. The house suddenly feels too big and too silent.

Earlier, life was filled with movement. Children running around……Guests coming home……Daily responsibilities

….Arguments….Laughter…..Noise.

Now entire days pass quietly. And what many parents miss is not luxury or financial support.

They miss emotional presence. They miss random conversations. They miss hearing another human being moving around inside the house. They miss feeling emotionally important in somebody’s daily life.

Sometimes loneliness is not about being physically alone. It is about feeling emotionally forgotten.

At the same time, children living abroad are also carrying invisible emotional burdens.

From outside, foreign life may look glamorous. But inside, many families are simply surviving under constant pressure.

Both husband and wife work endlessly. There is no support system nearby. …Mortgage pressure…..Career anxiety…..Visa uncertainty.

Teenage children slowly growing up with different cultures, values and emotional worlds.

And somewhere deep inside, many middle-aged parents abroad are themselves silently afraid: “Will my own children become emotionally distant from me one day?”

So now, one generation feels emotionally left behind by distance.

Another generation feels crushed by responsibility.

And both sides keep waiting for the other side to understand their pain first.

But healing begins only when both generations soften a little inside.

Senior citizens must slowly understand one difficult truth:

Your children did not leave because they stopped loving you.

They left searching for opportunity, stability and a future they believed would make you proud.

Please do not make guilt the main bridge between you and your children.

Love cannot breathe properly inside constant emotional guilt.

And children living abroad must also understand something important:

Weekly phone calls are not the same as emotional presence.

Parents do not always need solutions.

Sometimes they simply want involvement.

Not only: “How are you?”

But:

“What did you cook today?”

“Who came home?”

“What are you planning tomorrow?”

“What made you smile today?”

Old age does not only need care.

It needs emotional participation.

And another difficult truth:

Children abroad must stop expecting elderly parents to emotionally adjust overnight to silence, loneliness, video calls and emotionally empty homes.

Similarly, parents must also stop expecting their children to recreate the old Indian joint-family life inside modern global struggles.

The truth is…

Both generations are grieving the loss of a world that no longer exists.

And maybe healing does not come by proving who sacrificed more.

Maybe healing starts when both sides become gentler with each other.

Less judgment……Less emotional accounting…..More patience…..,More acceptance.

….More involvement.

Because finally, every parent only wants to feel:

“I still matter in my children’s life.”

And every child silently wants to hear:

“You are not failing us.”

Sometimes families do not heal through grand sacrifices.

They heal through small moments of emotional warmth repeated consistently over time.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️😊

@@ 178 | Inside Us Series -31 | When Children Choose a Life the Parents Never Imagined | 
One father told me recently:

“My son says he is happy. I smile and tell him I am happy too. But inside… something is still struggling to accept it.”

Today, many parents are silently going through this emotional conflict.

Their children choose someone they love beyond caste, religion, language, nationality… sometimes even beyond traditional ideas of gender and relationships.

Outside, many parents try to appear modern, calm and supportive.

They attend the wedding. Smile in photographs. Tell relatives, “Children’s happiness is important.”

But deep inside, there may still be confusion, fear, grief or emotional shock. And this does not automatically make them bad parents.

Because parents are not only reacting to one marriage. They are reacting to the collapse of an entire picture they carried in their mind for decades.

Since childhood, many parents unknowingly imagine their child’s future.

They imagine:

“This is how the wedding will happen.”

“These are the traditions we will follow.”

“This is the type of family they will marry into.”

That mental picture becomes emotionally attached to their identity, culture and dreams. Then suddenly, life moves in a completely different direction.

And inside the parent, two emotions start fighting each other.

One side says:

“This is my child. I love them more than anything.”

The other side says:

“This is not the life I imagined. Why is my heart unable to fully accept it?”

This creates silent guilt. Many parents then start pretending.

They force themselves to look cheerful. But inside, they feel lonely, judged, confused or emotionally disconnected.

Sometimes they even feel ashamed for struggling internally because society says:

“If you truly love your child, you should accept everything immediately.”

But human emotions are not like switches. The mind may understand faster than the heart. Real acceptance sometimes takes time. And that is okay.

But here is the danger:

If parents suppress everything silently without emotionally processing it, the pain slowly becomes distance.

Conversations become artificial. Warmth reduces.Both sides start feeling misunderstood.

So how should parents deal with this inner conflict?

First…

Separate love from expectation.Your child breaking your expectation does not mean they stopped loving you.

Second…

Do not emotionally punish your child for becoming different from your imagination. Children are not born to complete the unfinished dreams of parents. They are born to live their own life journey.

Third…

Allow yourself time to emotionally adjust without carrying shame. Acceptance is not forced acting.

True acceptance happens slowly when love becomes bigger than fear.

And finally…

Ask one honest question to yourself:

“At the end of my life, what will matter more?

That my child followed my script…Or that my child felt emotionally safe with me?”

Life is changing faster than many hearts can adapt. But one truth remains eternal:

Relationships survive not because people are identical…But because love learns to expand beyond comfort.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️😊

@@ 177 | Inside us series -30 | When Did I Forget Myself? | 
A friend came to me the other day, crying softly. She asked me a question that broke my heart:

"Every single day, I am doing something for someone else.

My kids.

My parents.

My job.

Everyone’s problems.

Everyone’s rules.

Everybody wants a piece of me.

So, I keep giving. I keep changing myself to make them happy. I keep saying 'yes' even when I want to say 'no.' But deep inside... I am just so tired. It is not my body that hurts. It is my soul. My heart is tired.

Sometimes, when the house is finally quiet, I sit alone and think:

When was the last time I laughed until my stomach hurt?

When was the last time I did something just for me?

Why do I feel like a bad person just for wanting to sit down and rest?

I love my family so much. I really do. But somewhere along the way... I think I lost myself."

The Public Bench

This is not just one person’s story. This is the story of so many kind, beautiful people today.

They are not really living anymore. They are just "available." They are available for everyone else’s sadness, everyone else's anger, and everyone else's needs.

Slowly, they become like a bench in a public park. People come, sit down, leave their heavy bags on it for a while, and walk away when they feel better. But nobody ever turns around to ask the bench:

"Are you okay?"

The saddest part is that everyone praises this. Society looks at them and says:

"Look at what a wonderful, selfless mother."

"Look at how much he sacrifices for his family."

But on the inside, that wonderful person is completely empty. One day, they wake up and realize:

"I know exactly what makes everyone else happy... but I can't even remember what makes me happy anymore." That moment hurts so deeply.

The Flute and the Music

Even the ancient stories teach us this. Think about Bhishma from the old Indian tales. He gave up everything : his throne, his love, his entire life, just to make his family happy.

The whole world clapped for him. But he lived a long, lonely life with no personal joy. He broke himself to keep others whole.

Now, look at Krishna. Krishna loved everyone. He saved people. He helped his friends. But he never lost his smile. He never threw away his flute. He never lost his peace. True love should never make you disappear.

So many people are not tired because they work too hard. They are tired because they never allow themselves to say:

"I need a break."

"I need some quiet."

"I cannot carry all of this alone."

"I matter, too."

The moment they think about resting, guilt arrives. Especially if they are a parent or a caregiver. But please, listen to me closely:

Taking care of yourself is not selfish.

Wanting a little peace is not selfish. Saying "not right now" is not selfish.

Think about it this way : if you keep pouring water out of a cup without ever refilling it, soon the cup is dry. And when a person stays empty for too long, their love slowly turns into exhaustion. Exhaustion turns into frustration. And frustration pushes the people we love away.

Even the doors of beautiful temples close for a few hours every day. Not because God has gone away, but because even holy places need a little silence.

Please, do not lose yourself while trying to save everyone else.

Even a flute must have empty space inside... otherwise, beautiful music can never be made !

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️😊

For the past few weeks, I have been missing in action.

THUD.

Fast scrolling.

Short attention spans.

Constant stimulation.

Private digital worlds.

Selective social energy.

Parents think:

“They still haven’t seen the world.”

Children think:

“Please allow me to experience the world myself.”

And somewhere in between… love becomes advice… advice becomes pressure… and pressure becomes emotional distance.

Perhaps the real shift needed today is not stronger control.

But deeper listening.

Less: “When we were young…”

More: “Tell me how you see life.”

Because wisdom does not reduce when we listen to the next generation. In fact, listening may be the highest form of wisdom. A child may forget many lectures. But they will never forget one parent who made them feel emotionally safe.

Thought for the Day

Every generation thinks the next generation is changing too much.

But maybe… every generation is simply asking for the same thing in a different language:

“Please understand me… before you correct me.”

@@ 175 | Inside Us Series-28 | The Cup of Tea That Turned Cold | 
Many parents say this quietly to themselves.

“My children are always busy.”

“They don’t have time for me anymore.”

“They are in the same house… but far away.”

So what do we do?

We stop calling them. We stop asking. We keep quiet. But very often… We see their absence. We don’t see their world.

Raman is 68…..Every evening, after his walk, he makes two cups of tea. One for himself. One for his son, Arjun.

He places the second cup on the table… and looks at the bedroom door.

“Arjun… tea is ready,” he calls softly. From inside, a reply comes, “Hmm… coming…”

Raman smiles. He waits. Five minutes… ten minutes…The tea becomes cold. Arjun doesn’t come. Raman doesn’t call again. He slowly drinks his tea… alone.

This repeats the next day.

And the next.

One afternoon, Raman’s friend Suresh visits. They sit and talk after many months.

Arjun comes out, smiles, says “Hi uncle,” and goes back inside.

After some time, Suresh gently asks, “Your son is at home only no? You both don’t sit and talk?”

Raman smiles faintly.

“He is there only… but I feel like I don’t have a son.”

Raman never complains. He tells himself, “He is busy… let him be.” But inside, every day… he is waiting.

Not just for tea. For a few minutes of togetherness.

Now look at Arjun. He has work pressure….Calls. Messages. Deadlines. When his father calls, he does think, “I will go in 2 minutes.” But those 2 minutes become 20.

He doesn’t realise….his father is waiting… not for tea…but for him.

Both love each other. But something is missing.

Raman feels, “If my son cares, he will come and sit with me.”

Arjun feels, “I am at home with him… that itself means I care.”

One evening, something small happens. Raman doesn’t call from the hall. He walks slowly to Arjun’s room. Stands near the door and says gently,

“Arjun… can we sit together for 10 minutes and have tea? I wait for that time.”

No anger. No complaint. Just a simple request. Arjun looks up. For the first time… he understands.

That evening, they sit together. Not for long. But the tea is not cold. And Raman… doesn’t feel alone.

What is really happening here?

Sometimes, people care. But they don’t show it in the way we expect. And sometimes, we expect…but never express.

Essence

In many homes, distance is not created by lack of love. It is created by silence. We assume the other person will understand. But unspoken expectations slowly turn into hurt.

A simple sentence… spoken with love…can bring people closer than many silent days.

Thought for the Day

Don’t wait for people to understand your silence. Say what you need… gently.

Because when hearts speak clearly…relationships become warmer.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏

@@ 174 |  | Why is balancing emotions so important in the long run? | 
A friend asked me:

“I keep hearing that long-term stress can lead to serious health issues like BP, diabetes, heart problems… even cancer. Is this really true? What is happening inside the body?”

A very useful question…Not to scare…Not to blame…But to create awareness that can gently transform how we live.

First… a very important clarity

This is not about blaming anyone who is already facing illness. Serious diseases happen due to many reasons:

* Body makeup (genetics)

* Food, sleep, lifestyle

* Environment

So please don’t think: “This is because of me.” This is only about understanding: How our daily emotional patterns quietly affect our body over time.

The Story of Two Buttons

Inside your body… there are two powerful buttons.

• The “Run Button” (Stress Mode - Sympathetic system)

When you feel:

* Fear

* Anger

* Worry

* Pressure

This button turns ON. Your body prepares for danger:

* Heart beats fast

* Breathing becomes quick

* Blood pressure increases

* Sugar is released for quick energy

This is useful…If a dog is chasing you… you need this to run!

• The “Fix Button” (Calm Mode - Parasympathetic system)

When you feel:

* Safe

* Relaxed

* Peaceful

This button turns ON. Now your body does its real work:

* Repairs small damages

* Balances hormones

* Strengthens immunity

* Cleans unhealthy cells

* Improves digestion and sleep

This is where healing actually happens.

What is the real problem today?

Earlier…Stress came… and went. Now…The “dog” never leaves. But today’s “dog” is invisible:

* Constant thinking

* Work pressure

* Emotional strain

* Fear of future

* Unexpressed feelings

So what happens?

• The Run Button is always ON

• The Fix Button is rarely used

What happens over many years? (Chronic stress)

Not in one day…Not in one month…But slowly… silently…

• Body stays in overdrive

Like a car engine always running fast. Over time:

* Blood pressure stays high → BP

* Sugar regulation gets disturbed → Diabetes

* Heart gets strained → Heart disease

• Repair system becomes weak

Your body is constantly repairing small damages. But if “Fix Button” is rarely pressed:

* Repair slows down

* Wear and tear increases

• Immunity becomes less alert

Your body has a natural cleaning system. Every day it removes unhealthy or abnormal cells. But under long stress:

* This system becomes tired

* Efficiency reduces

Where does cancer fit in this?

Let us be very clear and balanced. Cancer is not caused by stress alone.

But…A healthy body:

* Detects and removes abnormal cells early

* Maintains internal balance

Under long-term stress:

* Repair becomes weaker

* Immunity becomes less sharp

* Inflammation increases

So the body becomes a less protective environment….Not the cause… But a supporting condition over time.

A simple way to see it

If a house is cleaned daily…

Dust never builds up. If cleaning stops…Slowly… things accumulate. Same with the body.

The real message

Not fear. Not guilt Awareness.

Because most damage in life is not sudden…It is small imbalance… repeated daily… for years. The Good News is : The Power is in Your Hands. You don’t need big changes. You just need to press the “Fix Button” every day.

Useful tips

• Take a “Quiet Break” (10 minutes)

Sit quietly… no phone… no thinking.Just breathe slowly. Tell your body: “You are safe.”

• Don’t lock emotions inside

If something is bothering you:

* Talk to someone

* Write it down

A locked heart becomes heavy over time.

• Small pauses during the day

Even 2 minutes:

* Close your eyes

* Take slow breaths

This resets your system.

• Laugh and lighten up

Laughter quickly activates the Fix Button.

• Respect sleep

Sleep is when your body’s “repair team” works.

No sleep = no healing.

• Keep life a little simple

Reduce unnecessary comparison and overthinking.

Key take away

Health is not only about:

* What you eat

* How much you exercise

Health is also about: How often your body feels safe… calm… and relaxed.

Thought for the day

Every day… Ask yourself: “How many times did I press my Fix Button today?”

Because…

When your mind feels safe…

Your body starts healing.

When your body heals…

Life becomes stronger.

Take care of your inner state…

And your body will quietly take care of you.

Dhanyosmi 🙏

@@ 173 | Epic Series -56 | When You Feel Pulled Apart | 
A question from a reader:

“I am 41… working, managing home, handling everything. My parents feel my children are going out of control. My children feel my parents are too rigid. Every day I am adjusting, explaining, balancing…

But inside… I feel like I am breaking. What should I do?”

Let us relate this situation to another home .

Meera is 42. Morning starts with her mother saying,

• “Why so much phone all the time?”

* “Children should have discipline.”

Evening ends with her son saying,

* “Amma… please don’t interfere.”

* “You don’t get today’s life.”

Same house. Same love. But words… like small arrows.

One Sunday, things went too far. Her mother complained loudly. Her son reacted sharply. Voices rose. Doors shut. Silence filled the house.

Meera went to the kitchen… stood near the sink… and just held the edge tightly. Not crying loudly. Just… quiet tears.

At that moment, the power went off. Darkness. She lit a small oil lamp. And something simple… but powerful… happened.

Her mother slowly came and sat near the lamp. Her son also came… phone light on… a little awkward. For a few minutes… nobody spoke.

Just that one lamp… lighting all three faces. And suddenly, Meera saw clearly.

Her mother… not strict… just afraid of losing values. Her son… not rude… just trying to find independence. Different worlds. Different fears. Same love.

That tiny lamp… did what loud arguments could not. It did not choose sides. It simply gave light… to both. And Meera understood. “I don’t have to win this. I have to light this.”

In the Ramayana, think of Lord Rama. When he was sent to the forest, he stood between two powerful pulls:

* His father’s promise (duty)

* His own rightful place as king (personal life)

He did not fight either side. He did not reject either side. He held both with dignity. Like a lamp… he gave light to dharma… even in darkness.

The Insight

You are not in the middle to suffer. You are in the middle… to illuminate. Not to prove who is right. But to help each side see the other.

Like that small lamp…

You may feel small. You may feel unnoticed. But without that light….everyone sits in darkness.

Thought for the Day

If today you feel pulled between parents and children…Do not feel you are breaking. You are the quiet lamp in the house…standing still…giving light…helping love see clearly.

And that… is sacred.

Dhanyosmi 🙇‍♂️🙏
